the commission has already submitted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. - mr president, i am speaking on the second proposal for a directive. let me congratulate you on your election. i would like to inform the house that the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy adopted unanimously, on 12 june 2001, the opinion for which i am the rapporteur. this unanimous vote is important, since the committee on economic and monetary affairs has indeed recognised that public procurement procedures are instruments of the sustainable development strategy and that the environment must be integrated in a transversal way into all union policies, as the treaty calls for. in this context, the competent authorities, which are politically responsible, must be allowed to establish the environmental and social criteria which they intend to use in the award of public contracts, while respecting, of course, the principle of non-discrimination. i would like to draw attention to two points, without repeating what other colleagues have already said. the committee on economic and monetary affairs has proposed simplifying the award procedures, in particular by doubling the thresholds from which the directive applies to public contracts, and on the other hand it also proposes to protect small and medium-sized enterprises in particular by providing that contracting authorities are authorized to decide that 15% of the total volume of contracts they award is to be awarded to small and medium-sized enterprises.
- mr. president, congratulations on your election. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. this report has a far greater impact than one might think at first sight. every year thousands of contracts are awarded by public authorities, often regional or local. public procurement is now estimated to represent some 14% of total european gdp. the existing rules are too complicated, too complex, and have thus far deprived this segment of the market of the benefits of the internal market. the present report must not therefore be allowed to overstep its objective of simplifying legislation and opening up european procurement procedures. for these reasons, among others, i am opposed to raising the thresholds too much, which would mean that only 8 to 12% of all procurement in the european union would be covered by this system. the introduction of social and environmental criteria also requires caution. too many and too burdensome conditions make it much more difficult, both for our cities and municipalities and for our smes. we must realize that they will be the first to be confronted with this european regulation. we must bring them closer to europe and let them spread the european message. this is definitely not happening because of reglementitis.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the liberalization of road transport has undoubtedly led to a better supply and better prices in europe. the same is true of the increasing competition, which has inevitably led to the fact that in many companies this tends to be at the expense of safety and working conditions. the directive on the training of professional drivers in the goods and passenger transport sector which we are now discussing is intended to be a complementary measure and to improve the status of these drivers. the technological developments, but also the stricter environmental and safety requirements, are in our view sufficient arguments to demand that on the one hand this training must be improved and on the other that it must also be continued through regular training. in the course of our many discussions with the sector itself, we have also found that the shortage of labour must not lead to this directive being a hindrance here. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. firstly, we have created a kind of bridge to the driving licence, because we know that in many member states the training for driving licences already requires certain conditions which are equivalent to what this training suggests. we therefore believe it is important that the training should be shared, but not duplicated, once for the driving licence and once for the training. the philosophy was the same for the learning objectives. the aim was not to define all the details, but to keep the learning objectives in general and therefore also in the annex, because this allows for flexible handling of these learning objectives and leaves open the possibility of adapting these learning objectives to development. the training available varies from one country to another and we have therefore decided that the countries can examine this in detail together with the commission. the aim is to make the training equal. if a country already offers training, this directive should not change it. we believe that continuing training is an important point for further development, and we hope that the bridge will also be used for driving licences, because we believe that continuing training for driving licences will also be an essential element in the future. we have also opted for flexibility, i.e. training of seven hours a day, rather than larger blocks, so as not to place too much pressure on the farms. i think that we have not exaggerated by saying that a day is 0.05% of the workload of an employee. i personally also hold very strongly to the idea of a flexible training place. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of the training provided by the trainers. if this certificate is to be valid throughout europe, it should also be possible to make the training european. we are also aware that this is an interesting and important directive, but unfortunately it does not solve all the problems. mr van dam has just mentioned the bilateral licences which are still valid in europe, which means that abuse can be made in this field. we also know that certain companies continue to take advantage of the fact - and i regret this somewhat in mr van dam's report - that we are restricting this uniform driving licence to countries which are not members of the eu. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. i do not want to mention the names of any companies, but we know some names in this field. in conclusion, i would say that the whole thing could be unified if we had a uniform driving licence tomorrow, a directive on driving and rest periods, a card to prove this and then this driving licence directive and today's training. it is not just the commission, but also the member states, that we are calling for to improve the controls. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented correctly.
the commission has already submitted a proposal for a regulation to the council. - mr president, i have changed seats in the house. i speak today on behalf of the committee on development. the committee on development made a number of substantive remarks on fisheries policy in conjunction with development policy. it first notes that the european union is one of the most important players in the world in the field of fisheries and we must be aware of this. the union market is so vast that more than half of the fish consumed is either caught by the union fleet outside community waters or imported, mainly from developing countries. it is therefore understandable that the impact of the common fisheries policy on developing countries can be extremely important. the committee on development and cooperation believes that the priority is to ensure that the common fisheries policy is consistent with the policy of cooperation and development. this is of course essential because it is not a question of shifting the problems facing the european fisheries sector. and we know that there are many problems: overcapacity of the fleet, over-exploitation of resources, declining employment, a vastly degraded marine environment. so shifting these problems to other parts of the world is not acceptable and is not a smart and long-term policy. we also believe that fisheries agreements, which are an important element of the common fisheries policy, must be based not only on this first principle but also on reliable scientific assessments of the state of stocks and the impact of fishing on target species in particular. it is clear, as the lead rapporteur has said, that these scientific assessments are necessary. if they did not exist, as is unfortunately often the case in developing countries, the precautionary principle would be appropriate to avoid over-exploitation of resources. finally, it is necessary to ensure adequate protection of the communities which live off the coastal fisheries resources in third countries and thus ensure their food security.

commission. – mr president, i am under the impression that parliament would like me to make a statement on the case of the road haulage driver stuck in the grand-duchy of luxembourg. if that is the case, then i should like to say the following. based on the information at the commission's disposal, the luxembourg authorities conducted a search on behalf of an inquiry led by the tribunal of munich in germany of the premises in luxembourg of an austrian-based transport operator kralowetz. this company has been active in luxembourg since 1986 and was an international one established in eight different european countries. the technical manager, mr karl kralowetz, was arrested on 22 january, and since 26 january approximately 150 lorries belonging to the kralowetz company from different countries have assembled in luxembourg on a parking bay of a customs centre and the drivers have complained that they have not been paid for more than three months. the majority of them may be considered as illegally employed. the luxembourg authorities are in the process of organising the return of the drivers by plane to their country of origin and each driver will receive eur 1,000 by way of compensation. the real problem is the unscrupulous behaviour of the transport operator concerned. he had used illegal labour – i.e. without social security status – with a salary which is not in conformity with national law or collective agreements and without a work permit. for several months the judicial authorities and rogatory committees of several member states have been conducting investigations and enquiries and drawing up reports. all those actions have resulted in further searches, the arrest of some of those responsible and the confiscation of documents and equipment. the commission welcomes the action of the judicial and administrative authorities but, nevertheless, regrets that such an affair could ever have been possible at all. this proves the need for a correct application of national laws, as well as the following community rules in the field of international road transport operations. firstly, the rules on admission to the occupation of road-transport operator, namely the three criteria of good repute, financial standing and professional competence; secondly, the rules on driving times and rest periods, for which the commission has recently made a proposal for new, simplified and updated eu legislation. in addition, the following upcoming legal instruments are also relevant; firstly, the rules on working time, on which an agreement between the european parliament and the council is very much to be welcomed, and on which you will vote tomorrow. i should mention here the commission's particular thanks to mr hughes. secondly, the rules on a uniform driver attestation, for which mr van dam was your rapporteur, and for which the european parliament gave its approval on 17 january. that regulation will put to an end the illegal employment of non-eu drivers in the community, through checks on the legal employment status of such drivers. in conclusion, community instruments exist or are in the process of being adopted. they need to be correctly applied by member states, and this example should serve as a lesson to avoid any further repetition of such unscrupulous behaviour by cowboy operators.
- the example just cited by mr bolkestein is obviously a flagrant and probably also exceptional example of the abuses in the transport sector. it's not the only example. we have seen more of them in recent years, almost on the same scale as the example we have just heard. in addition to the many large-scale abuses, there are also many small-scale abuses, because the transport sector is characterized by a large proportion of abuses. the system we have now, namely stricter standards for working hours - it is not only driving time that is at stake, but also working time - is a tightening up of the rules compared with the past. this can only work if control is improved. if this does not happen - and i agree with what mr hughes has said - then all these standards are worthless. although control is a national competence, this does not preclude the preparation of a biannual report on the results of the control, which must be submitted to the commission. i would ask the commission to look again at this report and to reconsider the requirements to be met by the member states in this respect. one of the things the commission is apparently checking is whether foreign carriers are subject to stricter controls than domestic carriers, i.e. people of one's own nationality. that, of course, cannot be used as a criterion. the commission is right to look into this, but it cannot continue to do so as long as the hauliers keep people travelling too long at too high a speed, because they often have to arrive at their destination at a certain time. as long as, commissioner, - and i know that this is not your responsibility - there are different standards in the member states as regards the number of kilometres per hour that one may drive, all this is pointless. you drive 90 km/h in one member state, 100 km/h in the other and 80 km/h in the next. it's crazy, the way transportation is done in europe. all this should be improved. in addition to the controls, there is the problem of sanctions. in one member state the penalty is sister, in the other member state the penalty is so. in fact, there should be minimum standards for sanctions. i know that this is not a european competence at the moment, but it should be. finally, as long as the tachographs of hauliers and truck drivers have to be checked, fraud will always occur, because the tachograph is a very suitable instrument for this purpose. it is therefore high time that the regulation we are waiting for, which stipulates that in future we must drive with a digital box, is adopted.
- mr president, i have to go. in the last few weeks we have dealt with a number of issues which directly affect the transport sector, including social measures, and we in the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism have also taken the view that all these measures - and i believe that the working time, which has now finally been regulated, is an important chapter which is being closed - are only meaningful if there is also a desire on the part of the member states to tighten up controls and to adapt sanctions. i come from a border region and i know very well that in this area there is a saying: whoever knows the rules of each country best can make the most money. the lobby that is at play here is not a lobby for or against workers - i do not think it has anything to do with luxembourg legislation either - nor is it a lobby for or against employers. in the end, both sides lose in the luxembourg example, and we have also reminded the commission of this in the famous van dam report. we are not concerned with more and more directives, with more and more details, we are concerned with the countries being prepared to work together so that both employers can offer correct prices and not suffer from this dumping which is organised there and, of course, workers can also work under correct conditions. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the negotiations. a truck driver who also owns his truck - and i come back to the first report - is always on the verge of legality if he wants to work profitably. this is the situation we live in. this means that we must look at the development of road freight liberalization firstly from an economic point of view, and secondly from the point of view of the employer, but also from the point of view of the worker, with the same eyes, and i believe that we can represent the interests of both equally.

the president-in-office of the council. - mr president, the european union has a long tradition of effective protection of human rights and has succeeded in creating an area of legal certainty and essential freedoms, with rules inspired by our own legislation and by international codification. in our case, within the european union, we have had the parallel impetus of the council of europe itself, in addition to the demand that we make, both at national and european union level, in the field of human rights. recent examples show this concern, which is part of our very identity, such as the european charter of human rights, the commission's latest communications on the european union's work in promoting human rights in third countries, the very important work done by this parliament in this field and the conclusions of the general affairs council of 25 june last, according to which effective concern for the defence of human rights must be a permanent feature of the union's external policy. i would also like to recall the guidelines, both on torture and on the application of capital punishment, which are constantly taken into account in the formulation of our foreign policy and in our relations with third countries. there are two issues which are also always present in our heritage and culture and which are linked to the general trend of respect for human rights in any foreign policy action or initiative, which are the principle of non-discrimination against women and the rights of the child and their effective protection. all these concepts are undoubtedly at the heart of the european union's work during the sessions of the united nations commission on human rights, and it will be these principles that inform our action at the next session, which will be held between 18 march and 26 april this year. the commission on human rights is without doubt the most important human rights challenge facing the european union in the coming months. this commission, which - as everyone knows - is a body under the auspices of the united nations economic and social council, meets every year in geneva and is the forum for intense debate on all aspects of international affairs which may have any connection or relation to human rights. it is therefore an annual exercise, which is obligatory for us, of complex preparation and which requires the european union, year after year, to make a constant effort to achieve in the international context - as the international community expects from the european union, which it considers the bastion of the defence of these rights - an effective and increasingly universal protection of human rights. it is no secret that the european union is very active in the commission on human rights and that a significant number of member states of the european union are members of the commission (at present, spain, italy, portugal, france, the united kingdom, belgium, austria, germany and sweden). given our share in the so-called western european group, the european union is by far the largest shareholder. given the enormous complexity of the exercise we are talking about, it is difficult at this stage to establish definitive positions of the european union vis-à-vis the commission on human rights. the positions are always based on our value system. it is not a question of compromising any of our principles, but positions within the commission are often the result of prior coordination to achieve practical results, and there are some issues which, because of their sensitivity and their media coverage, deserve special treatment. in any event, our aim is, as in previous years, to present a series of draft resolutions and statements by the president of the commission on human rights, which include the developments in the last year since the conclusion of the 57th session of this body, which took place precisely in april 2001. the european union therefore understands that it will be necessary to intervene with speeches on highly sensitive issues in which our position must be reflected in some way. this year's speeches, which are prepared in collaboration with the partners, will focus on the following topics: racism, the right to development, violations of human rights and fundamental freedoms anywhere in the world, economic, social and cultural rights, civil, political and women's rights and gender perspective, children's rights, human rights defenders and advisory service, and technical cooperation in the field of human rights. the list is extensive but not exhaustive. it is a demonstration of the wide range of issues that we will have to deal with in the commission on human rights, where the scope of the defence of these rights must be one-sided and, of course, universal. that is why the work of the commission on human rights is linked to many other issues which, in one way or another, affect the work of the european union. firstly, it is essential to refer to the conventional framework in which the commission on human rights has exercised and exercises its function. two of the exercises to which the european union will give high priority are the discussions under way on the drawing up of two optional protocols: the one on the covenant on economic, social and cultural rights and the one on the convention against torture, the wording of which has already advanced from session to session and is quite close to completion. in both cases, the european union has a major role to play in drafting the protocols and in collaborating with the working groups which precede the commission's meeting in order to facilitate the adoption of these protocols. secondly, as everyone can imagine, there are many social and humanitarian challenges facing us and which we are determined to tackle together with our partners: all the issues of refugees and asylum seekers, emigration and all its consequences in the cultural, educational and social spheres, the problem of discrimination against indigenous peoples, the real advancement and practical equalization of women at all levels, and so many sectors, individual or collective, which are the object of attention by the international community. in the council we intend to make a special effort to ensure that our participation is as active and constructive as possible, both at the special session of the general assembly on children - which should have been held in september but was affected by the events of 11 september and has been rescheduled, although no date has yet been set - and at the world assembly on ageing, which will take place in april this year. thirdly, we should take account of the debate which has already begun on the possibility of improving the very meritorious work of the various committees of independent experts, the so-called treaty bodies, which monitor and control the full implementation of many of the conventions to which i have just referred. we must give them the means to do their job as well as possible. mr president, i should also like to mention two subjects which, because of their complexity, will affect our work in the commission on human rights: on the one hand, the fight against racism and racial discrimination. as you all know, last september a major world conference was held in durban, south africa, the preparations and conduct of which were conflicting but at the end of which, fortunately, consensus was preserved after long discussions and, in large part, thanks to the perseverance of the european union delegation as a whole. we must now be particularly vigilant so that the wounds of durban do not reopen and so that the consensus reached can be developed effectively and pragmatically, always with the help of the appropriate bodies of the united nations general secretariat. another issue is the abolition or restriction of the use of capital punishment. we are all aware that, despite the firm conviction we have in europe that the death penalty must be completely eradicated and abolished, some countries belonging to cultural and religious currents different from ours persist in maintaining and applying it. for our part, we will continue to pursue a basically humanitarian endeavour, trying to extend the scope of its abolition, reinforcing situations of moratorium or of non-effective application of the penalty, even if it exists in legislation, and appealing to the authorities so that - in very specific cases, of minors, pregnant women and mentally disabled people - execution is not carried out. these are the lines of action which were set out in the european union at the time, in the form of guidelines adopted in this connection, and to which we shall continue to refer as often as necessary, especially in the resolution on the death penalty which the european union traditionally presents to the commission on human rights. i would like to conclude, mr president, by reflecting on the new situation created after 9/11. i echo the concerns i have heard in this house this very morning. the presidency and the council are well aware that a certain climate of concern has arisen in many countries that the protection of human rights could be substantially undermined in the interests of strengthening security and combating terrorist organisations. the european union, and the council at its head, is convinced that this must not be the case; that there is no incompatibility between the security and freedom of our fellow citizens, and that in our sphere there will be no curtailment of either fundamental freedoms or everyday democratic coexistence. it is important to remember that the attacks of 11 september were a full-blown attack on this system of values and freedoms, which we are so proud of and which we all have the duty to defend. the commission on human rights will undoubtedly be an ideal framework for demonstrating our commitment to respect and protect human rights, for extending that commitment to all countries of the world, without using the pretext of the fight against terrorism to coerce them, and for reaffirming that the protection of human rights remains in the european union as a sound principle as ever.
commission. – mr president, this debate provides the opportunity for an exchange of views prior to the deliberations of the 58th session of the un commission on human rights. the human rights contact group has already brought together representatives of non-governmental organisations, members of parliament, civil servants from the eu institutions and others to discuss key concerns for geneva. the broader the input, the more confident the eu can be in the approach which it adopts at the un commission. as ever, when you invite the commission to these annual debates, it comes at a time when the eu has not definitively decided upon the initiatives which it will take at the unchr and their final form will only be determined after negotiations on the ground in geneva. regrettably, there is all too much choice when looking around the world for examples of human rights violations. that is why the eu must adopt a coherent approach when determining the best way to promote respect for human rights. our communication on human rights of last may, and the subsequent conclusions by the general affairs council, set out a strategy for such an approach by an eu that would actively engage with the world in the promotion of human rights through, for example, the human rights clause which forms a regular feature of community agreements and which finds its most advanced expression in the cotonou agreement; through the dialogue which we have undertaken to conduct in contacts with all third countries, as reinforced in the guidelines on human rights dialogues which were adopted by the council last december; through our engagement with ngos, including the extensive funding for human rights projects channelled through the european initiative for democratisation and human rights and mainstream programmes and through our cooperation with the un and other organisations. these factors should be taken into account by those who point to the inevitable element of selectivity in the countries and themes which are the subject of eu initiatives at the unchr. they are not the sum total of the eu’s human rights policy necessarily, just an element of it. there are several issues which will feature throughout this year’s un commission. first, the highly regrettable failure by the us to secure re-election to the commission and the participation of several states whose human rights records are – to put it very politely – less than acceptable. this means that the eu will have to work extra hard to ensure that core human rights values are reflected in the decisions taken. second, the fight against terrorism, which has rightly permeated every corner of the political debate since 11 september. human rights considerations are involved here at several levels. we must guard against any muddling of concepts. the eu maintains the important distinction that states are the perpetrators of human rights violations, whilst terrorists are particularly abhorrent criminals. and it is right that actions by states – whether at a legislative, judicial or penal level – are subject to international law and proper scrutiny. in fighting terrorism, we and our allies must uphold the values of respect for international human rights and humanitarian law. the international community must also be alert to opportunistic attempts by states to justify domestic repression and crackdowns by claiming that they are "fighting terrorism". zimbabwe is the most clear example of this. the fight against racism will also be given due prominence at the 58th session of the un commission, and the eu may present a statement on the issue as in previous years. after a rather bumpy journey from durban, we now have a solid declaration and programme of action – a welcome development which we must now put to good use. combating racism, xenophobia and discrimination against minorities is one of the four priorities under the european initiative for democracy and human rights for the period 2002 to 2004. eur 17 million per year have been set aside in order to support ngos and other organisations in the promotion of rights and awareness-raising as a concrete follow-up to the world conference. finally, a word on two countries of particular interest, both to me and to this parliament. first, china. this is the only country with which the eu has a dedicated human rights dialogue. the human rights situation in china rightly gives rise to a great deal of concern – from the extensive application of the death penalty to the suppression of religious and cultural rights in tibet, xinjiang and elsewhere and restrictions on the freedom of expression as evidenced in the recent measures on the use of the internet. in this difficult context, the eu dialogue and seminars – the last of which, in december 2001 brought together european and chinese experts to discuss the prevention of torture and the right to education – are one conduit for encouragement, cooperation and criticism. as the general affairs council has underscored, progress on the ground is a necessary corollary of the dialogue and that is why we evaluate the dialogue annually. second, in iran, some progress in reform is offset by continuing serious human rights concerns. the un special representative on iran highlighted this at the third committee last year, referring in particular to the sharp increase in public hangings and floggings. the eu is engaging with iran in an effort to secure improvement. the positive outcome for the eu resolution at the third committee shows that the international community shares our concerns. the acceptance of a visit by the special representative would be a further step in the right direction. the mandate for an ec trade and cooperation agreement is currently under discussion in the council. i can assure honourable members that human rights considerations will play an important part in any future negotiations. parliament’s resolutions and debates are a crucial stimulus to the process of preparing for the commission on human rights. i hope that, if possible, representatives from parliament will, as in previous years, visit the unchr in session and that they will contribute to the wider debate.
- mrs mary robinson, the high commissioner, has done a great deal in recent years to make the un commission on human rights the conscience of humanity and for that i think she deserves all the credit. but that does not change the fact that questions are increasingly being raised about this annual high mass in geneva. the human rights organization summed it up as follows: it's the foxes who have to watch the chicken eggs. it is indeed a matter of concern that countries such as kenya, sudan, togo and congo, which are not particularly outstanding in respecting human rights, should be part of this committee and should help set the standards. the procedure, particularly the system of majority voting, raises questions as to the composition of the committee. governments that are being targeted by ngos often band together to defend their common self-interest, and the motto seems to be: if we spare others, we don't risk being judged ourselves. that is why i support what commissioner nielson has just said. the us is no longer part of this committee, and the european union must now play a more pioneering role than ever before. europe must be more assertive and more consistent in its defence of human rights. it must support more resolutions, including those on major countries such as china and russia. some european member states are too ready to swallow their principles when economic interests are at stake. the union must also insist that the recommendations of the committee be followed up effectively. a lot of money is spent on early warning, but the reports of the experts on the subject are systematically ignored. the credibility of the commission on human rights in geneva and of human rights policy in general is at stake today, and i believe that the european union has a very heavy responsibility in this respect.
- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have to say that i am very sorry. the greens and the free european alliance congratulate mrs kratsa on her excellent report. i would also like to stress that we were pleased with the constructive way in which this report was produced, namely through cooperation between the groups. we share the rapporteur's view that euro-mediterranean cooperation is politically of crucial importance for europe. this cooperation is not only a sure way of promoting europe's economic interests but is primarily aimed at creating an area of peace, security, stability and prosperity. to accelerate this cooperation, a better understanding between the peoples of europe and the peoples of the partner countries is necessary, as is an active civil society capable of participating in this process. in all of this, women play a key role. they must be encouraged to acquire their own political identity and, to this end, they must be given the opportunity to overcome centuries of unjust backwardness through education and awareness-raising. women should no longer be seen as mere mothers, however important their role as educators may be. they should also be seen - provided they have access to education - as full partners in the labour market and in politics. in view of enlargement, the women of the south must conclude a pact of solidarity with the women of the north and support each other in their just demands. europe should be the driving force. our committee on women's rights and equal opportunities, with an inspiring chairwoman, mrs karamanou, has an important role to play in this.

- madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, an accident never happens by itself, but this is particularly true of the afflicted population in the east of the republic of congo. not only have they suffered for years now from war, with countless refugees, and occupation by neighboring rwanda, but now these volcanic eruptions have added to that. the recent dramatic reports confirm the plight of the population. the short dry season has begun and there is a lead heat. there's toxic waste. toxic fumes are rising from the lava. the people have no firewood, so they use the heat from the lava flow to make the fire. you will understand that it is a miserable situation with a city which, in terms of destruction, is at least partly reminiscent of hiroshima. the part of the city that was not affected by the eruption is actually living normally as far as possible under those circumstances and under the occupation. so that means that the people who are really the victims are very difficult to distinguish from the others and at the moment there is really no way of seeing who are the most affected. it is a message which i also find confirmed in a recent communication from the bishops' conference. it complains that the rations provided are actually insufficient, whereas there are sufficient food supplies; that a large part of the city has been flattened but that it is not really known who the people are who are really affected. surveys have been carried out, but the results of these surveys have not yet been confirmed. fortunately, a flood of international aid has been launched and the european union has played a leading role in this. i was afraid that in my speech i would have to make a number of complaints, because i am aware of them. i hear complaints, for example, that all aid has to go through kigali and that people are taking advantage of the situation there. on the other hand, i am told that kigali airport is the easiest in terms of handling goods and that aid is not currently being impeded. i know that aid has been sent from the democratic republic of congo to bukavu and that it must leave, but that aid is considered to be aid from the congolese people and the occupiers are not going to tolerate the distribution of the goods. i would therefore like to reassure you in a certain sense, but the measures announced by the european parliament are absolutely necessary.
- mr president-in-office of the council, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to speak about one aspect of the summit, namely employment, knowing that the socioeconomic policy we are pursuing is very much a function of employment. firstly, i think the summit should reflect on whether it will be able to achieve the objectives set in lisbon. i think that is impossible with the rapid pace we are currently witnessing in europe. i have looked at the figures for employment and unemployment. the last year has been bad and we will never reach the targets. this is partly because there is no longer any pressure from the european union to actually implement the national action plans for employment which have to be submitted by the member states every year. in the early days of the luxembourg process, the bad report the commission gave to the various member states was front-page news, but now it is only a pro memora on the last page of some newspapers. this means that the pressure of the open method of coordination is slowly decreasing. the summit should therefore reflect on what can be done to increase this pressure. and then two specific points: the cost to employers of creating jobs is far too high. if i look at the difference between the cost and what the employees end up with, then the lower staff, for example, on average, still have 45% of the cost of employment and the executive staff 35 or 36%. if nothing is done to reduce these costs for employers, the outflow of people from the workplace will remain very high. in conclusion, the following: the difference between unemployment benefits and wages is too small. many people remain unemployed and combine that situation with undeclared work because wages are too low.

mr president, i do not share the enthusiasm of most of our colleagues for the result achieved with this directive. i think that in practice, as far as the noise abatement policy is concerned, which is of particular concern to those living near airports, this is rather a step backwards. the proposal abrogates the council regulation which was due to come into force on 1 april and which had the effect of prohibiting the landing of 'hushkit' aircraft, old aircraft fitted with noise abatement devices but whose overall performance was recognised as insufficient. this regulation is replaced by a directive based on a so-called balanced approach to noise management, but which, in very practical terms, limits the scope for action of the member states in the fight against noise pollution and extends the life of 'hushkit' aircraft. the amendments proposed by the committee on transport and approved by the committee on the environment are undoubtedly improvements to the original text. but the fact remains that we are in a situation where member states which will do nothing to improve the control of nuisance in the vicinity of their airports will in fact be practicing a dumping policy which is likely to increase in the coming years as we are currently experiencing a significant acceleration in air traffic. the regulations for specific action are also relatively complex and will therefore hamper initiatives. i would like to remind you that the european court of human rights ruled a few months ago in favour of people living near heathrow airport in london who complained about noise at night, and the right to a healthy habitat and the right to sleep was recognised as a human right. i therefore believe that in order to be fully consistent with this decision of the court of human rights, the commission should quickly take a new initiative, a directive not only limiting noise around airports but also a directive setting noise exposure standards, which would thus allow for a harmonious situation within the european union.

- mr president, commissioner, the consumption of vitamins and other food supplements is more than a fashion, it is a real social phenomenon. the market it generates is huge. the lobbying we have been subjected to is proof of this, if any. let us come straight to the main question that is bothering many of my colleagues who are opposed to this directive: should we have legislated? i would say yes without hesitation, and for at least three reasons. firstly, this proposal for a directive provides a practical response to a reality, namely the absence of an internal market which has harmful consequences both for economic operators in this sector and for consumers. for example, it is now virtually impossible for a manufacturer of vitamins in the united kingdom to export and market its products in belgium or france, for example. secondly, it is important not to be short-sighted about the two main battles in the first reading, namely the inclusion of substances with physiological effects - fibres and plant extracts in particular - and the labelling standards which are essential to enable the consumer to make an informed choice. our parliament has been heard. i would add, and this brings me to my third point, that no quality food supplement, whatever its composition, will be banned by this directive. i would like to emphasize this point: we must stop raising the spectre of prohibition and a europe that castrates. the producers will have at least three and a half years, according to our vote, to submit their dossier and prove the safety of their products which are not currently included in the annexes, and if there were to be a ban, it would therefore be on doubtful substances, which is the least of my concerns. since i am now dealing with the purely health aspect of this matter, i would also like to see an end to the repetition that there is no danger in the abuse of vitamins. that's not true! that's not true! it should be noted that hypervitaminosis of all fat-soluble vitamins (a, k, e, d) is a problem, and to mention just one, excess vitamin a has known teratogenic effects, i.e. fetal malformations. so we can accept political opposition to this directive, but we cannot in any case allow scientific falsehoods to be made public. in short, for all the reasons i have just mentioned, we are dealing with flexible and balanced legislation which takes account of the interests of consumers, producers and distributors and which lays the foundations for the necessary harmonization in this sector. the majority of my colleagues in the liberal group will vote for mrs müller's very good report, which i too would like to congratulate. mr president, my colleagues and i believe that of all the sectors which are in the throes of liberalization, the postal sector is both the largest and the most abused. the commission, firstly, and the council of ministers, secondly, systematically ignore or underestimate the fact that, in addition to the service provided to the population, posts in all the member states and regions of the union play a considerable social role. under the pretext of liberalizing the service, the population, and first of all the most disadvantaged, is deprived of essential social support. parliament had, moreover, rejected the first proposals made to it. the amendments put to the vote today contain improvements but do not remove the social threat; on the contrary, it is becoming increasingly clear that nothing is being done to finance universal service or to compensate for the inequalities caused by nature. aggravating circumstance: the massive job losses that will be directly caused by the measures advocated will aggravate the violence that european societies are already suffering badly from. in these circumstances, my colleagues and i have supported all the constructive amendments, wherever they come from, and we will remain more vigilant than ever.

- i shall confine my minute to the pérez royo report on the taxation of savings. i am glad that the rapporteur has taken up the amendment which i tabled in the committee on legal affairs, which states that this directive will be conditional on an agreement being reached between the european union and a number of third countries which will then have to comply with the same notification obligation. the european council had expressed itself in this way in its conclusions of feira. such negotiations are already under way. it is therefore good that this condition is also clearly included in the text of the directive, which, strangely enough, has not been the case so far. such an agreement with a number of third countries and with the so-called dependent territories is, in my view, very important in order to guarantee equal treatment of savings both inside and outside the european union, to exclude tax avoidance and to prevent significant capital flight. secondly, i regret that no amendments were considered on the application of the directive to legal persons. this would indeed exclude the placing of significant savings of natural persons in a company account, and thus fall outside the scope of this directive, and that cannot be approved.

in writing. - domestic and industrial pollution has become the main source of degradation of our european waterways, of which only 40% meet the quality objectives set for different uses. in response to this damaging situation, the council and parliament adopted the so-called 'urban waste water treatment' directive in 1991. like the majority of my colleagues in the committee on the environment, i regret that eleven years after its adoption and four years after the deadline for agglomerations of more than 10 000 population equivalents to be equipped with urban waste water collection and treatment systems, many member states still do not comply with the legally binding timetable of this directive. i also regret that the committee on the environment did not adopt one of the amendments i tabled, a common sense amendment which explained why so many member states have still not implemented this directive. the very short time limits laid down by directive 91/271/eec (six and a half years for water treatment in sensitive areas) were unlikely to take account of the situation of several large agglomerations in the member states. in 1991, these did not yet have urban waste water treatment plants and had to comply with other common european standards in the fields of public procurement and environmental impact studies. is it necessary to mention that the average time for awarding public contracts in this sector is three years, and is it also necessary to point out that a water treatment plant with its connection for collecting sewage has an average cost of eur 400 million? in short, good community legislation is one which makes a distinction between what is desirable and what is achievable. it is clear that the drafting of the directive on urban waste water treatment failed to take account of this essential rule.

the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as you know, sport in general - and football in particular - occupies a very special place in our societies, both in the behaviour of our citizens and in the collective imagination. the crowds that regularly travel to major matches - and especially major international matches - and the number of viewers are simply astounding. today's professional football is a great sporting activity, a great board game, a great entertainment enterprise and also, it must be said, a great business. but unfortunately, major matches - and especially major international matches - are also too often the scene of aggression, riots, acts of violence unacceptable both from the point of view of the ethics of sport and from the point of view of the respect due to persons and property. no member state is completely immune from the risk of disturbances, even if some international matches are less sensitive than others and it is clear that the fans of certain clubs or national teams are easier to control than others. it is precisely to help to prevent and control the risks of disturbances that may occur at international matches that the kingdom of belgium has taken the initiative of the proposal for a council decision on which we are due to vote tomorrow. this draft decision, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, has its origins in three things. firstly, there is an increase in the number of international football matches, whether they are european competitions, european championships or other tournaments, which leads to more travelling by fans and, therefore, an increase in the risk of disturbances before, during and after the competitions. secondly, the best way of preventing disorder and maintaining order at matches is, firstly, to have an organized and efficient system for exchanging information on the risks posed by certain matches in general - and more particularly by certain types of fans travelling during these matches - and, secondly, to improve coordination between the security services. in this respect, it must be acknowledged that the current system of cooperation between police forces, which is simply based on voluntary agreements and contact by blow by blow at the time of each match, has shown its weakness if not its ineffectiveness. it's not reliable enough, it's not fast enough, it's not systematic enough. that is why, ladies and gentlemen, the draft decision formally proposes to make it compulsory in each member state to set up or designate a national football information point of a police type, acting as a single, direct and central point of contact between the authorities and police services of the member states responsible for ensuring security at an international match or international competition. each national football information point must therefore be equipped with adequate equipment and staff. of course, the operational requirements are not the same in finland, austria, france, germany, the united kingdom and the netherlands. the various national points thus created or designated must therefore function as a kind of network for the exchange of information covering both general data - the number of supporters on the move, risk groups, the presence of hardcore fans - and personal data, i.e. supporters who pose specific risks to public policy and public security. in approving the report, the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs agreed with the broad lines of the proposal for a council decision. it also approved 19 amendments, all of which aim either to make the network more operational or, as mrs buitenweg said, to increase the protection of citizens when personal data are exchanged. by adopting the report, our assembly will show its determination to combat hooliganism more effectively and, in so doing, help professional football to confirm its primary vocation, which is to offer a quality show to enthusiastic supporters, without allowing a few savages to turn the stadiums or the cities that shelter them into training grounds for hooligans or battlefields for rival gangs.
the proposal to set up a european network of permanent national football information points, in other words to improve cooperation between member states in the field of public order in international football matches, is a belgian initiative based on the experience gained with euro 2000, the european championship for national teams, which, as you know, was jointly organised by belgium and the netherlands in june 2000. the proposal is a good proposal. it is a good report and i think the initiative is excellent. i would like to make only two comments. firstly, the proposal applies, of course, only to the member states of the european union. the question may be asked: what about the countries outside the european union, many of whose clubs also participate in international competitions? turkey, romania, croatia also play against teams from eu member states. you will say: the applicant countries will of course have to adopt the acquis communautaire, and that is right. but that's not for today, that's not even for tomorrow. for some of them, it's as early as 2004. and then what about the rest of it? with the non-candidate countries? it is possible to work with uefa, with the national football associations, to adapt a sleeve. second point: i think that a greater involvement and cooperation of the clubs and the supporters' associations is important to improve the climate, to improve the quality. and i think that needs to be worked on.
president. - ladies and gentlemen, major international football matches are the highlight of the year for fans, often a costly nightmare for law enforcement and local authorities, and often a source of suffering for people living in the vicinity of sports stadiums, much misery due to destruction of front gardens, cars, terraces and so on. this council decision is aimed primarily at a hard core which has so far been able to take advantage of the gaps in the exchange of information between the various police services. efficient coordination of data exchange is therefore not an unnecessary luxury. it should be clear, however, that this is primarily a preventive instrument which must allow a proper assessment of the risks. the report by mr deprez is very good. he has succeeded in clarifying and sharpening the original text, in particular by paying greater attention to the periods before and after the games. a well-functioning network of 'football' information points must be able to deploy teams in a targeted and efficient manner. the international football competition consumes a great deal of energy and resources from the police services involved, which risks undermining other tasks. the key question with instruments like this, however, is how to control the control itself. the first, almost obvious, step is to inform the individuals concerned that they are being included in this database and that they are being given access to it. it could be argued that this hampers the control function of the police services and gives the targeted persons a tactical advantage. but this, i believe, is a misunderstanding of the practice. it is not just a matter of a self-evident right, namely that one knows whether or not one is in a database, it is also a matter of a clear warning and it also has an anti-trading effect.
- mr president, the liberal group also welcomes this commission proposal for a directive, as it welcomes the draft framework decision drawn up by the council in december 2001. it is also, of course, grateful to our two rapporteurs, mrs oomen-ruijten and mr di lello. these two initiatives are a concrete response to one of the major concerns of the population: 88% of european citizens want the union to take concrete measures to protect the environment. this is one of the indications of a eurobarometer survey carried out less than a year ago and which my colleague mr coelho mentioned a moment ago. in the face of acts of environmental crime, the liberal group supports the position advocated by mrs oomen-ruijten, namely that the community framework of the first pillar is the most represent only 2% of the total number of cases. the community must therefore use the deterrent at community level to enforce existing regulations and to tackle effectively the organized networks which have made pollution their new weapon of choice.

on the situation in kosovo: the energy sector is the most important item in which we invest and there is a paradox in this sector. incredible amounts of money are invested in energy supply, yet power cuts occur almost daily. this is a paradox for the people and in my report i ask that more efforts be made to make it clear to the people of kosovo that more energy savings will have to be made, that energy will also have to be paid for, so that we can achieve a more sustainable policy. finally, ladies and gentlemen, i have received reports of malfunctions at the kosovo electricity company, but i mr president, i would like to raise a point which is relevant to the two policies which are the subject of this afternoon's debate. this is the problem of the use of weapons and munitions containing depleted uranium. i would remind you that on 15 january 2001 the european parliament voted a resolution calling for a moratorium on the use of these weapons and ammunition. recently, on 27 march 2002, the united nations environment programme published the results of a study of six sites in serbia and montenegro that were affected by depleted uranium munitions during the 1999 balkan war. the study confirms general contamination for five of the six sites concerned; for two of these sites, the presence of depleted uranium particles in the surrounding air is noted. the results of the measurements show that depleted uranium dust was widely dispersed in the environment as a result of the impact of these munitions. even if the level of radioactive pollution is considered low by the united nations environment programme, the fact remains that this pollution confirms the risks that the use of weapons and ammunition with depleted uranium poses to the military, of course, but also to the civilian population, probably for several generations. today, although no official information confirms this, there is a great deal of evidence to support the thesis that a significant part of the heavily bombed afghan territory was contaminated by depleted uranium: a statement by secretary of state rumsfeld about a contaminated site, the concern expressed by pakistani scientists and, above all, confirmation of the use of remote-controlled bombs with very dense metal penetrators, probably depleted uranium. we have received information that in the coming weeks the unep is to begin a study on the impact of the war on the environment. however, there are still questions i would like to ask the council: is the council considering taking any special measures to protect the troops and humanitarian missions on the ground in afghanistan? what is the council's view of the long-term use of these types of munitions and weapons, given that they are indiscriminate weapons affecting both civilian and military populations?

the rapporteur. - maybe the interest is not overwhelming, but that doesn't make the legislation we're talking about any less important. we are working on the fourth report in the eric package and i think that together these two eric packages can represent a major step towards greater safety at sea. in the first package, we had the port state control, the classification society and the double-walled tankers. we are now at the first report of the second era package, which deals with measures to improve control and information systems on board ships and to better manage risks, especially in the event of bad weather. that is the purpose of the directive we are working on. so it is mainly about preventive measures, and i think they are particularly important. firstly, the control and information systems, which involve the installation on ships of transponder systems that automatically transmit a certain amount of data to the shore. the important thing is not so much the list of what is to be sent - we all agree on that - but that the data should be understood between the different member states. so the computer systems can talk to each other, and so can the people. i think we have to be very careful that that doesn't happen. secondly, and i think we should look at this when we are revising the directive, is the range of the transponders. at the moment, it is relatively limited. i think it is in the best interests of all of us - and i am looking to the commissioner - to try, in a revision of the directive, to ensure, in cooperation with the imo, a wider range, possibly with satellite communications, and to ensure that the system can actually cover the whole globe. the second point is the black box, which was already mentioned in mr watts' report. we fought hard to get it, but she's here. the black box is not a panacea either, but it is a very useful tool, and we also need to make sure that that tool is used in prevention, that we learn from near misses and from accidents, to avoid other accidents. we focus our checks on ships that are at higher risk because of their cargo, because of the history they have. and we must also ensure that every incident - and i think that is a very good point in the commission's proposal - is reported from now on, so that we also have a better knowledge of what is actually happening at sea, which is actually quite limited so far. the next point relates to the fact that most serious accidents at sea, with large and serious consequences, occur in bad weather, and concerns better management of the risks of bad weather and storms at sea. and we have been able to bring in a number of points which i think can help, and i think parliament's contribution has been great there. the authorities must take action in bad weather: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the fishing vessel is not caught by the fishermen. but i would like to stress that the responsibility for the ship, in any case, ultimately lies with the captain. i think we have also clearly restored that in parliament and that is a very important point. another point. member states should provide assistance to ships in distress. i think that is also essential and that is also something that parliament, together with the commission and the council, has put more sharply than was originally the case in the text. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted by the introduction of new fishing vessels, and that the community's fishing fleet is not disrupted by the introduction of new fishing vessels. the commission has undertaken to investigate this and to report to parliament within three years to see how things stand. colleagues, i would like to thank all those who have worked with us for their contribution, for the effort made. i think we did a good job. i would also like to thank the commission services and the people in the council, because i think that there has been a very good and constructive cooperation and that we have finally got a good piece of legislation. what is important, commissioner, is that we now, as we complete the six measures of the two eric packages, also monitor what is happening. the european parliament, and i am sure the rapporteurs of these various reports, must, together with the commission, examine at regular intervals what has happened, where we are and what difficulties we are facing. we have legislation, i think we have made good legislation, but safety, mr president, also at sea, is a human task. and i think we should also invest in those people, also in the union, in people who are doing better safety checks at sea and on land.
- the fact that the report before us today is of considerable interest in my country, flanders, has, strangely enough, less to do with the substance of the community patent case than with the proposed language regime, which unfortunately once again distinguishes between first- and second-class languages within the european union. the rapporteur is proposing, in effect following the council's position, that the procedure, the working methods of the office should now also apply to the community patent. i am very clear that this is unacceptable for us, for flanders, and that all the working languages of the european union must be treated equally, certainly including dutch, my language, which is spoken by more than 21 million europeans. and of course, there's a high cost associated with that. i know that. that is the price we must pay if we want to preserve the diversity which is the richness of our continent. i am also very much aware that the member states of the so-called 'major' languages are also prepared to make the european union bear these heavy costs if their language is threatened. the ovv, the consultative centre of flemish associations, a large representative organisation, which represents a large number of flemish cultural and political associations in my country, is demanding the same thing and decides that if the european union fails to do so, and i quote, "europe is in danger of becoming more and more an alien occupation rather than a partnership based on equality and respect for the national identity of all its members, as is stated in the basic treaties". this is a serious warning from a large and serious cultural-political association. the european union must also listen to this.
- mr president, commissioners and colleagues. we insist on the principle of equality between all languages. that is our priority. we are naturally in favour of a community patent. it is a service to the community of european citizens and i believe that all european citizens should be able to turn to the union's bodies in their own language, including for the application of a patent, and receive an answer in their own language. discrimination on linguistic grounds would be contrary to community law and the charter of fundamental rights. the choice of five languages is therefore not an honourable compromise. we ask the parliament, the council and the commission to support our amendments, which allow everyone to apply for a patent in his own language and also receive a reply in his own language, with translations into the other official languages of the community. we therefore believe that the use of the three working languages in the bureau would not be contrary to these principles, since it would also save money and avoid having everything done entirely in english.

commission. – mr president, i wish to begin by thanking mr florenz for the work carried out in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection and for the cooperation with the commission. let me briefly recall why we are tackling the specific problem of electrical and electronic waste. it is the fastest-growing part of the waste stream, indeed it is growing three times faster than the average. each of us produces on average about 14 kgs per year of this waste and by the year 2010, when this directive is fully operational, electrical and electronic waste will account for over 8% of the entire municipal waste stream. once adopted and implemented the directive on waste electrical and electronic equipment will allow us to divert this type of waste from landfills and incinerators to environmentally sound re-use and recycling. the directive on restricting hazardous substances in electrical and electronic equipment will prevent the presence of unnecessary hazardous substances, posing risks for human health and the environment in various stages of the life-cycle of such equipment, including its waste management. the work undertaken by the european parliament substantially reinforces the original proposal, and i welcome the majority of the amendments tabled. in particular i support the strengthening of individual financing, as proposed by the committee on the environment, and limiting collective systems to those cases where these are necessary for practical reasons. this can help to internalise waste management costs into the product price and give incentives for a better design for re-use and recycling. it is a first step towards fuller integration of environmental considerations into product design and consumption patterns. we also need to find appropriate solutions for the financing of historical waste put on the market before the entry into force of this directive. one of the options to achieve this may also be the use of a visible fee. this should, however, be done in line with articles 88 and 89 of the treaty. i welcome the principle of increasing the collection target to 6 kgs per capita and per year, though the timetable foreseen needs to be more realistic and take into account the time needed to collect and process data. i also support the proposal to advance the phase-out date for the substance bans in the rohs directive to 2006, though this should be done in accordance with the principles set out in article 95 of the treaty. let me also answer some of the points raised during the debate. i can assure you that we have carefully evaluated all aspects of this proposal, including its costs. for the overall target of 4 kg per capita collected waste, we estimate that the costs will be in the region of eur 500 to 900 million. we also base our estimates and evaluation on the existing systems, of which there are not many: the netherlands is the one example from which we can draw conclusions and learn lessons. these figures have been confirmed by practical experience, for example in the netherlands. if parliament wants to increase the collection target, this is obviously a political decision, and we agree with that. when it comes to subsidiarity, we have tried to strike an appropriate balance between harmonising certain elements of the proposal and leaving others to subsidiarity. this is why the way the collection systems operate is left to the member states. we also think it is not necessary to define at community level who is to finance the collection point up to the moment the equipment is brought to it. this is why we reject both amendments nos 69 and 83. what about small and medium-sized enterprises? the committee on the environment has voted in favour of deleting a temporary exemption for smes from the financing obligations of the directive. we can accept this point as we think that practice has shown that there are no excessive costs for these companies and that such an exemption is not needed, and it might even lead to market distortions. mr blokland, i hope that any day now we will be able to present the batteries proposal and i am working hard on that. finally, a total of 82 amendments have been tabled on the weee directive and 18 on the rohs directive. on the waste electrical and electronic equipment proposal, the commission can accept 32 amendments in full, two in part and 20 in principle. the commission can accept amendments nos 9, 11-19, 24, 27-37, 39, 40, 43, 44, 45, 50, 57, 65, 76 and 77. in amendment no 23, the commission supports a new paragraph 1 and paragraph 1(b), last subparagraph in full and paragraph 1(b), third and fourth subparagraphs as well as paragraph 2 in principle. in amendment no 73, the commission can accept the first part. in amendments nos 1, 3, 5, 6, 7, 10, the first, third and fourth sentences can be accepted in full. in amendments nos 20, 21 and 41, the first sentence can be accepted in full. amendments nos 42, 48, 49, 51-55, 66, 78 and 92 can be accepted in principle. on the rohs proposal, the commission can accept four amendments in full, one in part and seven in principle. the amendments acceptable in full are nos 3, 4, 10 and 17. amendment no 5 can be accepted in part – first paragraph – and in amendments nos 2, 6, 7, 8 and 9, the first paragraph can be accepted in full and amendments nos 13 and 15 in principle. i thank all members for this important debate.
the rapporteur. - mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it was on 27 october 2000 that the council of ministers adopted its common position on this draft directive on the placing on the market and use of certain dangerous substances, in this case pentabromodiphenyl ether, a flame retardant used - i would remind you - to 95% in the manufacture of flexible polyurethane foam. pentabde is bioaccumulative and persistent; it has the property of exuding foam and entering our blood and nervous system through the air around us. i therefore welcome the fact that the common position - as suggested by parliament in its first reading vote - bans pentabde in all its forms, including when it is a residue from the production of a sister chemical, octabromodiphenyl ether. this ban is scheduled for july 2003. the rest, however, i would say is a long way off. the council, following the commission's opinion, did not accept parliament's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to all polybrominated diphenyl ethers on the market: octabde and decabde in this case. it should be noted that the argument put forward by a majority of member states to justify this refusal is twofold: first, to await the final results of the risk assessments currently under way; and secondly, to ensure, before any ban on a flame retardant, that there are equally effective substitutes. may i remind you that this is exactly the reasoning followed by parliament at first reading and by our committee on the environment when it voted on 21 march - at any rate for decabromodiphenyl ether. let me explain: in asking for a ban on this substance, postponed until 1 january 2006 at the latest and subject here to the final results of the risk analysis, parliament is taking into account the latest available scientific information provided by the united kingdom, which is the rapporteur state for the environmental part of the risk assessment. the uk experts concluded by suggesting that the competent authorities should consider the need to apply the precautionary principle also to octabde and decabde. the european parliament is also responding to the wishes of the council and the commission, namely compliance with the rules laid down in the 1993 regulation on the evaluation and control of existing substances, the existence of substitutes and the difficult balance to be struck between the obvious fire safety qualities on the one hand and the proven environmental and health hazards on the other. in this connection, i would remind you that decabde, which accounts for 80% of brominated fire protection products, is a particularly strong and effective flame retardant. in the year 2000 alone, 155 lives were saved and thousands of serious burns avoided in the uk alone, thanks to these substances. that is why the committee on the environment did not want to apply the precautionary principle strictly, as i said, but gradually. that is the purpose of amendment no 4, which was adopted unanimously, and that is why i am opposed, both at first reading and during the vote in committee, to amendments nos 9 and 10 tabled by my colleagues mrs schörling and mr sjöstedt. i am in favour of amendment no 7 which provides for greater editorial clarity by explicit reference to decabde in the recitals. as regards octabde and its forthcoming ban, which i believe to be justified, there is nothing new and reassuring about this substance that would allow parliament to change its position adopted on 6 september. the results currently reported by the french authorities remain up to date, intermediate results which demonstrate the harmfulness of octabde to health and the environment. another reason for our request to restrict the placing on the market of this flame retardant and for the reintroduction of amendments nos 1, 2 and 3 is that octabde is used in a fairly small number of products and can be replaced in many cases by decabromodiphenyl ether, and it is on this basis that my colleagues and i call for the precautionary principle to prevail over all other considerations. a final word for you, commissioner. the commission is developing programmes and increasing the number of initiatives to ensure an ever higher level of protection of human health and the environment. the new strategy on chemicals initiated by your services is the best example of this. a strong and reassuring signal would be given here by a commitment to implement as soon as possible: a risk reduction policy for all polybrominated diphenyl ethers. i have of course noted the commission's progress in this case with its statement in the minutes of the common position in which you undertake to examine urgently the need for a new proposal to restrict the marketing or use of octabde and decabde, but the conditions imposed, mainly the need to wait again and again for the completion of the risk assessments, risk in this case to further burden the timetable where emergency measures are required. because where there is scientific uncertainty, there is political choice. that is why i think it is right not to wait for the end of an endless story of risk analysis and to favour a balanced solution, the main thing being to reconcile the immediate safety of citizens in the face of fire risks with the longer-term benefits for public health and the environment.
