- mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, with this report - for which i thank mrs de sarnez - the time is right for the european union to reflect on how it conceives the right to education - and more specifically to higher education - and how it intends to implement it. the twentieth century has seen the universal acceptance of human rights as the guiding principle of our construction. it is our responsibility to see that they are fully respected and implemented, because education is not only a human right, it is also an essential means of promoting peace - a crucial issue today - and respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms in general. for its potential contribution to building a world of peace to be realized, education must be universal, made equally accessible to all. the task is enormous, but let us not forget that even today there are more than 800 million illiterate adults on this planet and nearly 100 million children of secondary school age who do not have access to school. and millions of those who are in school today cannot get a quality education that meets their basic educational needs. the need to respond to these needs is growing in a world where the upheavals caused by globalisation and the revolution in information and communication technologies are marginalizing entire populations in extreme poverty, making them easy prey for extremism of all stripes. the right to education is now less a matter of access to education than of access to adequate training opportunities for all to meet basic needs. in this context, the right to higher education - as we see - is not yet a reality, especially for women. in higher education, which according to the universal declaration of human rights must be open and equally accessible to all on the basis of personal merit, enrollment in africa and asia is less than one in ten in the age group concerned, and the gap between these regions and europe and north america continues to widen. while there is no doubt that the realization of the right to higher education has made considerable progress throughout the world over the last half-century, it must not be forgotten that this vision, enshrined in article 26, is not only quantitative but also qualitative, since it embraces the aims and objectives of education, and therefore its contents. i am therefore very attentive to the task that remains - and i thank mrs de sarnez again for her report - and i think it is fundamental that for the people who are still excluded from all education, we know that there is no choice but that many people do not even know that they have a choice. if we are talking about dignity, about demands, about equal and inalienable rights, i sincerely hope that this report will enable the european parliament to continue to secure its place and not to stop along the way.
- mr president, commissioner, colleagues, children are worth their weight in gold. this is a statement that is usually made by parents and grandparents, but it would be good to make it in future when it comes to policy. as there has been a two-way interaction between parents and children, there must be an interaction between children and policy at all levels. in this regard, it is important to take into account the fact that children have changed a lot in recent times. it is a consequence of the fact that family coexistence and the division of roles within the family are no longer obvious. the traditional family is no longer the norm. kids want to be a part of it, the age aspect is no longer a factor. it is a message that policy-makers should take to heart. children should be treated as full partners in decision-making. it is the policy's task to provide for the appropriate procedures and consultative bodies. it would be particularly important if the european parliament were to take the initiative of providing a forum for children from the member states, but also for children from third countries, in the european parliament's buildings.
the rapporteur. - mr president, the thread running through the 2001 human rights report is actually the mainstreaming of the european union's human rights policy. first of all, it is necessary to put an end to the bad tradition in which the european union and the member states, in resolutions and reports, give high priority to the concept of human rights, but in practice, in their daily foreign policy, often allow other interests to prevail. i have just returned from geneva, where i attended the 58th session of the un commission on human rights with a delegation from parliament. a meeting with the 15 eu ambassadors revealed not only that the world's leading human rights forum is facing a colossal credibility problem, but also that the union is failing to pursue a consistent and coherent human rights policy, to speak with one voice. the european representatives in the un committee voted split last week on a motion on humanitarian situations in the middle east. of course, such a thing affects our moral authority. moreover, the impression is given that the european union is eager to sponsor resolutions on human rights violations in countries such as congo, sudan and colombia, but is much more reticent when it comes to strategically or economically important partners such as china. the new eu guidelines for human rights dialogues, if at least implemented, will undoubtedly strengthen the coherence and consistency of eu human rights policy. these guidelines also form the basis of the programme document which gives priority to the european initiative for democracy and human rights. commissioner patten has done groundbreaking work on this. however, we are concerned about its implementation. despite the high priority given by parliament to this budgetary chapter, appropriations were already reduced in 2002. in my report i have concentrated on two topical issues: the european union is also committed to the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, including the right to freedom of expression and the right to freedom of expression. although officially abolished 50 years ago, slavery continues to exist today in many forms: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. the few figures available are alarming. the european union has a great interest in tackling this growing problem, especially in the current climate in europe, where the far right is on the rise. this report calls for national and international legal changes to prosecute traffickers, protect victims and prevent people from being forced into modern forms of slavery. the second theme deals with the complex relationship between terrorism and human rights in the wake of the september 11 attacks. it is clear that terrorist attacks are serious crimes and should be treated as such. but human rights violations can also be a breeding ground for terrorism and at the same time the fight against terrorism can give rise to new human rights violations, serve as justification for human rights violations or make countries turn a blind eye to violations by allies in the fight against terrorism. prominent politicians such as lord russell johnson have warned of a repetition of the mistakes of the cold war, when dictatorships in developing countries were supported in the name of fighting communism. it is important, as part of the fight against international terrorism, to address the underlying causes that fuel extremist sentiments or are used by terrorists to recruit followers. the attacks of 11 september and the fight against terrorism have particularly painfully exposed the shortcomings of international law. the geneva conventions still assume that wars between states are the greatest threat to peace, whereas most of today's conflicts are civil wars or terrorist acts. the geneva convention on prisoners of war is based on the protection of soldiers of regular armies, whereas today the majority of combatants are guerrillas and militias and the majority of victims are civilians. the report therefore calls for new rules, new conventions and new instruments to take account of these new types of conflict. the union will have to play a leading role in the revision of international law and will have to ensure that the international criminal court becomes operational as soon as possible. we, as a european parliament with a world-wide reputation in the field of human rights, must be vigilant in ensuring that a number of universal, fundamental human rights are respected in all circumstances. i would also like to thank the members who have contributed to a balanced report which has already been widely accepted by the committee on foreign affairs. i hope that the whole of parliament will be able to support this human rights report tomorrow.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the report by mrs berger, whom i thank for the great work she has done, has clearly reignited the classic struggle in this parliament for more or less harmonization. it may be worth recalling that the original commission proposal was very much aimed at maximum harmonisation of distance selling of financial services. the council, on the other hand, took a completely different view. of course, there is much to be said for the commission's original position. it was undoubtedly the best solution, both for financial service providers and for consumers. the division in parliament, however, does not allow for this best solution to be imposed. in its common position, the council intends to introduce exceptions to the principle of maximum harmonization. this is unfortunate, because national provisions can be an obstacle to the completion of the internal market. only full harmonisation will guarantee a level playing field between institutions and consumer confidence in services offered from other member states. the exceptions to the principle of full harmonisation may seriously hamper e-commerce by obliging service providers to take account of provisions contained in the 15 different legal systems. i would also like to stress that the principle of the law of the country of origin applies in principle where there is no complete harmonization. this will be extremely disadvantageous for some institutions because the laws differ greatly in terms of restrictions. in conclusion, i can say that maximum harmonization is still the preferred approach, but once again it is clear that politics is the art of the possible. and maybe it's better to have one bird in the hand than ten in the air. to put it more clearly: it's better to have a less good solution than no solution at all.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, despite the tragic events of 11 september, the tourism sector is alive and well. it is in fact in a phase of continuous expansion at european and global level. as an economic sector, tourism accounts for 5% of jobs in the european union. the bulk of activity and employment is accounted for by some 2 million small and medium-sized enterprises, employing some 8 million people. moreover, these jobs are spread over various service sectors such as transport and the hotel industry. small and mediumsized enterprises are thus clearly the driving force of and for european tourism. the proposed actions should therefore be linked to actions under the multiannual programme for small and medium-sized enterprises. the question whether a community tourism policy should be drawn up for the tourist industry is, at least for the time being, answered in the negative. this does not mean that tourism policy cannot be coordinated and guided more effectively. tourism policy often gives the impression of being fragmented and chaotic. therefore, action in the field of harmonisation of information, the establishment of harmonised labels and networking should be strongly encouraged. the commission's initiative to establish an annual european tourism forum, where the industry can set its own priorities, also deserves our full support. in short, closer cooperation between all the parties involved to make tourism more competitive and sustainable seems to be a priority.

these backward, obscurantist perspectives are what we reject when we affirm the european will to create a research and innovation area. but beyond philosophical considerations, the sixth framework programme also marks europe's clear and resolute commitment to being at the forefront of technology, to being innovative, competitive, dynamic and to building our future. i want to see this in the many specific contributions it contains: human and social research, the principles of integrating the environment, sustainable development, new mobility, equal opportunities, the role of universities, education and openness to the world. the coordination between the various components of the european union under the two presidencies, belgian and spanish, is also remarkable. it is also worth noting the constant communication with the scientific community, the prospects for the creation of a network and the way in which this european parliament and the commission have developed to keep in touch with the real needs, the real demands of the population. a europe of research at the service of its people, of its researchers, is how we want europe to be built in everyday life and in reality.
mr president, commissioner, i too would like to thank our distinguished colleague, mr bruno trentin, on behalf of my group, and congratulate him not only on his personal qualities but also because the report which was adopted last night in the committee on economic and monetary affairs contains, in my view, a strong political message. the message is that if this report is adopted, parliament rejects the strategy of waiting on the governments of the member states and calls for higher quality growth. this message is expressed by the fact that in the triangle that economists are familiar with, namely the triangle formed by the volume of public debts, the volume of public revenue and the volume of public expenditure - whether it is current expenditure or, above all, the investment expenditure covered by this report - it is also a question of taking into account the level of public expenditure required to achieve the objectives defined in lisbon and also in gothenburg. and it doesn't take a great clergyman to see that this message is contrary to what we usually hear. all we need to do is look at the election programmes of the main parties, which all emphasize the decline in public revenue without really specifying which public expenditure should therefore decline by that much. so in the face of an accelerating situation of tax competition between member states, i think amendment no 7 is an absolutely crucial amendment, because it warns against the pressure on public expenditure. in addition to the tax aspects which, in our opinion, are not sufficiently developed in the report, this is also why amendment no 8 seems to us to be the bare minimum with regard to corporation tax and the implementation of the conclusions of the primarolo report. but we should also - - i want to thank the commissioner for his clear answer. i have to say that i was pleased with the ngo reports myself. i would just like to ask the commissioner, for the sake of clarity, whether, when these discussions are concluded, he will return to the general affairs council to obtain an official mandate, or does the commission already have the mandate in its own right? water was the subject of a great controversy, even in europe.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. as regards the framework programme for research and technological development, the commission recalls that the text of the decision on the fifth framework programme, which is currently being implemented after being adopted by parliament and the council in a codecision procedure, states that funding under this programme should be granted to projects pursuing civilian objectives. this means in practice that specific support for the arms industry is not possible through the research framework programme. as regards the structural funds, in 1993, in the context of the crisis in the defence industry, the commission launched a programme called 'konver', a programme of aid for the conversion of the employment pools most affected by the reduction of armaments activities or the closure of military sites. the aim of the community initiatives was to support the diversification of areas heavily dependent on defence by the conversion of defence-related activities and the development of activities in non-military industrial sectors. the multiannual konver initiative, from 1994 to 1999, financed measures such as the acquisition by staff of new civilian skills, the diversification of the economic structure of the enterprise, aid for the development of small and medium-sized enterprises, the reallocation of military sites to new sectors of civilian activity and the rehabilitation of the environment. budgetary funding for these structural funds amounted to eur 739 million at 1996 prices, an average of eur 125 million per year. in the 2000/2006 programming period, konver was abolished as a separate initiative, but actions funded by konver remain possible under objective 1 and 2 programmes.

the rapporteur. - in rio in 1992, the concept of sustainable development was enshrined by the international community. in the last ten years, there has undoubtedly been a change in the discourse on the environment and development, a positive change. solemn commitments have been made, conventions have been signed - if not ratified, at least signed - but it must be said that ten years later the situation of the environment has not improved, it has even deteriorated, and as far as the situation of the poor and developing countries is concerned, it has not improved either. inequality has increased; today, more than 800 million people continue to be undernourished, a billion people lack access to safe drinking water. this is clearly a difficult situation to bear. the johannesburg summit, which will take place in a few months' time, will not only be a moment of reckoning but also a moment of truth. we cannot be satisfied with an ecological mass, which would repeat a second time what was said in rio. nor can we be satisfied with a concert of lamentations: there must be an ambitious programme of political change on the agenda for the next ten years. that is the message the committee on development has asked me to convey to you. what we are proposing is that the fight against poverty and hunger and the equitable and sustainable use of natural resources should be given the highest priority. the message we are sending to the plenary of this parliament, to the commission and to the council is centred on five chapters: the first is the fight against poverty and the promotion of social development; the second is the fight against unemployment and the fight against poverty; the third is the fight against unemployment and the fight against social exclusion; the fourth is the fight against poverty and the fight against unemployment; the fifth is the fight against unemployment and the fight against social exclusion. there are a whole series of concrete proposals. the second is to control globalization. what does that mean? this clearly means that sustainable development must be the dominant concept. so trade regulations, international trade rules, investment rules must be subject to the criteria of sustainable development and not the other way around. the third is to manage and conserve natural resources sustainably. this means the development of new technologies, and i am thinking in particular of renewable energy sources. the proposals we are making in this area are both credible and ambitious. fourthly, the commission itself said in the first document published last year that the patterns of production and consumption must be changed. true, but that involves a number of decisions, some of which are difficult. i think, for example, that as far as we are concerned, certain policies of the european union - i am thinking in particular of the common agricultural policy, the fisheries policy and the trade policy - must be reviewed to avoid undermining the objectives of sustainable development. it will also give us much greater credibility at the international level, especially at the johannesburg meeting, if we sweep our own front door before we say what needs to be done at the international level. the fifth, finally. good governance must be ensured at all levels. the concept of good governance is often tainted with paternalism. i believe that applying it to all levels of decision-making, and particularly to international institutions, also makes the use of this term more credible. i think, for example, that international economic institutions, such as the international monetary fund, the world bank, the world trade organization, need a serious facelift in order to introduce a minimum of democracy and a minimum of citizen and political control. that is the essence of the message which the committee on development and cooperation would like to see taken up by the plenary and carried forward by the commission - i am addressing myself to commissioner nielson - since in the next few weeks there is going to be an important meeting: the bali meeting, the last preparatory meeting for the johannesburg summit. we have no reason to be optimistic at the moment, since there is no really ambitious document on the table yet. i hope that our contribution will help the european institutions to play a leading role in this preparatory meeting, if there is still time, and in any case i hope that we will avoid the pretense that entrusting the management of sustainable development problems to partnership initiatives with the private sector is the solution to the problem. this can be part of the solution, but in addition to commitments made by states and international institutions, not as a substitute. that is my message, mr president.
the rapporteur. - on paragraph 22, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, there are two amendments: one which i tabled on behalf of my group to take account of a proposal which had been made in committee by the epp rapporteur, mr wijkman. the aim was to add the reference to a global co2 tax. i think the epp amendment to delete the paragraph is acceptable if it is an addition, but if it is a deletion, i cannot accept it. i think it completely distorts the meaning of the original text. i would ask, if the epp agrees to accept it as an addition, to vote first on my amendment, which was in fact tabled at the request of the epp, and then on the epp amendment as an addition, provided that i have the epp's agreement.

- mr president, the violence between the different groups in india cannot go unmentioned by the european union. of course, this is a very important matter which must be considered in the light of the interest which india represents. not just in asia, but in the world. we must not forget that india is not only a great power with great economic potential but also a nation with nuclear capability. it is to the credit of the joint resolution that people have not been led to adopt radical positions. it should be clear to everyone that india is not a clash of civilizations. it should be stressed that muslims are largely integrated into indian society. a concrete example of this so-called multicultural society is the fact that muslims hold high government positions. but this does not change the fact that i deeply regret what happened in gujarat. i would like to point out that the events in gujarat have not spread to the rest of india. this is, in my view, proof that the hindu-muslim coalition still respects democratic values. on the other hand, we should be concerned about the growing muslim fundamentalism that is striking kashmir on a daily basis. the day before yesterday, nine more people were killed in an attack on an indian army camp. extremist militias of muslim activists demanding accession to pakistan are very active here. last tuesday, 32 people were killed in an indian army camp. this time, indian women and children were hiding here. these facts should also be brought to the attention of the international community in the context of this resolution. this dramatic incident once again underlines the enormous threat posed by the ever-increasing terrorism. i believe that the european union and the european parliament have a strong interest in continuing to support india as a state of law where hindus and muslims are treated equally in its efforts to respect democratic principles.
the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would first of all like to reassure you that i shall not be needing the ten minutes which have been so urgently allotted to me. mr president, the two reports which i have the honour of presenting to this house today are very different in nature and scope, although they both concern the same subject, namely europol, which is, as everyone knows, the european police office. the first is the report on the initiative of the kingdom of belgium, which relates to the adjustment of the basic salaries of europol staff. it is of very limited scope and obviously does not require long comments. in simple and clear terms, i am formally proposing that this house reject this initiative for two main reasons. firstly, i do not see why, and i hope parliament will agree with me, europol staff should be given a 5.2% increase for the period from july 2001 to july 2002, while european union officials in the netherlands, in the same city of the hague, will see their salaries and allowances increased by 4.3% for the same period. we have not been given any serious and credible justification for this increase. secondly, i think it is inconsistent and unacceptable that the council should consult the european parliament on such a routine matter when it systematically refuses to do so on the europol budget, even though both decisions fall within the procedure provided for in title vi of the treaty on european union and require the consultation of the european parliament. i therefore propose without hesitation or emotion that this initiative be rejected. the second report, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, is of a completely different nature. this is an initiative of the kingdom of belgium and the kingdom of spain which proposes, in addition to a necessary adaptation of europol's missions, a new mechanism to amend the europol convention. as you know, ladies and gentlemen, europol is an institution which is purely and simply an intergovernmental cooperation. in this context, any amendment, however important or urgent, to the convention governing europol shall require ratification by all member states in accordance with the requirements of their respective constitutional rules. this means, for example, that the ratification of the europol convention took no less than three long and successful years. it is obvious, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, that this is a procedure that is too long, too slow and totally inadequate if europol is to be given the capacity to react quickly to the many and changing forms of serious crime. by proposing that the council should henceforth adopt the amendments to the europol convention, the initiative of the kingdoms of belgium and spain seems to be taking a step in the right direction. in reality, in my view and in the view of the committee on civil liberties, this initiative suffers from three major shortcomings: 'it maintains europol in law within the framework of simple intergovernmental cooperation, contrary to all the requests made on many occasions by our parliament and even though the european council at virtually every one of its meetings entrusts more and more tasks to europol for the benefit of the union as a whole; 2. this decision, after the enlargement of the union, may lead to excessive slowness or even a complete blockage of the decisionmaking process because the convention provides and will continue to provide that all council decisions concerning europol must be taken unanimously. i ask my colleagues how they think we as 27 can agree unanimously on operational police decisions when we have crime in europe and high crime in certain sectors; 3. this proposal confirms the purely marginal role of the european parliament in all matters relating to europol, while depriving the european parliament for the future of the legal means and institutional framework which could enable it in the future to exercise genuine democratic control. i do not object to the fact that, thanks to the convention, there is a certain democratic control which is currently exercised over europol by means of the national parliaments. anyone who knows how europol works and how national parliaments work will know that there is no serious democratic control over europol at the moment. the council's choice to propose a system which is tainted by the serious shortcomings i have just mentioned is all the more inexplicable, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, since there is an alternative way in the treaty which would allow the problems i have just mentioned to be solved in a simple way. it is sufficient to make use of the provision formally provided for in article 34 (2) (c) of the treaty on european union, which simply replaces the europol convention by a council decision. the use of this provision would have the direct effect of integrating europol into the third pillar and thus into the system of community law, which would have three considerable short-term benefits: 1. improving europol's operational capabilities, since under article 34 of the treaty all council decisions relating to europol should be taken by majority vote, without any possibility of derogation; 2. the improvement of parliament's control over europol, because under article 39 of the treaty the european parliament should be consulted on all implementing measures adopted by the council and, secondly, it would have the possibility of bringing an action before the court of justice in the event of non-respect of these rights; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is on the basis of this argument, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, that i ask our assembly to reject the initiative of the kingdom of belgium and the kingdom of spain on the grounds that it is not sufficiently operational and democratic - a bad day for the kingdom of spain! - and to approve the draft recommendation which aims to transform the europol convention into a council decision and thereby integrate it into the institutional system of the european union. i would like to make it clear, as my colleague mrs frassoni has expressly said, that for me this operation must be only a transitional phase, preferably a brief one, before full com munitization, that is to say before the merging of the treaties and the abolition of the paralysing and archaic distinction between the three pillars. i hope that in this area the convention will be able to meet the challenges of history and the expectations of the citizens.
mr president, i have to go back eleven years. it was in 1991 that the council of environment ministers adopted a directive which will be debated this evening on the placing of plant protection products on the market. the aim of the directive was to define a common procedure for the evaluation of active substances for existing and new pesticides and to establish a positive list of substances authorized as being acceptable from the point of view of health and the environment. positive list means that everything that is not on this list is de facto prohibited. the timeframe chosen for the enormous evaluation work was 12 years. clearly this brings us to july 2003. but in the meantime the commission was required by article 8 to publish a report on the progress of the procedure. this report was published at the end of last year and is the subject of our debate this evening. what does the commission find? that of the 834 active substances on the market, 31 had been evaluated, i.e. less than 5%. it is clear that in these circumstances it is impossible to meet the deadline of july 2003, as the commission rightly observes. i shall not dwell on the causes of the enormous delay which has been imposed on us, except that it is largely due to a lack of political will on the part of both the commission and the member states. the member states had shared the task of carrying out the evaluation of the substances. the commission is proposing today to extend the deadlines and, for reasons of realism, the committee on the environment, for which i am the spokesman tonight, must agree to an extension. but while accepting this extension, she attached to her agreement strict conditions. i would like to mention a few: adoption of clear priorities for the new work schedule, priority for substances that are problematic for environmental health and drinking water, refusal to grant a bonus for laxity - in other words, companies that have not submitted their dossier within the deadline will not be able to have it dealt with, unless the requirements of the directive appear to be easily met. finally, we ask for a regular report on the progress of the evaluation to be sent to the european parliament and the council. the commission should also, as the committee on the environment would like, submit a proposal for a revision of the 1991 directive before the end of 2003. why this proposal for revision ? the commission itself proposes it, so we are only supporting it by adding comments which i think are relevant. there were major flaws in the process that took place. i believe that it is not enough for a pesticide to devalue the active substances, but that it is also necessary to evaluate the metabolites, that is to say the decomposition products, which are sometimes more dangerous than the basic substances. the committee on the environment also supports the introduction of the concept of comparative evaluation and the principle of substitution, which will lead to the emergence on the market and the use of the least toxic substances, the more toxic ones being eliminated. i would add that new criteria - in ten years things have changed, and even more so in twelve years - must be taken into account, such as the absence of endocrine disrupting substances - in 1991 there was no mention of endocrine disrupting substances; since then, it has become an important topic in environmental policy - or the specific impact on the health of children and foetuses, as well as the synergy issues related to exposure to several pesticides. in conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, i would ask you to support the report of the committee on the environment, drawing your attention to a particular point, which is paragraph 14 of the resolution. it called on the commission to publish a further proposal for a directive before july 2003 which would establish a programme to reduce the use of pesticides. a number of member states have so far done so. i think the environment committee is right to support this proposal which not only aims to bring the 1991 directive to an end but also to reduce the use of pesticides, which is certainly a very good way of reducing the risks.
- mr president, the commission's report makes it clear that the operation of directive 91/414/eec is marked by a number of serious shortcomings. the rapporteur, who has done a splendid job, as i have said on several occasions, and who has the full support of my group, is therefore quite right in criticising some of these shortcomings in his report. for example, it appears that it is not so much the intrinsic characteristics as the commercial interests that are decisive, that the evaluation is limited to certain aspects of, for example, hormonal disorders, and that effects on children are hardly taken into account. but the rapporteur has been thinking about this for a long time. we must conclude that we have no guarantee that substances on the positive list are really safe for man and the environment. we have little choice but to agree to an extension of the review deadlines, because so far only 3% of the substances have been evaluated. but we do so on a number of very specific conditions. the rapporteur has dealt with this in detail, so i shall not repeat it. i would like to take this opportunity to raise another point. pesticide policy cannot be limited to the authorization of substances as regulated by this directive, especially since it is unable to reverse the continuing increase in the use and dependence of pesticides, and thus the presence of their residues in water and food. we have some important things to do and i am waiting, along with many others, for the commission's initiative. there is a wide range of instruments that we must use at european level. firstly, the additional financial support for research into and application of non-chemical pesticides should be substantially increased, both for integrated cropping and for organic farming. secondly, compulsory training in integrated cropping methods and good agricultural practices is essential. thirdly, the lack of data requires the urgent establishment at european level of a publicly accessible database of both sales and use data on all pesticides and their toxicological properties. fourthly, a european harmonized level of vat. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial instruments are harmonized, but at a sufficiently high level. now it's between three and twenty-five percent, and we have to do something about that. we also need special taxes on external environmental costs associated with products and product prices. and last but not least, the commission has already taken a number of measures to reduce the use of pesticides, and in particular the dependence on them.
in his excellent report, the rapporteur again underlines the importance of the tens. i am therefore all the more surprised that the european parliament should not be involved in the decision-making on this list of priority projects. parliament demanded that in the first decision in 1996, but now it has that opportunity and is not using it. the commission supported parliament in 1996 and now, as is mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr rapporteur, you have been very much commended here today and i should also like to commend you for the commitment and persistence with which you have ensured that our rules of procedure are modernised. you won't blame me for sharing my concerns with you. what makes a parliament a parliament is, of course, the debate of people who are present with mind and heart and who represent the views of their own constituency. people from different backgrounds trying to reach a consensus and maybe even settle differences. why, then, this tendency to restrict the right to small groups, to give small member states less power and to eliminate small languages? (the president declared the common position approved as amended)
- mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr nisticò, for his cooperation throughout the preparation of this report. i would add to what mr lund has just said that what i find unacceptable is that a compromise between two groups is presented as a compromise of parliament, without the participation of the fictitious rapporteurs, at least of the other smaller groups. that being said, the liberal group, which i represent here, considers that the common position adopted by the council is an excellent compromise. it takes due account of the concerns expressed by blood donors, particularly with regard to the ethical requirements of donation, and by patients, who are calling for the necessary availability of plasma products throughout europe, which my colleague mr bowis has discussed at length. in other words, any amendment which seeks to upset this balance seems dangerous to us, and i would say in particular that we would be wrong to fight again for the total and compulsory gratuity of donations, because to impose voluntary donations as the only reference means to favour one national collection system over another, and this is in total contradiction with the treaties. why should we be forced to choose between the mixed system in germany, for example, the monopoly of the red cross in belgium, or the french system which, since the tragic case of contaminated blood, has been able to set up an efficient haemovigilance network, which is now being used by many member states? the main thing is elsewhere. the main thing is to promote, i repeat, the highest standards of quality and safety in blood donations and blood components, and it is not by making ourselves the champions of compulsory and absolute gratuity, i repeat, and by proposing the assimilation - which has so far been scientifically unfounded between ethics and quality, as is stated in amendments nos 7 and 10 adopted in committee and amendment no 26 - that we will be usefully preparing the future of the blood supply in europe and will be providing a concrete and lasting answer to the problem of self-sufficiency. the liberal group will therefore vote against these amendments. yes, of course, we have to listen to the voluntary donors. of course, their contribution is invaluable. but you also need to listen to patients when they tell you that no haemophiliac or primary immunodeficient person will inject untreated or uncertain plasma products, on the grounds that they come from voluntary donors. it is a message of generosity and solidarity that we must send here and now to the givers and the receivers. we feel it is futile to oppose their views. the main danger to the blood supply and the lives of patients is the shortage of medicines derived from plasma. it is by accepting this reality and by accepting, without ostracism, all healthy donors that the battle for self-sufficiency will one day be won in europe.
- the ten elected representatives of the free european alliance, that is to say the regionalist and nationalist elected representatives within the green group, have difficulty with paragraph c. it does say that regional constituencies can be set up, but - and i quote - 'without prejudice to the proportional representation of political groups at member state level'. i would point out that in the belgian system the member state level is totally irrelevant for the european elections. dutch and french speakers are going to the polls in two separate constituencies that have no influence on each other. i think recital c is an extensive interpretation of article 2 of the draft council decision. moreover, the various language versions - read the french, dutch and english versions - are not identical. the free european alliance also has difficulty with the reference to transnational lists in recital d. we believe that this practice will contribute to an even greater reduction in the political diversity of this hemisphere. it makes it even more difficult for smaller political formations to win seats, whether at transnational, national or regional level, especially when combined with the 5% electoral threshold. that is why the 10 members of the free european alliance will not agree with the present recommendation by mr gil robles.
- madam president, the bali meeting was a failure. i think that is a general observation. we must learn from this failure immediately. we have very little time left before johannesburg. the risk is that a concept which we do not want, namely the privatisation of sustainable development, will be imposed in johannesburg. the commissioner has just mentioned voluntary partnership agreements with private companies. i think that ideology should be avoided, if it can be considered as a complementary tool to policies which can be useful, provided that these partnership agreements are politically framed and that everyone knows exactly in what context they operate. however, this should not be a substitute for voluntary government policies. but this is clearly what a number of actors want, and i am thinking in particular of the united states. i believe that the european union, as the commissioner has said, must be ambitious for johannesburg. however, to achieve our ambitions, we need allies. perhaps we should give priority to dialogue with those countries which expect a lot from us, that is to say, the poor countries; i think, for example, that the group of 77 is an interlocutor that we must absolutely give priority to. in this respect, it seems to me that two proposals would be quite positive political signals to these countries. the first would be to propose, and this was mentioned by the president-in-office of the council, that we change our european policies which prevent developing countries and the poorest countries from progressing towards sustainable development or from meeting their basic needs. i am thinking in particular of aid for agricultural exports. it is unacceptable that we continue to maintain a system of export aid which is a penalty to these countries. so we have to make a big move on that. the second proposal would be to promote a binding legal framework for transnational companies, which would encourage them to assume their environmental, social and economic responsibilities. it is unacceptable that when states are constrained by international rules, transnational companies, which sometimes have much more resources than states, can act with impunity and freedom.
- mr president, the preparatory meeting in bali certainly does not bode well for the un summit on sustainable development this summer in johannesburg. i think bali was the last chance to define a number of priorities and to establish a concrete and binding action plan. but classic contradictions such as the north-south divide and transatlantic tensions have thwarted that. it had all started well, though. ten years ago, in rio, for the first time, environment and development were linked. the need for sustainable development was also recognised by all countries. but since then, little has happened with the results of rio. the kyoto protocol has been delayed indefinitely and it is still up to russia whether it will actually come into force. the us, the biggest polluter, rejected the kyoto protocol because it would harm the us economy, and now canada and australia have followed suit. the same attitude washington took in bali. the bush administration apparently does not want binding agreements, but voluntary and non-binding commitments from the private sector. but global warming and the future of our planet are too serious problems to be left to the goodwill of industry. what right does the united states have to make demands on the developing countries when it is the usa that is ignoring all international agreements? it is clear that the transatlantic divide is widening. kyoto has shown that the bush administration is putting america's short-term interests ahead of the well-being of future generations for short-term political considerations. the european union, on the other hand, has accepted its responsibility in kyoto and has thus also confirmed its moral leadership. i hope that she will do so with the same consistency and the same vigour in johannesburg.
commission. – mr president, i am sorry if i misunderstood the procedure. i understood i should make a statement in relation to the consumer protection issue, answer questions on that and then move on to this issue. however, i am of course happy to do it this way. i am pleased to bring parliament up to date on the nitrofen contamination in germany. this is a very disturbing discovery. nitrofen is a herbicide and has been a banned substance in the european union since 1988. it is a probable carcinogen, with teratogenic effects. consumers are quite rightly worried that control measures failed to prevent this contamination. the commission was first made aware of the contamination on 24 may. there were three immediate priorities. first, to establish from the german authorities the source and extent of the contamination; second, to trace and recall contaminated products; third, to ensure that the control authorities in the member states were kept fully informed of developments. i am satisfied that very good progress has been made in addressing each of these priorities. the origin of the contamination is now known. it can be traced to the storage of organic grains in a warehouse that had been used in the past to store pesticides. i am sure you will share my concern that such an elementary precaution as a thorough cleaning does not appear to have taken place before this very radical change of use was undertaken. in addition, the contamination was discovered in industry tests in january but was not reported to the responsible authorities until may. this delay in notification has resulted in the circulation and consumption of contaminated material before the german authorities were in a position to react. feedingstuffs from this warehouse were delivered to 90 producers of organic products through a firm that supplies to the organic sector. the german authorities have taken the necessary measures to block supplies from these producers unless and until they have been checked for the absence of nitrofen. this tracing exercise included some limited exports of poultrymeat to the netherlands, denmark, belgium and austria. the authorities are also in the process of tracing smaller quantities of organic cereals supplied directly from the warehouse to an estimated 20 other producers. products from these companies are currently blocked. the german authorities state that full tracing will be finalised by the end of this week. this tracing exercise includes a quantity of organic wheat exported to france in january. there is also evidence of storage of conventional non-organic cereals in this warehouse between 1995 and august 2001, although not necessarily in the contaminated section. the authorities are still in the process of verifying this information. finally, a quantity of organic lupines was also exported to denmark. the danish authorities have also been notified. they have traced this product and blocked what remains on the market and the farms concerned. the german authorities are satisfied that the origin of the contamination is confined to that particular warehouse. they point out that both organic and conventional produce have been extensively tested and no nitrofen has been found. the commission notes the assurances that any remaining contamination is limited and can be contained. we also note the considerable efforts to trace products and remove them from the market. member states have been kept fully informed of this entire process. a further meeting of the standing committee on the food chain today took note of the assurances from the german authorities and agreed that there is no need to take additional measures for the moment. the overall situation obviously remains under close watch. the standing committee will meet again on friday to review developments. in particular it will want to learn of the progress in tracing the estimated 6% of contaminated produce which remains to be fully traced. while there is considerable concern in all member states, especially with the still fresh memories of recent food safety crises, only belgium has announced national restrictions. this may be a legacy of the dioxin crisis which has understandably left deep scars in belgium. i expect, nonetheless, that the clarifications provided in the standing committee this week will prove sufficient to lift these restrictions. we must be careful also to focus on the broader picture. these recent events confirmed for me that our new approach to food safety is going in the right direction. in particular, the necessity for food traceability from farm to fork and for rapid and transparent exchange of information has been confirmed. both are critical to the identification and swift recall of contaminated products. as always, lessons need to be learned and the commission is looking at the following issues in particular. how did such contamination occur in the first place when nitrofen has been banned in the eu since 1988? can we be fully confident that warehouses formerly used to store dangerous substances are safe for food storage? could more be done to ensure that federal authorities are notified immediately of incidents of contamination, allowing them to notify the commission and other member states, not just in germany but in other federal countries as well? in particular, what should be done to address the fact that the contamination was known to the laboratories and industry for months before the authorities were informed? similarly, was the rapid alert system notified of these developments as intended? these are issues i will now be taking up with the federal authorities in germany. a mission of the food and veterinary office will take place shortly to assist in this process. previous fvo reports on both residue control measures and controls in the organic food sector in germany will also be taken fully into account. i can assure you that parliament will be kept fully informed of all these developments.
- the problems of contamination of turkey meat in germany are very reminiscent - the commissioner has also said this - of those of the dioxin contamination of chickens in belgium in 1999. however, the commission then criticised the information provided by belgium so strongly that a panic reaction in many countries inside and outside the eu led to the closure of borders for virtually all belgian agricultural products, with severe economic consequences. the commission immediately imposed an export ban on the potentially contaminated products, with a very strict surveillance system attached. the commission's response to what has happened in germany is very different. germany has notified too late, much too late, despite the fact that they themselves already knew, which was not the case in belgium. we were a little late, but not that late. the commission is informed, but unlike in the case in belgium, no complaints are lodged against germany. there's no acting and so forth. so you should not be alarmed that in belgium there is a feeling that once again the commission's approach is completely different because it is a large member state. i would therefore like to know how this is happening, commissioner, and i would like to hear your reaction to your approach.
- commissioner, i am not satisfied with your explanation to mrs smet. the crisis has been going on since september. the german minister for agriculture has been informed since 21 may. the commission was informed on 24 may. we're almost three weeks in. we have worked together on the regulation on the european food safety authority. this is the first test for this regulation. and what does article 53 of that regulation say? that when a serious risk to human health, animal health or the environment is identified, the commission must or can take a number of measures without delay. for example, the suspension of the placing of the products on the market. you don't do this, you reassure us now, when intelligence tells me there's 100 tons of wheat lying somewhere, i don't know where, that's been contaminated. you say there's only one source. you have information that there is only one source, while the contamination has been going on since september, while we all know that the pilot plant in vorpommern has been in operation since october. so you're not convincing me. i really have the impression that a large member state is being favoured over a small one like mine.
commission. – the eu scientific committee on veterinary measures relating to public health was asked to review its previous opinions of april 1999 and may 2000 in the light of any new information, including the results of the studies launched by the commission. in its opinion of 10 april 2002 the committee confirmed the risks posed by growth-promoting hormones to consumers that it had identified in its earlier opinions. in particular the committee concluded in its 1999 opinion that for 17ß-oestradiol there was a substantial body of evidence that it has to be considered as a complete carcinogen and that it exerts both tumour-initiating and tumour-promoting effects. no safe threshold could be established for any of the six hormones evaluated, and prepubertal children are the group of greatest concern because of their extremely low levels of endogenous production of hormones. it was also concluded in 1999 that the adverse effects included developmental, neurobiological, genotoxic and carcinogenic effects. in saying that "no amendments to those opinions are justified", the scientists confirmed the validity of the previous opinions. new evidence was reported on the disposition of esters of 17ß-oestradiol and their accumulation in edible animal body fats. this evidence needs to be considered in any exposure assessment as well as the consequences of the newly recognised, more complex metabolisms for synthetic hormones such as trenbolone, zeronol and melengestrol acetates. the possible adverse effects on human health of these residual metabolites require further assessment. finally, recent evidence was noted regarding possible adverse environmental effects. the commission considers that its approach to consumer protection in this area is fully justified by scientific advice. the commission shares the honourable member's views concerning the importance of transparency and credibility, which is why the committee's opinions are immediately published on the website of the commission's directorate-general for health and consumer protection, together with other relevant information on this issue.
commission. – i fully agree with the honourable member's assessment of the situation in the sense that i believe that it is absolutely essential that the credibility of the commission and of parliament and of the institutions of the european union be maintained in all respects, but particularly in this respect. i fully agree with that. however i have to say, at the risk of repeating myself, that the relevant scientific committee has looked at this now on three separate occasions. they have furnished their opinions on those occasions, most recently within the last couple of months when they took into account the earlier opinions but also all the relevant up-to-date scientific opinion on this particular issue. they have come to the conclusion that i have presented to you. i have to say that when i am engaged in the exercise of risk management, i have to follow the advice of the risk assessors, i have to follow the advice of scientific evidence and scientific opinion. if i fail to do that, or if any of us fails to do that, in my view we are completely lost. we are then at sea without a compass. that is why it is important for us to follow closely the advice that we are given. and i have to reiterate this is the advice that we have been given and that is why we have followed it closely on this issue. may i make one further point, while i am on my feet: we are at the moment bringing through legislation in relation to this issue which is in response to the opinion of the wto some years ago where a particular infirmity was found with our position by the wto. that related to only one issue, and that was essentially a procedural issue. that issue has now been addressed in the amending legislation that is before this house and no doubt it will be discussed by you, mr mccartin, and indeed other members when this piece of legislation comes before the house on second reading.

- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the structuring of a coherent and supportive european research area is a contribution to the building of european citizenship and is the subject of the structured dialogue between science and society which this programme proposes. a dream that i would only wish to attach to this specific program? i guess so. but beyond the dream, in a concrete and precise way, we, the european parliamentarians, are thus affirming the existence of europe in all the strength of the creative intelligence it has in reserve, whether through its companies, its universities or its researchers, and above all through their mobility. it would be unthinkable for us to continue the race against the clock that researchers must face every day in order to be able to concentrate in peace on the very essence of their work, in the face of regulations, the vagaries of social security, the administrative delays of obtaining visas and the problems of career. hence the clear and unequivocal statement in these programmes that one of the sine qua non conditions for this achievement is mobility. mobility that transcends natural, cultural and economic boundaries, that promotes gender equality, that allows the exchange of research practices. a mobility support that the relevant dg may have to launch soon: today a simple green number, tomorrow a crossroads to a europe on the move. to this we must add, in advance, the need to develop an interdisciplinary doctoral training which opens up a new conception of the mental and technoscientific space of research, a conception close to the expectations and needs of the citizens. it is a strategic challenge for universities to open up this perspective, both for teaching staff and academics. is it a dream to structure the new skills and attitudes needed to take forward-looking research approaches that take account of the true european dimension, to build up an inventory of these potentials, these reservoirs of grey matter, to network them, to create the prolific alchemy of the european research area? no, i'm not going. because structuring the architecture of the european research area means drawing on the strength of researchers, their potential, the academic and university resources, on the mobility that opens up the brewing of knowledge and know-how, and thus creating the catalytic and prolific shock that will allow europe to exist, to reach the substantial core of the european research area of this millennium, with the citizens of europe, for the citizens of the world. thank you to the designers of this vision.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am speaking here in response to the problem of asylum seekers from the ceecs. it seems to me that it clearly raises the question of the european union's ability to determine our rights and status in the field of asylum, as citizens or future citizens of europe, on the eve of the great challenge: 'what enlargement?' the addition to the amsterdam treaty of the protocol on the right of asylum excludes the possibility for a member state of the european union to take into consideration an application for asylum made by a national of another member state. in doing so, the treaty introduces discrimination against european citizens on grounds of national origin. this is contrary to article 3 of the geneva convention, which defines a refugee as "any person who has a well-founded fear of being persecuted on account of his race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion". the reasoning behind the inclusion of this protocol is that member states respect the rule of law, democracy and human rights and have internal and external legal means to put an end to violations of these principles of law and that nationals of member states already enjoy rights in other member states which render the right of asylum, the right to free movement, the right of free establishment, the right of residence and other laws relating to citizenship unnecessary. what about the recent condemnations of various member states by the european court of human rights, or the reports by amnesty international which every year denounce violations of fundamental rights in the majority of member states? moreover, how can we predict the future and say with absolute certainty that the member states or future members of the european union are safe from any drift? the attitude towards minorities is a good example. let us ensure that everyone can claim the right to asylum within the european union. let us make sure that everyone can have his asylum application examined. would that not be the best way of verifying our democratic commitments?

in writing. - (nl) the high number of road deaths has finally made road safety a political problem. public opinion is rightly alarmed at the many victims among vulnerable road users who are killed by deadly traffic. the petersen report contributes to this problem. cars must not only be safer for the driver. they must also be for those who, as vulnerable users, also participate in traffic. i strongly agree with the wish expressed in the petersen report that the european parliament should be involved in legislation on this matter. road safety cannot be the business of the executive branch and the car manufacturers alone! as long as the superior speed of cars is a selling point, i will continue to defy the industry. the number of road accident victims cannot be reduced substantially unless speed limits are introduced on the roads, drivers adapt their behaviour and infrastructure contributes to prevention and safety. road accidents are not natural disasters. we have to warn them.
