- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. since 1998, the war in the congo has been going on. there are 3.5 million victims. two-thirds of the territory is still occupied. recently, international opinion was again shocked by the news of a mass slaughter in kisangani in which 200 people were killed. that massacre came two years after 1,200 people had been killed in that same city as a result of a quarrel between two rival military factions. the civilian population is obviously the victim, but the perpetrators were never punished. amnesty international therefore draws a connection between the events of then and now. the war in the congo has not yet received enough attention from the international community. the european union has made greater efforts during the belgian presidency than in the past to initiate the dialogue. but i'm still wondering. why is monuc not being reinforced? monuc is even humiliated, officials are expelled by drc goma. why has kisangani not been demilitarised despite the security council resolutions? what are we doing to make the peace process succeed, since the important parties are still not at the table? what are we doing to open up the congo river? the population in the interior is still deprived of any food or medicine. no one can reach these people because the congo river is probably the only way you can do that. preparing for the reconstruction of the congo is wishful thinking in these circumstances. it's not happening. on the contrary, the demilitarisation is not going ahead. only namibia has done anything. i understand that rwanda wants to be safe, but why does international opinion not protect rwanda and force everyone to leave the country because they are only there to plunder the country's resources. they're not fighting each other, but they're taking what little civilians have left.
mr president, i shall read in his place the speech which my colleague mr vermeer wished to make. mr vermeer is hampered by the freight traffic which is not allowed to drive on sunday but then on monday causes so much traffic that he doesn't get here in time. the usefulness of this proposal to harmonize driving bans on the transeuropean network is not questioned by my group. the way you do. a general extension of the weekend driving ban is a very sensitive issue for our group. a driving ban, however useful, is a serious breach of the principle of the free movement of goods and affects the peripheral countries the most. the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council and the commission on the subject of the internal market. the introduction of a driving ban simply increases the flow of traffic on a limited number of days and thus also creates a very different traffic congestion, also in the so-called transit countries. the irony of this artificial course of action is that on the ten network, which is often financed with eu money, member states have the right to introduce driving restrictions without these being adequately examined for their usefulness, for example by the commission. i have therefore tabled a number of amendments on behalf of the eldr group which should make this possible. the aim of my amendment is to allow member states to introduce driving bans if this is justified on the basis of various criteria such as the environment, safety or social grounds. this should prevent a proliferation of driving bans. i therefore ask for your support for amendment no 23. as regards amendments nos 4 and 13 by the rapporteur, which propose topographical reasons to justify driving bans, my group does not agree with them. what exactly is a topographic reason and how is it defined? the reasoning for that amendment refers to the alps. but the dutch polder landscape, for example, can also be a topographical motive for a driving ban. i think that this concept is very susceptible to abuse. if these amendments, nos 4 and 13, are adopted tomorrow in the vote on this report, i, as shadow rapporteur, will recommend that my group vote against the report as a whole. let us keep the internal market open and not create too many restrictions or opportunities for restrictions.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is with a certain emotion that i address you today, although i had hoped for a more packed auditorium. it is indeed the last time i shall be addressing this parliament because, as from 1 september, i will be heading the foundation which is responsible for allocating funds to improve flemish cinema. and that's the whole point of the work i've done here for the last three years. that is what the european commission is trying to do, and that is what i will try to do in flanders. the commission communication on which my report is based has obviously been a very positive step. it's a step in the right direction. the great merit of the communication - and my report points to this - is that things are finally becoming more transparent, especially in the legal field, and that an attempt has been made to clarify the unclear situation as to what could or could not be done in the area of state aid to the audiovisual sector. we can only applaud that. but if we look at the communication from another angle, we must regret that the european commission seems to be suffering from a lack of ambition when it comes to audiovisual policy. the commission has also adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. as if there were any competition in the audiovisual sector in europe! it is extremely strange that europe should devote so much attention to looking at the situation in the various member states when those member states are faced with a crushing competitor who is not a member of the european union, fortunately. i am talking about the united states, whose film production accounts for 80 to 85% of cinema attendance in most european countries. that is, of course, a staggering figure. in the face of such a disastrous situation, it is to be hoped that the european commission will finally succeed in taking a real initiative which is on the level of what the european union should be. there is no written law that says europe must lag behind the american film industry. but it is clear - and the european parliament has pointed out in previous reports - that member states face different handicaps. it is therefore time for the european commission to show that it is not only aware of this, as it obviously is, but that it is also willing to commit resources. it is still vindictive, ladies and gentlemen, and the example has been given so often that it is a bit old-fashioned, but it is still so illustrative that europe spends 10 times more money on tobacco subsidies than on the media programme. in europe, it is better to be an olive grower than a filmmaker. europe has a very clear and explicit policy on this. but when it comes to one of the most ambitious sectors with the best prospects, including employment, europe still says no. no, i am exaggerating, europe is present but on a very, very modest level. i would therefore like to call on the european parliament, and this is my last appeal to the european parliament, and on the european commission and the member states to keep the fire burning so that perhaps one day we can speak of a genuine european policy which will be able to give the european citizen what the european citizen has a right to, namely a high-quality audiovisual industry of its own.
- mr president, i am sorry to say that the seville summit was a great missed opportunity, and i am even more sorry to say that it is again partly the fault of the belgian prime minister, mr verhofstadt, who has sabotaged the opportunity to finally do something about this huge problem of asylum and immigration, even though in all the european member states - and this is shown, for example, by the election results of recent months - there is a huge majority of the people who want this problem to be tackled. i am also sorry to say that the european union is already blindly marching towards enlargement with the european countries of the former eastern bloc. although i personally, in principle and in substance, support this, i say that it cannot be done without first taking all the necessary measures to ensure that enlargement does not lead to a new mass migration of people towards the west. and in the meantime, the irish people may go to the polls again. but we already know: if they accidentally do not vote "yes", their "no" vote is simply not counted. it is surprising that this european union is increasingly regarded as undemocratic by more and more citizens and that it is being rejected by a large number of people, which is regrettable.
- mr. president, it reminds me a little of a greek drama: whatever you choose, it always ends badly. and i think we're facing a similar drama here. the objective is clear, we agree. the consumer must have a choice: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the labelling of products is not distorted. and we have to judge that based on paper evidence, for one thing. we need to build a system that we can trust, and that's not necessarily just scientific testing. our group does not want labels on the processes by which food is produced, i.e. processing aids. we are still debating products from animals that have been fed gmos. but if we add those two, we're gonna have to label pretty much everything, and then my question is: are we helping the consumer? does it make it clearer through the whole forest? we therefore call for the establishment of a system of non-gmo, which is already being used by some of the distribution chains as an argument. the big problem with paper evidence is gonna be fraud. i think that if we do not set up a good and reliable system - since we are dealing mainly with countries outside the union - we can expect the first scandal. as regards the threshold: i am not going to go into the details of the vote. the figures from eurostat clearly show that a majority of consumers want good labelling. but should we follow the commission's system? does this provide the necessary guarantees to check the paper evidence in a total trade in a number of products? are we sure we have a really good label? secondly, if we label as much as possible, including enzymes, including animal products, are we not going to label almost all products and will the consumer still benefit from this? i think the best combination is to combine a non-gmo label with a gmo label for products wherever you can find them. that system is not watertight, it's not perfect, but i think in my version of greek drama, that's where the least casualties will be.
- i shall concentrate on two important aspects of the commission's proposals which, on the whole, are moving in the right direction. however, there are still a number of gaps which i believe have been filled by the committee on the environment, and i hope that the plenary will follow the proposals of the committee on the environment. the two points in question: first, the introduction of a 1% tolerance threshold for unauthorized gmos, provided that their presence is accidental or technically unavoidable. it is quite inconsistent: if it is an unauthorized gmo, it should not be in our food. this is a proposal which is an incitement to fraud and a flagrant breach of the spirit and letter of directive 2001/18/ec, which was adopted last year. it is therefore unacceptable. the second important point is the problem of the coexistence of gm and non-gm crops. the problem is not being tackled by setting pollution thresholds. it is important to raise the fundamental question of the legal liability of operators. in short, farmers who are growing or will be growing gmos must take all necessary measures to avoid contamination of conventional and organic products. it is the polluter-pays principle that must be applied and that is the case with an approach like this. i hope that tomorrow's vote will be in that direction.
- on behalf of the liberal group, i would also like to express our satisfaction. the result achieved in conciliation would not have been possible, i would like to stress, without the perseverance of professor trakatellis and the constructive cooperation between the delegation of our parliament, the spanish presidency and the services of commissioner byrne. despite tough negotiations, mainly on the budgetary side, the three institutions wanted above all to send a common message to the european citizens. health is now at the top of our priorities and this is welcome, because it was essential that europe, so to speak, 'take care of its health' and finally have a comprehensive and integrated strategy in this area. food insecurity, the increase in alcohol and tobacco consumption, especially among young people, require more than ever this kind of coordinated action at community level in disease prevention. the increase in the number of epidemics such as aids and tuberculosis, and the emergence of bioterrorism, as mentioned, required the union to have this capacity for rapid reaction. these are all reasons and concerns which justify an appropriate budget for the next six years: the verdict has been delivered, so eur 312 million will be paid, in addition to a commission statement providing for the review of the financial framework in the light of enlargement, as well as new financial perspectives. it is less than the 380 million we were hoping for, but it is still much more than the 280 million originally granted by the council. this compromise is therefore, one might say, a victory for our parliament, and political will has prevailed over budgetary dogmatism.
- i would like to thank our rapporteur, mr lisi, for the quality of his work on a very technical subject, but also one which is particularly sensitive to our opinions. originally, seveso 3 was supposed to be just an improved seveso 2. following the baia mare and enschede disasters, the main purpose of the revision of the directive was to extend its scope to mining activities and to lower the thresholds for pyrotechnic substances. on 21 september, the situation changed with the catastrophe at the azf factory in toulouse, a very cruel reminder of reality. to be effective, a law must first be applied perfectly. this was clearly not the case in the pink city: no hazard assessment had been carried out in the building where the explosion occurred; the commission has also been working on the subject of the protection of the environment, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. article 9 is the backbone of this directive. it is therefore necessary to act in advance, to ensure that the safety report is drawn up in the most transparent way and is updated as soon as a major change occurs on the industrial site. the liberal group welcomes the adoption in committee of amendments nos 17 to 19. on the safety report now, and more generally on another crucial point in this review, the control of urbanisation around industrial sites, i note that the member states have very different, if not in some cases opposite, methods of calculating the perimeter of danger, and that is why we welcome the vote in committee on amendment no 20 aimed at the eventual establishment of a single european method of calculation. as a representative of the commission said to the french national parliamentarians after the toulouse disaster, a process which, i quote, 'tries to harmonize approaches without harmonizing basic philosophies will never succeed'. for this reason, and for the sake of consistency, the liberal group is also in favour of amendment no 55 on this point. it is also essential for safety reasons, as provided for in amendment no 39, to lower the thresholds for the fourth category of ammonium nitrate, i.e. those composed of waste and which exploded in the hangar of the zdf plant. in conclusion, madam president, the competent national authorities must enforce the obligations which already apply to the site, obligations which will be reinforced here with the network 3. we must constantly combat this trivialization of risks in certain industrial sectors, and this is our priority in memory of the 52 victims of toulouse and enschede and all the others.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i know that this is a sensitive subject for some of my colleagues and i appreciate their efforts. i would like to use my speaking time to clear up two misunderstandings in the world that have been fueled by an aggressive email campaign that we've all been victims of in the last few days. firstly, this report does not advocate the harmonization of legislation at european level. it imposes no obligations on the member states or the candidate countries. we remain convinced that legislation and action should remain the responsibility of member states and the report therefore takes full account of subsidiarity. what it does is launch a call to initiate a learning process at european level, to collect data on the situation in the member states, to examine how member states' policies on sexual and reproductive health and rights are interlinked and which measures are delivering good results. colleagues, i have reasons to hold this plea. firstly, there are many areas where the european union has set up a similar practice of best practice, of mutual learning, of exchange of information. we have not yet had a debate in this chamber on the trakatellis report on a european health strategy, which defends and encourages such an approach in the context of an action programme. now, sexual and reproductive health should be part of a health promotion campaign and so i don't know why that doesn't apply here. secondly, when we talk about development cooperation policy, this parliament and the whole union rightly advocate that sexual and reproductive rights should be part of such development cooperation policy. it would be particularly strange if we were to advocate this for the developing countries but the european voices were silenced when it came to our own policies. besides, there are reasons for concern. there are huge differences in the situation of sexual and reproductive health in the member states. why is it that in some of the candidate countries the rate of abortion is up to 10 times higher than in the best performing member states? it is understandable because in some of the candidate countries the price of contraceptives is up to one third of women's wages, while abortions are free. this is not the kind of policy we want to promote! another example: how is it that some countries are seeing an incredible increase in unwanted teenage pregnancies while others are managing to address this problem? so we have to ask ourselves, are the teens that different or is the policy that different? so, colleagues, please let us learn from each other without harmonizing. second bias. this is not an abortion report. on the contrary, the report seeks ways of enabling women to choose whether, when and how many children they want and to live their sexuality in a healthy way. that is to say, the report seeks to prevent unwanted pregnancies and thus also abortions. paragraph 8 says very clearly that abortion should not be encouraged as a method of family planning. but we have to develop a three-track policy. a first clue is open sex education and information. too many young people still think that you can't get pregnant from the first time you have sex or get your sex education through porn. this information should be about relationship building and responsible interaction. if sex education is reduced to biology, it's not going to work. the second track is that every woman should be able to make a free choice regarding her sexual and reproductive health. this means that she should have access to a wide range of contraceptives and good services and information on the advantages and disadvantages. there is no such choice when women have to pay up to a third of their wages. it also means that we have to make an extra effort for those groups that are not being reached now. all the campaigns that say this is eugenics talk are lying, after all. i am convinced that emergency contraception should also be part of this concept. so that means the morning-after pill. and i hope that the ppe will continue to support the amendments tabled by both groups. the third track is the most controversial. i also believe that abortion should be the last resort in any policy on sexual and reproductive rights for women who have failed to use contraception. in fact, abortion is legal in most countries. and please, let us not turn a blind eye to abortion tourism, to the forced displacement of some women who have to console themselves because they have indeed failed in their contraception. this brings with it a serious social injustice because only those fortunate enough can afford to take such a journey. the report also calls on the member states not to prosecute women who have had an illegal abortion. and we are only repeating what the beijing+5 platform has already said. finally, mr president, in the run-up to this debate we have had a great deal of discussion. just before this meeting there were also attempts to reach maximum consensus. i hope that these efforts will be rewarded tomorrow and that the commission will support us in launching an initiative. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i should like to express our deep respect for the rapporteur and for the way in which she has produced this well-founded report. mrs van lancker has not only taken all the groups into account but also the different sensitivities within the groups. the sensitivity of this report is obvious to all. it is a matter which urgently requires a clear policy. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of women in the field of sexual and reproductive health are respected. this means that the right to decide what happens to your own body also differs in the member states. all member states must take their own responsibility in this respect. in some lands today, abortion is not even possible in a legal and safe way. consequently, we are seeing more and more "abortion shopping". i am not going to go into the details of the matter. but women will go where they can go. last year, for example, as many as 6,600 women from ireland went to britain for an abortion. the numbers are increasing every year. my colleague here can testify to that. she's from ireland. such figures show that it is a fact that affects almost all families sooner or later. in particular in those countries where sexual education and access to contraceptives remain low. i understand those who oppose the legalization of abortion, but ask yourself: do our policymakers have the right to deny women an abortion? every policy maker must be fully aware of his role as an educator, and for this very reason we must prevent pregnancy from becoming an insurable or uninsurable risk in the future. in no case should it be possible to abort a pregnancy against the woman's will, by any pressure whatsoever. finally, the crux of the matter is therefore the protection of pregnant women on the one hand and the acceptance on the other hand that it is always the woman herself who must ultimately decide what happens to her body. the member states therefore have a huge task to prevent abortions that are not done for specific reasons related to women, such as poverty, career or stigmatisation. we shall vote in favour tomorrow.
- thank you, mr president, given the late hour and the limited speaking time, i must confine myself to one single reason out of the long list of reasons for rejecting this report, and so i will pass over for convenience the fact that parliament has no jurisdiction whatsoever with regard to the basis of this report and that what is being said should be regarded as a kind of ideological occupational therapy, as yet another breach of the principle of subsidiarity and as an attempt to impose the practice of abortion on the applicant countries as a kind of community acquis, and that is unacceptable in any case. a fundamental reason then. i do not understand how the termination of life by abortion can be regarded as a victory in the field of human rights. i am happy to debate with people who ask for understanding in emergencies, but the barely disguised abortion propaganda in this report is off-putting. i think killing life is the opposite of reproductive health, by the way. the truth is that many abortions, the majority of them, are the result of parents not having the material means to take care of children, for example because marriage is still punished by taxation, because child benefits are too low or because our society does not value families. however, there is a huge demographic problem in europe which obviously cannot be tackled by abortion but by a well-thought-out positive family policy, a well-thought-out child-friendly policy.
- but what is the debate we have among ourselves on this report? you might think this is an administrative debate. there is no question of that: this is a debate on human rights, and the reproductive rights we are discussing tonight represent a further step. if the first generation of fundamental rights dealt with civil and political rights, the second focused on social rights and, finally, here we are, we are talking about reproductive rights, we are talking about the rights of women's bodies. we are now in the process of defining these rights, and in this sense the who's conception of health as a fundamental right supports efforts to develop a framework that establishes reproductive health in law. but let us be aware that reproductive choices in europe and the world are not free. for men and women to have access to this freedom, they must therefore have access to information about the different options available and their respective consequences, hence the need for sexual and emotional education which plays a leading role. but this education is conditioned by the different political, cultural and family contexts, and so the challenge is important: to build a space for a possible debate, respectful of individuals, of their culture, a debate where respect for each individual must restrict us to an education that addresses all issues and does not censor information in any way. today, in many countries, public policies aim to empower women through a series of measures, including education, which must touch all areas of life to be a tool of democracy. to exclude certain aspects is to enter into censorship, obscurantism and disregard for the ability of individuals to choose and examine freely. to bring the laws closer to the reality of life is to organize the emancipatory evolution of a society. this is another sign, another project that europe can highlight and of which we can be proud.

- mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the impact and development of the use of digital networks and the internet, which we can only welcome, have given a new field of expression to racist and xenophobic messages. this profusion is facilitated by a feeling of impunity linked to the allegedly virtual nature of the offence committed on the web. it is true that the internet provides a public space for all to express themselves. but some seemed to forget, as they sat at their computers, the public dimension of their words or actions and the responsibility that necessarily followed. the international dimension, the proliferation of intermediaries and the anonymity of the internet have obviously fostered and accentuated the impression that the virtual space enjoys a kind of extraterritoriality and thus escapes the law. if the space is virtual, the responsibility is very real. it is clear that if the internet is to be one of the instruments of freedom of expression, if it is to have the rights and the space, it must ensure all the obligations. and if this cross-border tool is to contribute to the emancipation of societies and their 'cyber-citizens', it can in no way play in the gaps between the lack of coordination of national policies to build, or rather destroy, the very objective that the european union has set itself, that of being an area of peace, justice and security. i thank you, commissioner, for this proposal. that is what this report is trying to say. so it is not a matter of ordering, but of encouraging.

a multitude of elected politicians and officials of international institutions, we have not succeeded in creating on the same scale and with the same efficiency the necessary regulations to enable the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, unless aviation safety is improved to the level of 1996, there will be one major accident every week in 2015. we can read that in our explanatory documents, and this underlines once again the importance of the commission's proposal for a directive. today, more than ever, aviation safety is at the forefront of the debate. in the run-up to the dramatic anniversary of the events of september 11th, aviation safety is a top priority for many of the world's airline users. this safety must not only apply to our own european aircraft but also to aircraft from third countries. the commission's proposal is therefore of particular importance, as it is now being discussed in the context of the proposals which have also been explained by my colleagues in their various reports on open skies. the commission has already reopened the procedure for the adoption of a directive on the safety of third-country aircraft. it is dramatic that the procedure for this directive was stopped, because it was almost ready in 1997, but then it was put on ice because of a dispute between spain and the united kingdom over gibraltar. fortunately, we can now count on a great deal of unanimity, although i regret that the matter is still being dealt with by member state and that the member states are still so in control of the procedures that they do not yet have the best control of the way the checks are carried out. the need for a community security policy is, however, beyond doubt to me. we must recognize that the risk of accidents in the world varies greatly between regions and continents. airlines in africa, asia, south and central america have an accident risk at least twice the global average. we in western europe and australia are fortunate to have the lowest risk. however, eastern european countries have the highest accident risk, about 50 times higher than in western europe. and to think that we are facing this massive enlargement of the european union and that the likelihood of an increase in insecurity is therefore also growing for us. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of passengers is not compromised. the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to apply the principle of subsidiarity to all air transport operations. recent accidents show that insufficient knowledge of the language of aviation, english, can have dramatic consequences. i need not remind you that a few major accidents, one in france, one in italy, were both the direct result of the fact that the air crew had too little knowledge of the language of the flight, english. other shortcomings can also have serious consequences. in 25% of cases involving aircraft from third countries, the flight manual or navigation chart is missing or the gps systems are insufficiently updated. coordinated action with exchange of information is more effective in identifying early possible aircraft deficiencies than national measures. for example, member states can rely on the eu's common position with regard to third countries to avoid unsafe aircraft being diverted to airports in neighbouring member states where such checks are not carried out. specific elements in our report concern the fact that airport residents can lodge complaints about the safety of aircraft and that they can also obtain information about the unsafe operation of some aircraft that fly into their airports. the people of ostend will be very pleased to hear if this part is approved. the european commission is, however, reserved on this point. i would stress that there must be no discrimination. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on the protection of the environment are respected. since the events of september 11th, we have had to take greater account of the safety of those living near airports and air routes. moreover, and this is my last word, we must prevent privatizations in the aviation sector, and particularly privatizations of safety, from undermining safety itself, because then there may be further cuts. i hope that the savings on safety, as demonstrated in überlingen, will not have fatal consequences. (applause from the left)
- mr president, noise is a nuisance which weighs heavily on the well-being of many european citizens. the noise in the vicinity of airports often reaches a level that makes it unbearable for millions of our fellow citizens. it is therefore absolutely essential to combine regulatory and economic tools to minimise this nuisance. the first step is to eliminate the noisiest aircraft. to date, it has only been partially implemented. we know that many aircraft which meet only the standards of chapter 3 continue to fly at european airports. it is therefore imperative to complement regulatory measures with economic tools. so using the fee tool, in application of the polluter-pays principle, is certainly a good initiative. we therefore welcome the commission's proposal to introduce a charging system within a harmonised community framework. the aim is to encourage airlines to use the least noisy aircraft and, in addition, to avoid dumping between airports. such dumping by noise is obviously very damaging to the citizens living near these airports. however, while the commission's proposal is good in principle, when it is examined in greater detail it is found to lack rigor and efficiency, as the rapporteur has already pointed out. the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a regulation on the application of the levy. in addition, the distinction between nuisances is insufficient. the proposed ratio of 1 to 20 between the lowest and highest fee is insufficient, so the rapporteur has suggested raising the ratio from 20 to 40, which is quite acceptable. we agree with the rapporteur's proposals in general, but we have added an element which the rapporteur unfortunately rejects, namely the inclusion of night noise and day/night/evening noise, which are two very important noise indicators for differentiating charges. we would like the rapporteur to reconsider his position and take this problem of nighttime noise into account, because it is obvious that nighttime noise is certainly the most harmful nuisance that we can encounter. the problem will not be solved by royalties alone, but in any case this measure can help to improve the situation.
the rapporteur. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i should like to thank all my colleagues and in particular the shadow rapporteurs with whom i worked closely to bring the report on promotion to the plenary. it has been a long road with many obstacles, going back to 1996 with the first publication of the green paper, followed by the commission's follow-up communication in 1998. on the basis of extensive consultation, the european commission came forward in october 2001 with a pragmatic and clearly defined proposal for which i was appointed rapporteur in november 2001. the aim of this draft regulation is to eliminate all the ambiguities and major differences in the relevant legislation of the 15 member states in the field of promotional measures. these many differences are particularly hampering the entry of small and medium-sized enterprises into the various european markets. it is clear that if companies cannot communicate across borders, they cannot trade internationally. in addition, the promotion has many aspects and can be adapted to different circumstances. it can be used to market innovative products, to promote customer loyalty, to stimulate short-term actions with a competitive objective, to react quickly to declining sales and as a means of efficient stock management. in addition, this regulation will also greatly improve the position of consumers. the consumer will not only receive much more information but will also be able to benefit fully from better prices through the development of the internal market. the single market means greater competitiveness, which in turn leads to lower prices, helped by the effects of the euro. i therefore consider this proposal to be a very important initiative for the further completion of the internal market. it is also a first step towards finally putting into practice the fine words of the lisbon summit. no one can deny that two years after this highly debated meeting, there has been a regrettable lack of progress. did europe not want to become the most competitive knowledge-based economy in the world? i think that the time has come to take another step in the right direction with a regulation on promotion. the commission's proposal has not been without its counterparts, who have already referred to it, because of its far-reaching nature and the many interests involved. i would like to highlight the main challenges. as regards the legal basis, i have always considered that a regulation is a much more efficient legal instrument than a directive because of its quicker entry into force and its direct applicability. the sale at a loss is temporarily postponed because of the divergence of views in the various member states. i have therefore tabled a compromise amendment asking the commission to carry out a thorough investigation of this matter. the reason for such an amendment is that i do not want the liberalization of loss-making sales to have a detrimental effect on small and mediumsized enterprises. i have therefore tabled an amendment on the latter, expressly asking for their protection. the mutual recognition, or in other words the principle of the country of origin, is essential to this proposal. this principle is also the basis of the internal market. it simply means that members must recognise that they cannot apply their own national legislation to incoming promotional actions. this mutual recognition of each other's legislation is a flexible means of avoiding over-regulation. in addition, this is already a very common form of legislation in our community legislation. i am thinking, for example, of the television without frontiers directive and the e-commerce directive. finally, the age of the children. i have always been in favour of the age of 14, unless specific products require different age limits. after much bilateral consultation with colleagues from the various committees, i have been able to resolve these important challenges in a pragmatic way. i am happy, mr president, with the result, although i realise that further examination of this draft regulation will require further adjustments. i hope that parliament has given the council a substantial lead and i look forward to this debate in the meantime.

commission. - as the honourable member knows, the principle of contractual freedom applies to undertakings providing financial services. in the insurance sector, the prior or systematic approval of rates and pricing policies by the public authorities has been abolished by the third non-life insurance directive. compulsory bonus malus schemes infringe that principle as laid down in the directives. i believe that the free trade of insurance products is in the interests of the vast majority of motorists. they can choose the insurance company that rewards good drivers with lower premiums. as regards the question raised by the honourable member in connection with the possible increase in the number of uninsured vehicles, i would nevertheless draw attention to article 3 (1) of the first motor insurance directive. this article makes insurance against civil liability compulsory for all motor vehicles in the community. the national authorities are responsible for ensuring that this obligation is respected. the recent commission proposal for a fifth motor insurance directive, to which the honourable member has referred, does not affect this provision. this new proposal, the fifth directive, does not affect the basic principle of freedom of tariffs. the main purpose of the directive is to revise the four existing directives on motor insurance. the main objectives are to update and improve the framework for compulsory insurance for the protection of victims of motor vehicle accidents, to fill in gaps and to clarify certain provisions of the directives. the commission is doing its utmost to improve the efficiency of the internal market in insurance. what it intends to do is set out in the financial services action plan. the commission is convinced that more competition will lead to lower prices and a wider choice of products for consumers.

the rapporteur. - the annual scoreboard on the implementation of the social policy agenda aims to monitor progress towards the objectives set in lisbon and nice. the essence of these objectives is the construction of a european social model that eliminates poverty and unemployment, creates high-quality jobs, encourages lifelong learning, modernises the social security system and eliminates all forms of discrimination. the period 2000-2006 is a crucial one in this respect, as economic, demographic and migration developments have rarely been as significant as they are now. it is therefore natural that parliament, which is the foremost interpreter of the feelings of the european people, should be very careful in assessing the progress made. the document presented to us by the commission gives an excellent overview of the actions undertaken and of what we can expect in the short term. but what is missing - and here i come to the main criticism - is a six-year overview of what needs to be done. parliament considers that the degree of annual progress can only be assessed in the light of what should be done in that six-year period. parliament expects more from the commission than an annual report. it calls for a six-year programme setting out the initiatives to be taken and the instruments to be used for each initiative: the commission will be able to provide the necessary information to the member states and the social partners, for example, on legislation or the open method of coordination or negotiations between the social partners and on the time limits within which these initiatives will be taken. the commission should make an effort to draw up a different type of scoreboard and present it to parliament in its mid-term evaluation in 2003. as requested by parliament last year and again this year. and then i come to my second criticism, namely the lack of involvement of parliament. the commission is well aware that its main partner in the social aspect of european development is the european parliament, much more than the council of ministers. there are many examples of this, starting with european legislation, which is usually pushed further by parliament than by the council. it is therefore a mystery to me why the commission has made so little effort to involve parliament in the social policy programme in good time. the commission document is dated 19 february 2002 and was already adopted at the spring summit in march. parliament needs at least two months to complete a report in committee and in plenary, and that is very short. i therefore advise the commission to ensure that its next report is tabled by the end of december so that a useful dialogue with parliament can be organised on this subject. the dialogue could be positive and rapid if the commission takes account of the comments made in the two previous reports of parliament. finally, a few words on one of the instruments of social policy, the open method of coordination. the success of the method lies in the fact that a way has been found to coordinate the policies of the 15 member states, to get the 15 member states behind a coherent set of objectives, to provide the 15 member states with the best practices to achieve those objectives. all this for matters where binding legislative action by the european union is not desirable or possible. the difficulty for parliament, however, is that parliament's involvement is not or is not sufficiently regulated. it is a method which involves a great many actors outside this parliament. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption by the council. we are counting on the support of the commission.
- mr president, on the eve of the commemoration of 11 september, europe must speak with one voice and defend peace more than ever. it must firmly tell the world that the fight against terrorism, which it fully supports, does not require war and that, on the contrary, a war announced as a preventive measure against iraq would sacrifice a people and set the middle east on fire, without offering any guarantee of a result. europe now has a historic opportunity to speak with one voice and to distance itself from the policy of the axis of evil which the united states is trying to impose, whether willingly or by force. in its report on iraq of 16 may, the european parliament called in particular for the un to play a leading role in the search for a peaceful, political and regional solution, and for europe to finally adopt a common position on the issue. those words were hardly heard. europe is once again divided over the strategy to be followed. baghdad is reluctant to open its doors to unmovic inspectors and the iraqi people continue to mourn the victims of an inhumane embargo that has failed to destabilize the regime of saddam hussein, while president bush is waging his crusade for good, while violating international law with impunity. what game are we playing? that is why i shall avoid the language of the wood and ask the commission and the council the questions that everyone is asking today. what guarantees can europe give iraq that unmovic inspection missions will be fairly conducted, that they will be limited in time and that they will lead to the lifting of economic sanctions? in other words, these missions will not be, as before the 1998 crisis, infiltrated by us intelligence agents mapping out future bombing. secondly, what guarantees can europe give baghdad that evidence of the dismantling of its arsenal, or verification of the absence of weapons of mass destruction, would not immediately lead to an american attack if a disarmed regime is found? because while europe is trying to prevent the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction, america, as we know, wants a regime change. is it really europe's vocation to seek to overthrow regimes? and there are others we'd like to knock over! thirdly, what would be the european reaction if an attack on iraq were to take place without the approval of the un? a war against iraq would be neither just, nor clean, nor effective. nor is it right, because without the un's green light, it would be a cover for international law. nor is it a clean war, because, as mr morillon has reminded us, there is no such thing as a clean war, let alone a war to test new weapons that would strike the iraqi people. nor effective, because in terms of fighting terrorism, and afghanistan is there to prove it, bombs are powerless: bin laden is still running and saddam hussein, probably, would continue to run. that is why when i hear mr van orden, or when i heard mr blair yesterday, i am frightened. and i will have only one message, that of the count of auteroche at the battle of fontenoy: gentlemen of england, if you want to follow our american friends, shoot first, but do not count on us to follow you!
- the president, mr commissioner, mr president-in-office, i have a number of questions at the end of this debate. the consensus on condemning the dictator saddam hussein seems unanimous. do we not forget that in the eyes of some muslims and even of large masses of them, he is seen as a hero? that's a major problem. secondly: how far does our unity go? we are convinced that the resumption of arms inspections will bring us closer to the solutions we want. do we think that they should be unconditional? do we think there should be an ultimatum? should the un issue that ultimatum? and what conclusions will we draw if nothing is discovered or if something is discovered? i think all of these scenarios should be explored in advance. is the existence of weapons of mass destruction in iraq a necessary or sufficient reason to start a war? or will it be negotiated to dismantle it? who will provide the proof of the existence of these weapons of mass destruction? some of our colleagues are already saying: that evidence is provided because he had it before. on the other hand, we have heard that some weapons were destroyed during the second gulf war. is there a consensus in the union on the real interests at stake in the middle east, on the instability of the region, on the risk to the gulf states? not that they're models of democracy, but what will happen there? what happens if there is no solution to israel and palestine first? is that solution getting any closer? is it the european union's ambition to avoid a short-term war against iraq alone? is it the ambition to overthrow saddam hussein? or is it rather our ambition to achieve peace in the middle east with the palestinian problem at the heart of the matter? should we not express our ambition to defend human rights and democracy so that partnerships can be developed in the future? how many agree with mr schröder's view? we are not only saying 'no' to war now, but we want europe to have the ambition to seek a definitive and stable peace in the middle east.
the rapporteur. mr president, i am very surprised by mrs patrie's proposal because nothing new has emerged from the extensive debate we had here yesterday morning and evening and because commissioner bolkestein has also once again emphatically confirmed that the green paper and this draft regulation are perfectly compatible. i would also point out to the house that this report was adopted by an overwhelming majority in the committee on legal affairs, with two votes against and two abstentions, and that normally there would have been no further discussion in the house if we had applied the new procedure. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is our responsibility, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, to assume our responsibilities freely. this is no longer our mission.
- mr president, commissioner, mr president-in-office, after the first war, after the defeat of the russians in afghanistan, we made a big mistake. we did not then pay much attention to the reconstruction of afghanistan and did not take care that conflict prevention was done by the reconstruction of this society. we must not make the same mistake again. afghan society is complex with its many groups, tribes, religious and ethnic differences. the various cultures must be integrated and afghanistan, a country which has perhaps always been heavily militarised and certainly still is, must learn anew that the rule of law cannot be created from the barrel of a gun. fundamentalism has certainly not disappeared from afghan society, but we must make the return of refugees possible by planning our programmes well and guaranteeing the maximum participation of the population, including women. we must make that a condition, because if they get a penny and can then banish the women back to the domestic hearth in their burqas, then something is very wrong. the economy must be rebuilt, the countryside must be rehabilitated by demining. all of this will require a lot of money and, above all, a lot of patience. since gangsterism is also widespread, we should not be surprised to see armed clashes between the groups from time to time. that is why i insist on the need to take care of human rights. we must ensure that nothing goes unpunished, that there is an investigation into any crimes against humanity. we can perhaps help the united nations to do this, so that their moral prestige is also supported with money.
- our relations with asia have improved greatly in recent years, which is a good thing, since asia accounts for 56% of the world's population, 26% of total world gdp and 25% of world trade. despite the crisis in asia, trade and investment relations have expanded considerably, with asia now the european union's third largest regional trading partner. we can even say that in europe we have contributed to the relatively rapid economic recovery in asia by keeping our markets open and by our investments. still, there is much work to be done. our political ties must be strengthened and widened. we are still not seeing asia as a significant partner in multilateral discussions in international fora such as the world trade organization. i would like to refer in this connection to the recent acceptance of the kyoto protocol by china. that's a good point. indeed, asia is slowly coming to realize that it faces the same global challenges as we do: promoting peace, security, minimum social standards, environmental protection and combating cross-border crime and trafficking in human beings. finally, i know from personal experience that asians still know too little about europe. this has resulted in the european union's trade deficit with asia, for example, increasing almost tenfold over the last five years, partly as a result of the crisis. we must therefore urgently increase our presence in the region and make the asians aware of the importance of europe and, above all, of the importance of the european union, so that our companies' trade and investment opportunities can also be enhanced.
the council. - illegal immigration is a major problem for the eu member states and a matter of great concern to the population. the seville european council decided to strengthen the eu's action in this field. it was agreed that the measures taken in the short and long term to manage immigration flows in common must strike a fair balance between, on the one hand, a policy of integration of immigrants who have settled legally in the country and, on the other, an asylum policy which respects international conventions, in particular the 1951 geneva convention. at the same time, our policy must include a resolute fight against illegal immigration and human trafficking. that was point 28 of the seville conclusions. the measures to which the honourable member refers are legislation adopted by individual member states as national measures to deal with the problems they face. it is not within the competence of the council to decide whether measures taken by member states are compatible or incompatible with the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. i would refer to article 51 (1) of the charter, which states that member states are bound by the charter when they implement eu law and only when they implement eu law. the regulations to which the honourable member refers are not, in general, adopted in implementation of eu law and i therefore do not think it makes any sense to ask me to assess whether the member states' measures are in conformity with the charter. the charter has a different character. it is aimed at the implementation of eu law. but i would like to say with my other hat as danish minister for integration that as far as danish legislation in this field is concerned, it fully meets all international standards. i would like to stress that.
- i thank the minister and my former colleague for his answer, of course, but we cannot play a word game. i have asked clearly whether the council did not consider that the measures adopted by the member states were contrary to the spirit, the spirit of the charter. i know that the charter is only a solemn declaration, mr minister, but i can read some very beautiful passages in it: the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. you must admit, mr minister, that in your country too legislation is now being enacted which is at least contrary to the spirit of what is in the charter. i would still like to call on the council, the ministers of the 15 member states, to respect the spirit of the charter. i think this will help to create a more beautiful society.
president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, we have often observed that moments of crisis are always moments from which policy-makers must learn. they're also moments where we learn a lot about society. mrs roth-behrendt and mr bowis have pointed out that there are apparently irresponsible, careless people in the food industry, for whom the only thing that counts is gross profit, gross profit and greed. what lessons can my group and i draw from the nitrophen case in germany and the whyeth pharmaceutical/biolandza case in ireland and belgium? firstly, we must prevent products from recycling waste from entering the food chain for humans and animals, and we must also strengthen controls on the waste stream from the chemical and pharmaceutical industries. for my own country - and i assume this is the case for most countries - we must strive for better cooperation between the services which control the food chain and the services which manage waste streams. i am glad, commissioner, that you are coming to the same conclusion. secondly, there is a european early warning system, including for animal feed, and i think it is working quite well at the moment. should we not urge our national and regional colleagues to have a kind of early warning system set up internally within the member states themselves, especially in federal member states such as germany and belgium, where the powers are spread over several authorities? thirdly, we must ensure that the raw materials that end up in animal feed are perfectly traceable. fourthly, we must, and my group is calling for this, make illegal all practices which do not add nutritional value, do not add any benefit to the consumer and do not provide any technically necessary measures. fifthly, the controls must be increased, that is clear. they also need to be diversified, because the only way to find something is to look for it. this was clearly demonstrated by the nitrofen case in germany, thanks to the fact that the bio-sector itself carried out voluntary controls. sixthly, i am in favour of full producer liability. the whole debate on environmental liability, ladies and gentlemen, needs to be stepped up and we need to get this done more quickly. finally, commissioner, i would like to repeat my call for the adoption of a positive list of products which may be used in animal feed. you announced a feasibility study on that. i would like to ask you specifically: how do you feel about that? in conclusion, there are many jokes in the food industry and only a well-organised government with strict controls can ensure that these jokes are finally eliminated from the sector.
commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, mr president, we are discussing here today a report on a lifelong learning area. this report is part of the process we started a few years ago and which was already discussed at the european councils in stockholm and lisbon, for example. but above all the feira council concluded that lifelong learning will be crucial in the development of our european society in terms of social cohesion, labour market opportunities and so on. in this context, the member states, the commission and parliament were also asked to develop strategies and policies. the commission's communication now, but especially the memorandum published before it, is in that context. what are the points of concern which the european parliament raises in its report? let me start with the principles on which the concept of lifelong learning should be based. that's not unimportant. we talk about it a lot, but do we do it on the same basis? we have already stressed a number of points in parliament's first report. i'll get them to you in a minute. the first is democratization and the principle of equality, which i believe is of crucial importance. if we fail to achieve better and equal access to education and training through lifelong learning, we have failed. everyone knows that there is already a huge gap between the highly educated and the lowly. if we can't make it and succeed in it through lifelong learning, we're going to fail. a second crucial principle is actually the right to individual self-development. it's not just about lifelong learning, it's about lifelong learning. this is a question of the right to work for the individual's self-development, and not just for the labour market. we must also strike a balance between the right which we must create for our citizens, a right which we as a government must be able to guarantee, also through our own means, on the one hand, and the duties and responsibilities of the individual on the other. a second absolutely important aspect, on which we still have a lot of work to do - and i am looking particularly at the commission - is formal and informal learning. formal learning is very important in the union, because we have to ensure that the diplomas of our citizens are also recognised in the other member states. that they know their degrees are worth as much as theirs. to this end, the commission proposes the european curriculum vitae and, later, the portfolio system. that is good, but i find it a bit vague and i also miss very much the link with informal learning. that informal learning is just the crucial aspect of the whole story. we need to ensure that we can validate, certify and also control those informal skills or those acquired elsewhere. because this is again the big difference between those who have a degree and then build on that degree, and those who don't have a degree, who have already fallen off the boat. and we can measure that by recognizing informal learning. the commission has a lot of work to do in this respect, madam commissioner, because we are not really anywhere near there yet. and i expect - and i think my colleagues agree with me - that you will develop the necessary instruments together with the member states. i have already spoken about individual self-development and the fact that it is not just lifelong learning, but also life-long learning. in my first report, and now in the second report, i introduced the concept of the individual learning account. we are asking the commission very explicitly to look into this and to see, together with the european investment bank, whether we cannot free up resources to invest in what is so precious to us, namely our human capital. i will end with some critical notes. i see that we in the union are able to formulate clear objectives or even to enforce objectives. the maastricht treaty, the employment strategies and the benchmarks are all very well known. when we talk about scientific research, we dare to put forward a benchmark. but for education and for lifelong learning, we do not seem to have the political courage to do so. i am actually concerned about that because i think it is crucial that we do. in conclusion, i would like to say this. we also have to find a new word because "lifelong learning" sounds like a punishment to me. if we have to say to our young people coming home from school: you've done well in school, you've got your degree, and now you're going to be learning for the rest of your life, that's not motivating. we can write the best texts and develop the best policies, but if our people and our students do not want to be part of that, then i fear, madam commissioner, that it will be only words and not action. and that's exactly what we need to avoid. so maybe we should hire a communications agency to develop a new word for lifelong learning.
in writing. - i fully supported the commission's proposal on european citizenship. it is certainly not easy to increase the political dimension of european citizenship by decree. it is clear that we must strengthen the actions on the ground to make citizens understand that there is an important space for them to be part of the european citizens' area. obtaining legitimacy for the european union is at that price. we must find the channels of communication which will enable us to inform citizens of the challenges they themselves will face in the future. i regret that, among the requirements that emerge, the eu does not create a specific history course that would reintegrate the various upheavals, wars and revolutions that took place on the european continent as often painful steps to achieve this vision that some had 50 years ago.
in writing. - i fully supported mrs van brempt's report and i welcome the commission's initial proposal. however, if it is inconceivable to imagine that in the changing society in which we live, the acquired skills are obtained once and for all, if a permanent updating is therefore necessary and if it is necessary to recognize that academic systems are not the only generators of knowledge, lifelong learning should not be considered only as a means of adapting the worker to the needs of the market. in the word education and in the word training, there is the fundamental concept of socialization which is one of the pillars of the structuring of a society. if we offer education and training in uncoherent and unconnected, overlapping modules, we will be training citizens without sufficient professional and identity basis. the proposals we are making now must be supplemented by a basic system which, in addition to the knowledge that has been built up, is the know-how of the future citizens who will build europe.
in writing. - like my belgian socialist colleagues, i abstained from the final vote on the resolution on the floods in europe for two reasons. firstly, solidarity must not be limited by borders. as my colleague olga zrihen said in the public hearing, the european parliament is wrongly trying to list the states whose regions have suffered from water damage. italy had to be added, which was omitted from this list, and the final text omits, in particular, the floods in switzerland. secondly, if we want to protect the union's territory better against flooding - and this is certainly the wish of the european parliament - we must take into account the river basins, which ignore the boundaries drawn by history. it is regrettable that this much-needed detail was not included in the text in haste.

- commissioner, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this report is part of a series of reports on asylum, immigration, refugees and security. and you might be surprised to find the last dot. in any case, this report gives an overview of the task; i hope that it will help to maintain the cohesion and consistency of our approaches and guidelines and thus put an end to the completely cacophonous image that a europe without a backbone in this field is giving us. the multitude of sources of law requires that we have a global view and i believe that the proposed method of coordination between member states will contribute to the development of higher standards in this area, while promoting transparency, visibility and, finally, evaluation. from tampere, our actions are aimed at creating a european area of freedom, justice and security. these three words must apply to a single area, where freedom must have the same weight as security and justice, hence the need for a common interpretation of the law and, please, a forward-looking view of a concerted policy. the european space must also be built with the actors on the ground. it must offer all european citizens a protective status, not a refuge from the fear generated by 9/11, that irrational fear which leads to the cold retreat which allows some to maintain xenophobic and racist attitudes in the name of so-called pragmatism. behind all these words, i should remind you, are men, women and children. it is therefore our duty to ensure that their rights are respected, with particular attention to women, children and victims of violence. the affirmation of a europe of human rights is also our way of affirming here in europe, in the face of racism, xenophobia, fanaticism and against the death penalty, that we want to build a europe of freedoms in safety and not a europe of security in the name of racism and xenophobia.
- the proposal for a regulation on the common organization of the market in bananas (com (89) 6 final - doc. i am glad that this regulation offers us a modern version of the outdated legislation on slaughterhouse waste. it was about time, after two years of negotiations. the important point in this regulation is that carcasses of animals and animal products of inferior quality can no longer be introduced into the food chain. only products from slaughtered animals approved for human consumption are allowed. it is also clear that the reuse of animal meal in animal feed is only open to discussion if cannibalism is excluded by the establishment of separate lines and full traceability. the proper functioning of this regulation is therefore a prerequisite. and the final word has not yet been said on that. the prevention of cannibalism will require a major reorganization of the collection and disposal of animal waste. we know that there are a number of manipulators and fraudsters in this sector, who are engaged in very obscure practices, and that this is a very fragile area. this will require a major effort on the part of the member states in the area of controls. mr whitehead has rightly pointed this out. finally, ladies and gentlemen, a word on the kitchen and restaurant waste containing animal products. we reached an honorable compromise on this. it wasn't easy, but the compromise is in front of us. this compromise was reached mainly under pressure from our german and austrian colleagues. i do not want to conceal from you that this is a very sensitive issue in my country. all the agricultural organizations in belgium are calling for an absolute reduction in this practice because of the health risks. small causes can indeed have big consequences. let us think of foot-and-mouth disease and african swine fever. i can, however, tell you that the majority of my group will end up approving the texts presented.
- mr president, the most important thing, as is also clear from the commission proposal and mr sjöstedt's report, is that the cartagena protocol be transposed into european legislation and that it can therefore be enforced here. biosafety and biodiversity are very important issues and it is therefore essential that we ensure that the protocol also becomes eu law. but are we to assume that the protocol is good in itself, or are we to make an exception on this occasion and be stricter than the protocol? if the protocol is to be transposed, we must do so, and i think it is important for scientific research in the union that we do not in any way unnecessarily isolate the union from the rest of the world or make the work unnecessarily more difficult than in the rest of the world. scientists work, trade is needed and borders must be crossed. that's the way science works, and i think it's the least obstructive to that research. what is necessary we must do and what is not necessary we must not do. so i think we should transpose the protocol and there are a number of things in the report that do not help us do that. in the light of the reports on gmos from a few weeks or months ago, i have already pointed out that i personally have a problem with detectable or non-traceable products, things that you can detect or not detect. we are not going to have a second - the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the permissible sound power level of towed vehicles; it cannot be stressed enough that the proposal is important for the third world, among others - and this is sometimes forgotten - and that adequate resources must be made available. i would also like to express my thanks to the rapporteur, not only for the great work he has done, but also for the good work he has done and for the political courage he has shown in making a number of points of criticism against the commission's proposal. i am also a little surprised at what i am hearing here in the plenary, where the division is once again becoming clear. the right side says, look: either we follow the protocol strictly, but if we do, we're doing it right. at the same time, we must ensure that science can continue its work. then we're the good guys. the other side of parliament are those who are always afraid of everything and who want to impose restrictions on everything. well, that's not the way to look at it. you have to look at this in the context of the general debate on gmos. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the consumer is not left behind. we're still not 100% sure about this technology. so we have to make sure that at least the consumer has a choice. we should put this in the protocol. this is also what we must dare to choose for now. i can therefore fully support the rapporteur's political courage and the criticisms he has made. i hope that the plenary will agree with me tomorrow.
