commission. – mr president, first of all i wish to thank the parliament for its invitation to this debate today. i tend to interpret your invitation as the recognition of the political importance of the areas of justice and home affairs and information policy, and definitely not of its budgetary importance, because comparing my budget with those of my colleagues, i feel like a beggar who is invited to a christmas party in wall street. as far as budgetary issues are concerned, in order of presentation, i wish to thank the libe committee, mr ceyhun in particular, for the non-stop support this commission has been given in establishing the area of freedom, security and justice. once again, the libe committee proposes the increase of the budget lines thought to be politically more important. the pdb was prepared by the commission based not only on political considerations, but also on the financial framework approved for each of the programmes, or on past experience in the running of the preparatory actions or pilot projects. moreover, it presents a difficult balance between the human resources needed to ensure the correct management of the credits and those needed to ensure the creation of this area from a legal point of view. for these reasons, the proposed amounts represent a certain stability concerning the total amount, but with a re-centring of activities, a recast of the cooperation programmes, and an intensification of the actions in the area of asylum and immigration, including the external border control and the establishment of eurojust. the commission welcomes the very constructive opinion of the libe committee, which brings into the pdb the major political developments which have occurred since its presentation – such as the conclusions of the european council of seville – which obviously could not have been considered at the time our pdb was presented. nevertheless, i will make a plea for careful examination, bearing in mind such difficulties as human resources. i now turn to the cultural committee, mr perry in particular, and also to the economic and financial affairs committee, particularly mr bergman. in terms of information and communication to the public in general, over the last few years we have seen great progress in cooperation between our institutions, in the provision of information to citizens, at political level between our relevant services and most importantly, on the ground, in the member states. recognising that the challenge of communication with 370 million citizens is immense, and the means available are definitely limited, we are now working increasingly with the member states so that they share the responsibility for informing their citizens, engaging them in a dialogue that is a prerequisite for good governance and increased public support. the adoption by the commission, in july, of the communication on an information and communication strategy for the european union, and the warm welcome it received, both by the culture committee and the interinstitutional group on information, provide tangible evidence of our shared political will to be proactive in generating awareness, combating ignorance and apathy and improving perceptions about the union. returning to budgetary aspects, i must underline that, as in all other aspects of the budget, the commission took a very realistic position in relation to information and communication. we recognised that, whilst informing and communicating with citizens must be a priority, we have to ensure that we have the capacity, especially in terms of human resources, to manage the expenditure of the funds. the pdb is therefore the firm basis for our consideration of amendments, with one exception that relates to the new priority topic proposed in the july communication mentioned above. i am referring to the role of europe in the world. if the interinstitutional group on information confirms this proposal at its meeting later today, we will suggest that an additional eur 1.5 million should be committed for the prince programme. this will be for a campaign which specifically relates to the role of europe in the world and our actions to regulate globalisation so that it can bring benefits to our citizens and the wider world. the commission welcomes the constructive opinions of the culture committee as well as the economic and monetary affairs and the constitutional affairs committee. at the meeting of the interinstitutional group on information later today, we must reflect on the amounts proposed in the amendments and the practical implications. i will of course emphasise the need to ensure that we have the necessary human resources available to spend those amounts in an appropriate and proper way. on the subject of expert assistance and the priority campaigns, we particularly welcome the specific proposal that the budget line for financing of prince correspondents should also be increased. without such an increase, we will not be able to engage member states in developing the priority prince campaigns. we also strongly support the amendment to restore a cut of eur 500 000 made by the council, in the line that provides for the use of information correspondents to work with the media in the member states and for the use of eurojust experts to answer citizens' questions relating to european law. on the euro campaign, i must re-state what the commission has already said in the past. the information to the candidate countries on the euro is included in the enlargement campaign, and the commission believes that, in the case of the countries which are not yet part of the euro, the necessary funds will be proposed the day that each of these countries decides to join the euro. finally, in the new basis of cooperation between the parliament and the commission and engaging the member states, we will be able to improve our information and communication policy and therefore make better use of taxpayers' money.
- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, as a member of the epp committee on civil liberties, i should like to begin by expressing my regret, as did mr ceyhun, that the commission and the council have not really translated into the budget the priority they claim to give to the creation of an area of freedom, security and justice. it is good to state a priority, but it must also be translated into action. however, the appropriations for the area of freedom, security and justice are increasing less than the average rate of increase for internal policies. why, commissioner? i would like to say that the epp group supports the amendments of the committee on civil liberties proposed by our rapporteur in the important area of immigration and asylum policy. i would like to know whether the commission and the council are prepared to follow us in this direction. this is the first time that all the instruments for an active and balanced policy on the management of migration flows have been identified and strengthened in the committee on civil liberties. we want to strengthen the means of better understanding migration flows by better subsidising the european migration observatory. we want to strengthen control of the external borders. we support the strengthening of the means of cooperation with third countries. we support the increase in appropriations to support the integration efforts of immigrants who are legally established on our territory and we also support the increase in the appropriations for the european refugee fund, which we regret that in the draft budget and the preliminary draft budget they have been reduced compared with the previous year. i would like to know whether the commission and the council are prepared, in these matters which are strategic, not only from the point of view of the evolution of the european union, but also from the point of view of the way our fellow citizens perceive them, to strengthen the means provided for in these various areas. a final question to the commissioners and the council. last year, the european parliament allocated eur 5 million in the 2002 budget to europol to fight terrorism. why did the commission wait nine months before proposing a legal basis? is the council prepared, yes or no, to accept this legal basis and to ensure that these appropriations can be used effectively?
the commission has also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. - mr president, i shall be dealing with two different points on behalf of the committee on women's rights, which i believe are the two most important of the note. firstly, as far as the equality budget as such is concerned, i think it is important that the money for the initiatives in favour of equal opportunities for women - initiatives taken by the ngos - can indeed be allocated in an easy way. the conditions which the commission is now imposing are far too heavy and far too difficult. it is a matter of the european parliament, the council and the commission. for a number of women's organizations, this is not possible. that is why we have proposed to allow initiatives from three countries, which may also have a more limited budget. i urge that the budget be used more efficiently. the second point which is of great importance to us is the gender policy. this should not be limited to the equality budget alone, it must be present in all community policies. i would like to draw particular attention to one point, namely the conditions for cooperation with developing countries in the political, economic and cultural fields. commissioner nielson said just now that the conditions for cooperation in the field of human rights with the acp countries, asia and latin america are very difficult. i do not think that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. that is why we are absolutely insisting - and this is a real must for us in the women's committee, and i think also for all the women in this parliament - that cooperation with countries be suspended if they do not take measures to prevent violence against women, stoning, public chastisement, genital mutilation, burning or rape. we therefore ask that the aid be withdrawn in such cases.
- mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i want to go straight to the door. the most environmentally friendly mileage for a truck is a non-driven mileage. so we need to do more work on transport prevention. the marco polo programme is only about other transport, not less transport. a programme such as marco polo must be more ambitious than the current design. this ambition must therefore also be translated into projects aimed at transport prevention. the european union aims to achieve annual economic growth. however, this economic growth is accompanied by growing transport demand. worse, that transport demand is now even greater than economic growth. the link between economic growth and transport growth is dramatic in several ways. the european economy is thus becoming less and less efficient in transport. we're driving more and more miles to achieve the same economic results. there's more traffic jams and accidents. second, the explosion in mileage is costing enormous amounts of fossil fuel and causing environmental and social damage. i cannot agree with the rapporteur's comments on the dubiousness of the external cost calculation and the increasing eco-efficiency of road transport. individual trucks do better, but more and more people are driving. this increase in mileage negates the profit that the fuel-efficient and clean engine brings. displacement must be avoided and economic growth and transport growth must be decoupled. we therefore call for projects aimed at transport prevention to be included in a programme such as marco polo and not excluded from the outset. marco polo is a very important project for us. marco polo may be more ambitious, but it must also provide opportunities for projects which avoid transport, that is the real innovation.
the rapporteur. - mr president, this is the second time that social policy and equal opportunities policy for men and women have been brought up so late in the agenda. i would ask those who draw up the agenda to change their agenda and put economic and agricultural policy on the agenda at a later date, for example, so that we can discuss our reports at noon and not always have mrs diamantopoulou and us sitting here at midnight. now the report. why has a report been drawn up on the representation of women in the social partners? firstly, because there has been a huge increase in the number of women in the workforce. in europe, women account for 42% of the total working population. the same increase is not reflected in the bodies that have a say in policy. what are those organs? these are the structural bodies of the social partners themselves, their presidents, their secretaries-general, their executive committees, their general meetings, their negotiating committees, etc. so women are underrepresented in that. even where the social partners act as delegates in advisory councils, these delegates are usually not women. however, the social partners are asking for, and we are happy to give them, a great deal of power in europe and in the member states. the collective agreements they conclude, which therefore also concern women, have, of course, in general the force of law. a large part of the legislative power, namely that relating to labour, has been ceded to them. well, this is possible provided that the social partners are representative. if they're not, this can't really happen. i claim that they are not representative. if, for example, the unions have less than 25% of women on their boards of directors, and in some boards less than 12% or 10%, and if the employers' organizations have 1% or 2% of women on their boards, then i say that these social partners are not representative of the world of work. so that's the report. we therefore call, first of all, for data to be collected, for a database to be set up on the presence of women in the social partners. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the unions are still doing this, they're keeping track of it for a while. for employers, this is practically impossible. if you ask member state x how many women are in its employers' organisation, you get no answer, no figures, no data. there's a reluctance or they're not available. the creation of a database is therefore a first requirement. i know that the commission is working on this and that a study has been commissioned. but that mandate will run for four years, and i hope, madam commissioner, that we will not wait four years to take action. if we have to wait for the numbers, it's gonna be a very long time before anything happens. i think that the european commission, the member states and the social partners should pursue a policy similar to that in the political world. fortunately, the political parties have gradually been persuaded that the political bodies must be representative of the people of their country. if 50% of the population in these countries are women, and this is the case in all countries, often more, then the political world must also be representative of these women. a policy is being pursued both within europe and within the member states. unfortunately, the same is not true of the social partners. the report therefore proposes a series of measures which must be implemented either by the european union or by the social partners themselves, with a little pressure from the member states, in order to achieve an acceptable representation of women in all the negotiating bodies and in the bodies of the social partners, with the aim of including women's problems in the negotiations. it is obvious that those who sit around the negotiating table are the most sensitive to their own problems, and that is always the case. that's normal, they know them best. it's like having a negotiating table with disabled people. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. that goes for negotiating tables where only men sit. they are very sensitive to their own problems and not so sensitive to those of the women. so our theory is that, please commission, you should also have a policy on this whole issue.

- i would like to congratulate mr titley in particular because just as the 1998 eu code was an important step towards a more coherent and transparent european policy, so too the successive titley reports are a constructive and important contribution by this parliament to the achievement of a binding european code of conduct. the need for such a code has indeed been demonstrated recently in my country, belgium, where a minister resigned because of an arms shipment to nepal. i would like to say, by the way, that i regret that some green colleagues are using this report for internal political purposes and i am surprised, mr staes, that they are tabled amendments condemning a decision of the belgian government when their green colleagues in the government have approved that decision. but much more fundamentally, the belgian government has announced that it intends to incorporate the provisions of the code of conduct into national law, thereby making it legally binding. i hope that other member states will follow suit. in this way we can avoid that arms orders which are refused by one country can be accepted by another. in his report mr titley also uses the existing belgian law on arms trafficking as an example. indeed, that law provides for severe penalties for those who deal in arms without a license. but the problem is not just in the country of origin. it is particularly concerned with the final destination. a renowned brussels research centre announced this week that half of all arms transactions do not comply with the legal requirement of the certificate of arrival. there is therefore an urgent need for a final control system at the final destination. it is crucial, mr president, to know in whose hands the weapons will end up. otherwise, the european code of conduct, however well-intentioned, is useless.

in writing. - the european union code of conduct is the most comprehensive international arms export control regime. the european union is actively involved in the limitation and control of arms exports and is involved in the moralization of the arms trade. but beyond legal procedures and treaties, complementary action is needed to limit the proliferation of weapons. the prevention of conflict is not just about limiting arms exports. to be effective, it must be accompanied, in the developing countries concerned, by support for democracy and the liberal democratic system (protection of civilians by the state, education in a culture of peace, creation of institutions for the negotiated settlement of conflict, etc.). it is therefore important to stress that a nascent and fragile democratic regime cannot be prevented from defending itself against a rebellion that seeks to overthrow it. in addition, industrialized countries must also support micro-disarmament, demining, security force training and legal support programmes in their development cooperation projects.
- mr president, we have always been in favour of a united and strong europe in which each european people has its place and can preserve its cultural identity. the same applies to the peoples of eastern europe, who for decades have lived under the yoke of communism and who for decades have not been able to count on the solidarity of anyone in this hemisphere or of a political family in this hemisphere who are now, however, in the lead. i am therefore not opposed to the principle of european enlargement, provided that it is of course european enlargement and not a small asian enlargement with a country like turkey, which belongs neither geographically, nor historically, nor politically, nor culturally to europe. i am opposed to the hasty and dogmatic enlargement which is being prepared today and which i fear is likely to cause major social, economic, cultural and societal problems. problems both in the present member states, which will no doubt face another large wave of immigration, but also in the candidate countries, whose highly educated people, engineers, doctors, nurses, construction workers and so on, will no doubt leave their country for financial reasons to find better paid employment elsewhere in europe. if the free movement of persons, goods and capital is in fact going to lead to more poverty, more immigration, more crime, more uprooting for the countries of eastern europe and for us, then the politicians responsible must first of all think about how to make this integration more harmonious. so let's think about it a little bit more, let's do that extension, but let's say, at the moment, today, free movement of persons is not yet possible. if we do not do this, we are today threatening to plant a time bomb under europe which may explode sooner than we think, or, in the words of la fontaine: let us not let europe suffer the tragic fate of that frog, that frog which once dreamed of becoming an ox too soon.
- mr president, commissioners, in flanders we have dreamed of being flemish since the 19th century in order to become european. the accession of ten new countries will enlarge the european area of peace and stability. the space for human rights and democracy is growing. that is a reason to rejoice. candidate countries were given strong incentives to make the necessary changes. we have not, however, made the same effort ourselves to bring about the changes needed to make this a real europe. previous enlargements have shown that prosperity has increased, both in the countries concerned and for the citizens of the union. mr prodi has said that the costs will be compensated, but not all of our citizens are convinced of that. on the contrary, it will take courage and persuasiveness to overcome feelings of uncertainty and fear, especially in a time of economic recession. in addition, there are still gaps and gaps. mr prodi himself mentioned fraud, corruption and human trafficking. the problems of cyprus and the internal problems of poland were discussed. there is also the debate on turkey which is hanging over everything. we must not create false expectations. that is why it is necessary for us to tell our citizens what is at stake. as regionalists and stateless peoples, we are very sensitive to the still existing problems of discrimination against peoples and cultural minorities. we are sensitive to the lack of recognition of regional and historical realities in the various acceding countries. all of this makes us wonder whether we still have the time and the instruments to put these things right before accession becomes effective. but today we are saying yes to accession because to say no would be a sign of hostility, whereas we are dreaming of that area of peace and stability.
- mr president, after 9/11, a broad international coalition was formed to fight terrorism. this is a fight that is still being fought today by us europeans. fifty-two countries are still home to dormant al-qaeda networks. after 30 january, president bush's state of the union address has left many europeans baffled. the axis countries had nothing to do with international terrorism anymore. fortunately, commissioner patten at the time made a clear distinction between a manichean and unilateral us policy. today, we are being forced to accept a concept of pre-emptive war in iraq that is outside the framework of international law. in the present discourse of the american government, no one can dissociate oil interests from the legitimate concern about weapons of mass destruction, from the equally legitimate concern about human rights and the very person of saddam hussein, whom mr bush says he hates. this amalgam cannot be ours, because it deserves different treatment. europe has always been aligned with the united nations security council. but in recent months, at the instigation of the united states, we have seen a real opa on international law, a policy of double standards: sometimes violations of human rights and un security council resolutions are ignored in a country and not sanctioned, and sometimes they are. we are also witnessing, and this is dramatic, an internal division in the european union and the preparation of a war which would be a real clash of civilizations and would undoubtedly strengthen the terrorism we want to fight. the credibility of the un and europe is at stake in this arms race and race against time. one thing is certain: in the event of military intervention in iraq, the iraqi people who have already suffered so much will defend themselves to the last man and we will not enter baghdad as liberators.
- mr president, i do not think that the recent conflict in côte d'ivoire is a surprise to anyone who is trying to follow developments there on a permanent basis. it has a history of political, economic and, above all, ethnic contradictions that are constantly intersecting and reinforcing each other. the motion for a resolution before us deliberately or unconsciously ignores the responsibilities of those in power today. ivory coast is the world's largest producer of cocoa and one of the most important producers of coffee. the country has almost 5 million migrants, mainly from neighbouring burkina faso, mali and senegal. in times of economic boom they are used as cheap labour. in the event of a political or economic crisis, they are the scapegoats par excellence. the xenophobic campaign in the state media was fuelled by president gbagbo's accusations that burkina faso was behind the mutiny, without providing any evidence. many people in northern ivory coast have their roots in burkina. they feel that they are now treated as second-class citizens, especially since their leader ouattara was excluded from participating in the presidential elections because of his alleged burkinabe roots. the populations in the north felt disqualified from the political system, as did ouattara. that is the crux of the problem. ivory coast has become a broken nation through the political abuse of religious differences and the concept of ivoirité. madam president, the international community has made a special effort to evacuate french nationals and 200 children of american clergy. i hope that it will show the same diligence to prevent further civilian casualties and that the instability in côte d'ivoire does not develop into a regional conflict with unforeseeable consequences.

- mr president, i would like to thank the services of commissioner liikanen, and especially mrs grossetête and mrs müller, for the quality of their work on one of the most important public health issues of this parliament. the main points have been said, and i shall now deal with the remaining points of friction. as regards the european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products in london, i think it is important to stress the essential role played by this authority over the last seven years, a role which, in view of the forthcoming enlargement and for the better functioning of the internal market, deserves to be extended to all medicinal products using a new active substance. that is why the liberal group is formally opposed to amendment no 136 to mrs müller's report. on the protection of commercial data, the compromise adopted in committee, 8 + 2, balances the requirements and i am perfectly happy with it. why would you do that ? because no one has been able to demonstrate a direct and unique link between industrial protection and the success of generics. the case of germany and the united kingdom, with ten years of firm data protection and generics occupying 20% of the market, are striking examples. attacking development and research is a misdirection and a threat to the very foundation of health care. it would seem to me that it would be more honest to recognize the real influence on the development of generics of a genuine policy of price and reimbursement incentives and, at the same time, of educational action by health professionals towards their patients, and i agree with mrs oomen-ruijten's arguments. finally, on the subject of patient information, i am one of those members who are not satisfied with the vote in committee. firstly, because the european commission has had the courage to tackle head on an important and practical problem which, whether we like it or not, must be solved. the free access to medical information on the internet is currently uncontrolled, with potentially damaging consequences, as commissioner liikanen pointed out at the beginning of this debate. secondly, because correct information, even if it is to be reported, is not necessarily advertising, and because the amalgamation which some have cleverly cultivated is reductive and misleading. it was therefore necessary to clarify and refine the commission's text, as mrs grossetête tried to do, and not to delete it altogether. in conclusion, mr president, and this is the most important point, the rapporteurs have sufficiently emphasized it, it is imperative to maintain a fair balance between the essential priorities of public health and those aimed at promoting the competitiveness of the pharmaceutical sector, and thus research in europe, and the completion of our single market. mr president, the reports by mrs grossetête and mrs müller which are on the table today have in common an implicit but fundamental premise: medicine is not a product like any other, it affects health and health is not negotiable. i do not know whether it is a divine right, as has been said, but it is an inalienable right. the rapporteur has, however, achieved the almost impossible. i would like to medicinal product, which is still pending. yet too many examples from the past, like thalidomide, show us that the side effects of a drug can last well beyond five years, and, furthermore, medicine is evolving so fast that for the same disease, the treatment changes very quickly. the phenomenon cannot be fully covered by pharmacovigilance, which is very important. the second point is the idea of the therapeutic value added for a new drug. this simple idea, very different from cost-benefit, should be understood by researchers in the pharmaceutical industry. because what good is an innovation if it can't demonstrate its therapeutic advantage? if we accept that the pharmaceutical industry should pay for its research in the cost of its product, we can rightly expect it to do so in accordance with the most common scientific criteria.

- council - decisions on the granting of export licences are taken individually by each member state, taking full account of the code of conduct for arms exports and the criteria laid down therein. the implementation of arms export policies and thus of the code remains the responsibility of the member states. the council has not been informed of the situation mentioned by the honourable member in his question. in any event, the council does not receive information on the reasons why member states either grant or refuse export licences, and it is not within the council's competence to pass judgement on them in any way. at its meeting on 25 january 1999, the council discussed a proposal by the presidency that the code should be legally binding, for example through a joint holding company, but there was no agreement on this and the question of changing the code into a legally binding act has therefore not been raised since.

in writing. - the european parliament must oppose any change to the common agricultural policy that would upset the terms of the contract that, until 2006, binds the european government and the agricultural sector, especially as everything indicates that the union's agricultural expenditure remains far below the standards set in berlin in 1999. the common agricultural policy must give priority to ensuring food security, both in terms of quantity and quality, and the european union must impose the same quality and safety requirements on agricultural products imported from third countries as those applied to union production. on the other hand, given that agriculture is heavily involved in maintaining economic activity in rural areas and in the right balance in terms of spatial planning, these characteristics require the establishment and maintenance of specific measures. regulatory stability and the maintenance of income policy guarantee investment choices and the sustainability of farms and agricultural enterprises. we must avoid any limitation of aid to farmers that encourages large farms to develop further, to the detriment of the family farming model, which is human-sized and respectful of the environment.
in writing. - the proposal from the commission to the council (com (89) 0176 - doc. this resolution calls for the maintenance and strengthening of the european agricultural model currently in force. this is bad news for developing countries and for africa, where the majority of the population depends on agriculture for their livelihood. europe's agricultural subsidies ensure that farmers in developing countries cannot compete with their european counterparts. export subsidies also result in the dumping of surplus eu agricultural production in developing countries at prices below those at which these countries are able to produce themselves. while income support independent of production is justifiable, export subsidies are not. they are a factor of market distortion, they are harmful to the farmers of the poorest countries and they are contrary to all the principles of liberalization of the world market. the system is above all unfair: whereas the poorest countries were forced to abolish their agricultural subsidies under the terms of the imf in the 1980s, the rich countries continue to subsidize their agriculture 20 years later. who 's paying for it ? the european taxpayer and small farmers in developing countries. the big agribusiness companies are the winners. in writing. - article 11 (2) of the charter of fundamental rights of the european union states that: "the freedom of the media and their pluralism shall be respected". indeed, what is more fundamental in a democracy than the freedom to communicate and receive information? however, in today's europe, phenomena of concentration, which are even more pronounced today by certain technological developments, mean that this indispensable plurality of media is threatened, if not already compromised. it is time for the commission to take up the matter and ensure, in particular in the television sector, a plurality of operators, as it already does in many other sectors. if the current founding treaties do not provide a sufficient legal basis for this necessary regulation, then it is essential that the convention work on a new constitutional basis for media pluralism. but this is not enough, because the plurality of private operators cannot on their own ensure the fundamental role of information in a modern democracy, nor safeguard cultural diversity. the importance of public service must therefore be reaffirmed here. i am thinking not only of national public service television channels but also of local channels, whose usefulness in terms of conviviality and the social link that has been restored cannot be disputed. (intervention reduced pursuant to article 120 (7) of the regulation)
- as a european parliament, mr president, we must respect the principles of the rule of law, which means that we must follow the decisions of our european court of justice. as far as tobacco advertising is concerned, as we know, this court of justice has annulled an earlier directive on the grounds that it was based on an incorrect legal basis. i am not surprised by this because when we were discussing this first directive, the committee on legal affairs, which was chaired by me, had clearly advised that the european union had no competence to issue such a directive. in short, the law has its rights. i would therefore like to congratulate mr medina ortega on his achievements as rapporteur. i want to emphasize right away that i will be following him during the vote. the rapporteur has made sure that the directive which is now being drawn up is legally sound. the european union can indeed impose a ban on tobacco advertising on member states if there is a clear cross-border effect. but i would add that i think it is necessary that the ban on tobacco advertising, if it has a cross-border effect, be treated in the same way in all the member states. member states which now take initiatives themselves are disrupting the proper functioning of the internal market and often putting themselves at a competitive disadvantage. i take the example of my own country, belgium, and the sponsorship of major sporting events in belgium. our country has already passed a law banning all tobacco advertising from 2003. this goes far beyond the current proposed european regulation. is that wise? i think that a general european regulation, as proposed by our rapporteur, should, by its general application, ensure that unilateral measures with often unfortunate consequences are avoided in the future.
- mr president, commissioner, i will not repeat what my colleague heidi hautala has already said. it is obvious that my group and i share his view. i would simply like to refer to what our colleague mr berger said. i think the commission should make a proposal to the european convention to amend the current legal bases of the treaty in the field of public health. mrs berger is right to insist that the convention's working group has delivered conclusions which have been severely criticised in the convention's plenary. i think it is absolutely essential that we should be allowed to legislate on public health, because the arguments put forward here on subsidiarity do not hold water. on the one hand, the council and the community institutions retain their discretion as to whether or not to legislate. secondly, if we follow the who studies, we understand that for the ban on advertising to be effective, it must be general and comprehensive. the way forward is global legislation on this matter. let us at least give ourselves the means of european legislation, otherwise - and i link this to the previous debate on the concentration of the media - what will happen is that economic operators will play the card of national legislation against others. this argument also argues in favour of european legislation.
- the president. - mr maaten has rightly touched upon the substance of the debate. in this connection, i regret that his report is not being discussed together with that of medina ortega today. i think it would have certainly sharpened the momentum of the debate. tobacco kills, as my colleague mr tsatsos has pointed out. 548,000 deaths a year in europe, that's one every minute. mr maaten rightly said that a ban on tobacco advertising is working and is legal. i would like to add a few examples. norway has had a ban on advertising since '75, resulting in: 31% less consumption, finland since '78: minus 34% consumption, new zealand since '90: 33% less consumption. we must also rightly denounce the anomaly that we are allocating eur 1 billion a year in subsidies to tobacco producers and that we are having difficulty in coming up with a comprehensive strategy to reduce tobacco use. finally, ladies and gentlemen, i would also like to draw attention to the tobacco lobby politicians. i ask you to support the amendments by mrs stihler and others, which i have also signed, calling for the introduction of a system of recording donations from the tobacco industry to political parties and politicians.
- mr president, whatever some of my colleagues may say, this debate is not about emotions. it is a question of the extent to which we can and will remove the existing obstacles to the functioning of the internal market in the field we are discussing. anyone who has read the judgment of 5 october 2000 will know that the court of justice has precisely chosen to annul the first directive in its entirety. on the one hand, because it recognises that there is effective scope for the legislator to act in a harmonising manner and, on the other hand, because it did not want to put itself in the legislator's place as a court of justice. this was a wise decision by the court. i hope that we as co-legislators will act with the same wisdom. i think that means that we accept the commission's proposal unchanged, because the alternatives are less good. if we were to impose limits on tobacco advertising beyond those proposed by the commission, our ambition would run counter to the limits of our competence and we would risk a new judgment. if we go any further than the commission proposes, we are making a political turn. because, ladies and gentlemen, did we not pretend in '98 to want to go for maximum public health protection? the commission proposal for a directive respects the limits of competence. that is not only my political belief, but also my legal belief. it is a light version of the first directive, but we have to live with it for legal reasons. however, we must not go so far as to make this light proposal ultra-light. that is not necessary and makes us politically unreliable. let us therefore approve the proposal unchanged. that is what people expect of us and that is what we are indeed competent to do.
- mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, if the peaceful unification of the european continent began symbolically with the fall of the berlin wall, we now know that the enlargement of the european union to include the first ten candidate countries will take place, according to the foreign affairs council yesterday, 1 may 2004. but we also know that the transition, or rather the process of transforming the economies of the countries concerned, will take much longer. i would simply point out that a country like poland has an official unemployment rate of 17% and that in 2000 its gdp per capita was only 39% of the community average. so, in connection with this process of transformation, i hear many of my colleagues repeat - and they are probably right - all the conditions which the applicant countries still have to fulfil. i would like to ask you, mr president-in-office, very directly and very specifically what the council is going to do in response to the demands made by the governments of the 10 applicant countries in warsaw last friday. i admit that i am not an expert on the subject, but their demands seem to me to be quite legitimate: a financial package that generates macroeconomic stability, a net financial situation that must be improved not only for 2004, but also from 2004 to 2006, and finally the full use of the budgetary commitment of berlin. that seems fair enough to me. i am afraid that the danish prime minister said this morning that he did not intend to depart from the positions adopted at the brussels european council, and i would like to know exactly whether, in response to these legitimate requests from the governments of the applicant countries, you are going to make a better offer than brussels, or whether you are going to stick to what was decided in brussels. i think that beyond the generalizations we have set out, there are concrete demands to be met, which have been, i repeat, formulated, in my opinion, with all legitimacy, by the governments of the applicant countries.
- mr president, the debate today focused on the question of the degree of readiness of the applicant countries for accession. we regret that the question of whether the european union is ready has not been asked, or hardly has been asked. in the euphoria of the moment, we must not be blind to a number of problems. the main issue, i think, is still the financing of enlargement and the reform of the agricultural policy which is inherently linked to it. i continue to regret that the necessary reform has been delayed until 2007. i also regret that farmers in the new member states will not be treated in the same way as farmers in the present european union until 2013. the present european agricultural policy, which links subsidies to production, is indefensible and unacceptable. it creates surpluses, high food prices and environmental damage and it is also very harmful to farmers in the third world. i think that we as a parliament must therefore continue to support commissioner fischler in his efforts to bring about a more rapid and thorough reform of the agricultural policy. with a reorientation of subsidies towards rural development and the new member states. we must avoid at all costs the development of a two-speed europe. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
- the president, madam commissioner, mr president-in-office, ladies and gentlemen, mr caudron has just given us a list of good intentions, and i am not going to repeat them. i would just like to say that i am happy about that, but i would have preferred a more strategic debate. i think, as does mrs wallis, that we must set priorities and that europe must define its place and its role in the world and in the future of the world. that's actually my top priority and our top priority. first, we must adapt our own institutions to this and ask ourselves whether our own home is in order. i would like to draw attention once again to the role of the regions, which is becoming a pressing issue. now so many small states will join the european union with all that that entails, including their own language, their own place in the council, in parliament and in the commission. the constitutional regions, the other regions too, the regions with their own language, their own culture, even with their own legislative and executive institutions, are deprived of all this and are left empty-handed. i ask you, then, whether they should be allowed to take a respectable place in the european union? that is an incitement to separatism. secondly: we want the full integration of the countries which will become the european union with us. but do we not need to do more to ensure that the people of those countries are actually involved? for example, i get complaints that many people do not find the european union documents available in their own language. but i would like to pay more attention, because that was the debate yesterday, to the circles of partner countries which we have to build up. i think that this debate on the development of the second circle is essential if we are to have a positive debate on the union's borders. i personally do not believe that europe should extend to the borders of iraq, iran and syria, but we should be able to offer turkey an attractive partnership. we are not talking about that at the moment. then there's our role in the world. peace and development today involve more than just the follow-up to johannesburg. as long as europe is more part of the problem of poverty than part of the solution, all our good things, which we are certainly doing, mr nielsen, are to be questioned. because we know that as long as we farm, as long as we trade as we do now, we are more part of the problem.
in writing. - the adoption of the european parliament resolution on the european commission's report on enlargement - progress report - provides strong and positive support for europe and actively follows up the conclusions of the laeken european council of december 2001. this resolution gives a balanced view of the impact of enlargement on agriculture and smes. the vote also rejected amendments seeking to exclude turkey from the normal accession procedure. the votes which safeguard turkey's chances of joining the european union should be welcomed.
in writing. - when the council adopted a directive in 1999 authorising the application of reduced vat rates to certain labour-intensive services, it was understood that this measure was an experimental measure which would expire on 31 december 2002. in the circumstances, the decision to extend this measure until the end of 2003 is a positive one, as the rapporteur has stressed. it is necessary to avoid, after 31 december 2002, a legal vacuum which would be detrimental to the undertakings concerned, which are mainly selfemployed, smes and small and medium-sized enterprises. the horeca sector should benefit from a preferential vat rate, given the very high level of social and tax burdens it generally bears. as a labour-intensive activity, the catering industry and the caterers are having difficulties not only to finance their investments but also to recruit additional staff. the professionals in this sector rightly believe that the lowering of the vat rate would make it possible to hire a considerable number of employees. a lower vat rate would also allow many establishments to adjust working hours. finally, this measure would make it possible to collect tax more fairly.
- i regret to see that the european institutions, the commission, the council and unfortunately also parliament are making a fool of themselves by voting, on the one hand, for restrictions on tobacco advertising and, on the other hand, for continued subsidies to those who produce it. as long as this schizophrenia persists, it will be hypocritical to vote for moralizing resolutions, and i will not support them.
- mr president, is it really not naive to think that this directive is the panacea to the harmful effects of smoking? does the european commission really believe that a complete ban on tobacco advertising will stop smoking? i am of the opinion that this is wishful thinking. i also believe that people should be free to ask questions about products, as long as they are not prohibited products. i also think that there are more appropriate ways of tackling this problem, for example by drawing up european guidelines which impose standards on tobacco products. finally, i would draw your attention to the fact that this is a contradiction in terms. we are now going to ban tobacco advertising, but every year millions of european subsidies flow to tobacco growers. understand who can.

mr president, the proposal for a directive on additives for use in animal feedingstuffs must be welcomed as a major step forward in the field of human health, animal health and the environment. it does indeed correct, albeit partially, but that is already an unacceptable situation, since it proposes to ban antibiotics as additives in animal feed. i would remind you that antibiotics, in addition to their ability to kill bacteria, the source of infectious diseases, have a property discovered a long time ago - back in the 1940s - namely that they improve the conversion of food in animals and thus accelerate growth. so a battery-raised broiler, with antibiotic-doped food, can reach a weight of eight pounds in seven weeks. it is then ready for consumption, whereas its natural counterpart weighs barely 1 kilogram after these same seven weeks. so he'll have to wait twice as long before he's ready to consume. the economic benefits of using antibiotics as additives are obvious. the practice of doping farm animals with subtherapeutic doses of antibiotics has become widespread with the development of intensive livestock farming. the use of this has been officially justified by the fact that it not only helps to accelerate growth but also protects animals from infectious diseases. it should be noted that the conditions of livestock farming play a major role in the spread of these diseases. this practice was legalised in 1970 by directive 70/524/eec, which at the time authorised the use of 13 antibiotics as additives. since the 1950's, however, a potentially harmful consequence of overuse of antibiotics has been discovered - the emergence of resistant bacteria that render the antibiotic in question ineffective. over the years, the phenomenon has been confirmed and i would remind you that in 1985 - 17 years before the commission's proposal - it led the swedish parliament to vote for a general ban. although it is a pity that we have waited so long, we should be glad that we have decided to act now. i propose, as mr kindermann has done, that coccidiostats be banned also as additives and that they be retained only as medicines.
- mr president, like my colleagues, i too think that this is a generally positive proposal, that the objectives pursued by the commission must be supported and that the rapporteur's proposals are generally good. my group will therefore support them. i think, for example, that the proposal to set up an autonomous committee on herbal medicines, with the same powers as the committee on conventional medicines and obviously under the authority of the european agency, is certainly a good idea. it's a great idea, and i think it will give us safe access to quality herbal medicine. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's health and safety standards are maintained. the second positive element is the amendment which makes it possible not to confuse food supplements with medicines. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right of initiative. finally, there is the amendment proposed by mr davies: i think it is reasonable and i share his view that the same traditional shelf life should be required for herbal preparations, whatever their origin. i think it is a bit arrogant to think that countries outside the european union cannot provide us with accurate and high-quality information, which would allow us to use these medicines safely.
i would like to add my thanks to the words of thanks already spoken here for ieke van den burg and i would like to stress once again the importance of this directive. i have already been told by a number of colleagues that the temporary agency sector is a very fast-growing sector. if we want to ensure that this sector provides flexibility in the labour market, at the same time providing quality jobs for the people who work in it and, moreover, not suppressing permanent recruitment, then good protection rules must be put in place, also at european level. bartho pronk is right when he says that the systems and the arrangements differ in the various member states. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. that is why the non-regression clause in the directive is so important. however, there are also many member states which do not have adequate protection. the commission has already made a number of proposals. this is not a one-size-fits-all directive. there is room for a variety of national choices. i would like to make three very brief comments, ladies and gentlemen. first of all, in connection with what stephen hughes said just now. it's crucial to us that temporary workers are protected from day one. that, commissioner, was a weak point in the commission's proposal. you will therefore understand that it is absolutely unacceptable for us to agree to the liberal amendment which excludes temporary agency work for up to a year. that would make the directive an empty box. secondly, it is important that temporary workers have good working conditions. i still believe that the best way of ensuring equal treatment is to compare a temporary worker with the employees of the hiring company in terms of his essential working conditions. but then, of course, we must also ensure that what we define as essential working conditions is also a good definition. such a definition without the concept of remuneration? that's where my leg breaks. that would completely undermine the principle of equal treatment. thirdly, an exception to the principle of equal treatment is acceptable, but only in the case of schemes which provide good guarantees internally in the employment sector itself, including for the period between two contracts. the countries where this is well regulated usually guarantee contracts of indefinite duration or long contracts. i would like to warn my epp colleagues not to go down a dangerous slippery slope by over-emphasizing these guarantees. i hope, commissioner, as do you, that we as legislators will be able to do what the social partners have not been able to do, namely to guarantee all temporary workers throughout the european union a proper social protection. i am sure that ieke van den burg will be able to do that.

not all, of our colleagues are opposed to the renationalisation of competition policy, which is a strong political message. you have just referred to the recent judgments of the european court of of the competition council on this subject. efforts must be made to ensure that the various commission actions are compatible with and consistent with european policies. in this context, the that a specific attitude was adopted towards the newly reunified länder. the last point of disagreement between members of the committee is the assessment of the liberalization of a number of sectors of the economy. some consider the processes under way to be rather satisfactory and balanced, while others consider that these liberalization processes do not meet all the objectives of the european mr president, the proposal for a council recommendation on tobacco prevention only partially reflects my view. the european commission is going too far in some areas. in addition to the many aspects of prevention and control, this recommendation also aims to regulate tobacco advertising. why does the commission come up with two texts which both seek to restrict advertising? it is not the intention that advertising which is not intended to promote tobacco should be covered by this recommendation. it is a pity that the european parliament has no eye for the brand diversification businesses. these are mainly specialist and often very small companies using an existing brand name to launch new products on the market. but i can of course be satisfied with the fact that it is ultimately just a non-binding instrument which should serve as an impetus for the member states.
in writing. - tobacco kills, the litany becomes tiresome, so i will allow myself to irritate some, and to repeat that tobacco kills one out of every two smokers. the speech may tire those who do not want to hear it. but clearly he is not a scary man. the figures are encouraging: in europe, 500,000 people are killed by this scourge every year. in belgium, 33 per cent of young people aged 15 to 24 are regular smokers and 51 per cent have already smoked. worse, a quarter of smokers had their first cigarette before the age of 15. in short, young people are smoking more and more, and no tobacco control policy is currently able to stem this infernal trend. i therefore welcome the adoption by our plenary of the jules maaten report which, taking up the major tobacco control measures advocated by the who, proposes to the council measures which would enable effective prevention of smoking and non-conditioning of adolescents by cigarette manufacturers. that is why i am categorically opposed to the sale of chocolate cigarettes and other liquorice pipes: to mimic this gesture at the age of five is to risk recording it, in any case making it a norm. the risk is proven, the symbol is disastrous, and all for a perfectly ridiculous price!
in writing. - this vote completely distorts the spirit of the directive on temporary agency work. equal pay for equal work, of course, but the central issue is that of reference. for the commission, the reference was clear: the user company was the reference. today, however, the salary of a temporary worker should be assessed according to the salary scale in the agency. a subtle but decisive move. it is a de facto legitimization of a dual labour market and even the creation of genuine networks of slave traders who break prices. those who are happy about this vote are short-sighted. the fact that a wage barrier is drawn between temporary workers and workers in the companies using the system is a fatal blow to social cohesion. and that is the interim's achilles' heel. the insertion is not promulgated by decree. temporary workers are more likely to be injured and more likely to be the victims of harassment than other workers. they pay dearly for their status. the social reality is unrelenting. if they are paid less for the same work in the same company, they will lose any chance of being integrated. the temp, a worker like the rest? - no , please ! and this dead-born directive will surely fail to do him justice.

in writing. - the implementation of prudential supervision rules in the european union is likely to protect savings and restore confidence in movable financial investments. the globalisation of risks should also encourage the european authorities to develop international surveillance rules. in this context, parliament's invitation to the commission and the european supervisory committees to organise, together with the us federal reserve and the security and exchange commission, a transatlantic financial forum is a step in the right direction. however, other, more far-reaching initiatives are not to be excluded.
in writing. - the effects of announcing without following up on concrete results for the effective liberalisation of gas and electricity markets are a considerable handicap for european credibility with citizens. these liberalizations must be activated urgently so that they are perceptible to households. in addition, given the european inefficiency vis-à-vis south korea in the area of unfair competition in the shipbuilding market, it is urgent to implement the wto dispute settlement procedure, both for the sector in question and to pave the way for the emergence of an international competition policy.

commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, before we get into the actual discussion of mrs sörensen's report, we would like to express our appreciation for the quality of her work and congratulate her on her personal involvement in the fight against the real scourge of human trafficking. the proposal for a directive before us today is undoubtedly concerned with a very worrying problem: illegal immigration in its two particularly odious forms, namely the development of smuggling networks for reasons that have nothing to do with humanity and the exploitation of foreigners in the context of human trafficking. more specifically, this directive concerns the victims of trafficking and smuggling, two types of crime which should not be confused with each other. one of the key elements in dismantling the networks exploited by migrants is the acquisition of information. victims are the first to provide this information to the authorities. and without this information, authorities will have an increased difficulty in fighting trafficking networks. the aim of this proposal is therefore to strengthen the instruments for combating networks by creating a residence permit for victims of illegal immigration and trafficking in human beings, the scheme of which encourages them to cooperate with the competent authorities against the perpetrators of the offences in question, but avoiding any abuse in the use of this mechanism. it should be noted that, since trafficking in human beings and the promotion of illegal immigration are often interlinked, the scope of the text covers both offences because, in essence, they are a common struggle.
the rapporteur. - mr president, let me first of all thank all those colleagues who have put so much of their time and energy into what i believe has been a very fruitful cooperation. in mid-september, the most important conference on trafficking in human beings to date was held in this chamber. it was organised by the international organisation for migration and the european commission. the result is the so-called brussels declaration, and in that brussels declaration you can clearly see the central importance of the directive which is being put to the vote today. it is important to point out at the outset that human trafficking and smuggling are an integral part of our economy today. it is essentially a matter of treating people as if they were mere commodities. in addition, victims of trafficking are victims of a whole range of abuses and crimes. human trafficking has also become a sectarian issue, and almost everyone seems to want to profile themselves as such. the fact that human trafficking is rampant is often overlooked, because corruption, as well as indifference and unbelief, are rampant. in a number of countries that will soon be part of the european union, parents are selling their children in the hope of giving them a better future. this proposal for a directive is a first, but also an important step towards finally doing something concrete. the directive requires member states to provide for the short-term residence permit for victims of trafficking in human beings who are willing to cooperate with the competent authorities. the essence of the proposal is that the interests of the public authorities and those of the victim coincide here. in particular, the criminal networks are being dismantled. the commission has combined trafficking in human beings and smuggling of human beings for this specific directive. it is true that the two are clearly distinct matters from a purely legal point of view. in reality, however, the distinction is extremely difficult to make. however, this proposal for a directive is based on the sound practical knowledge of experts working in the field. the experience we have gained in belgium, for example, shows that the instrument of a short-term residence permit does not miss its purpose. other member states have similar systems, it said, with varying success. i fully support this commission proposal and it is essential that its content is not affected. this means, firstly, that some amendments such as those tabled by the committee on civil liberties are not acceptable. i particularly welcome amendments nos 7 and 27 for the simple reason that they leave no stone unturned in the whole proposal. they're making an empty box out of it. voting for these amendments is a thinly disguised endorsement of the practices of organised crime. what i am saying is that this directive stands or falls with the minimum social provision for victims. a victim will never cooperate unless he or she is offered a beginning alternative to the situation in which he or she is now. let me briefly explain two other aspects. firstly, some would like this directive to be applied directly to minors. this seems to be a good idea at first sight, but because one can never do enough for the extremely vulnerable position of minors. but this does not take account of the fact that what this directive offers is, in the end, only a minimum. in all member states today, minors are offered much more and it would be a real setback if minors were granted only such minimal protection and rights. this does not mean that special efforts are not needed for the victims who are minors. finally, i would like to clarify a number of the original amendments tabled by mrs van der laan. amendments nos 20, 25 and 35. its alert legal spirit provides for the possibility that contact between victim and offender may be permitted in certain cases by the authorities in order to further the investigation. it is a tempting idea, but it is legally problematic in a number of countries. the risk of abduction or even manipulation and intimidation is also real. victims who want to cooperate with the authorities want absolutely no contact with the criminals, even from a distance. i would also like to emphasize that this directive gives special consideration to specialized and recognized ngos, but also to international organizations and public organizations. as mrs van der laan has rightly said, this has not been included in the text before you because of a technical error, but it is noted on the voting list. ngos also have a special role as intermediaries between the investigative and prosecutorial services on the one hand and the victim. it is also important to emphasize that ngos must be recognized by the state. after all, anyone, even criminals, can start an ngo. or, as is not uncommon, ngos can be infiltrated and manipulated. that is why i think that everyone who deals with this matter should sign a code of conduct.
in writing. - between 700 000 and 2 million people, mainly women and children, are victims of human trafficking each year. human trafficking is the third largest source of illegal income in the world after arms and drugs. it generates $5 to $7 billion in profits a year. the current rate of child trafficking is already 10 times higher than the transatlantic slave trade at its peak. this is the fastest growing form of forced labour. human trafficking is a scourge that affects all parts of the world and is taking on alarming proportions in the wake of globalisation. criminal networks quickly realised the immense profits they could make from the plight of immigration applicants. the proposal for a directive on the creation of a short-term residence permit for victims of illegal immigration or trafficking in human beings who cooperate with the competent authorities, which is being put to the vote today, is one of the instruments that will help to combat this scourge.
in writing. - one of the fundamental but too often overlooked problems of european scientific research is the flagrant underrepresentation of women. this is not only a question of equality and justice but also a question of efficiency for european research, which thus demonstrates its inability to mobilise its human resources potential. that is why, in view of the objective that the union set itself in lisbon of becoming the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world, it is particularly important to vote in favour of this report, which gives the issue of women in research the attention it deserves. the aim is to support the efforts made under the european research policy, for example the role played by the 'women and science' actions under the specific programme 'structuring the european research area'. we also see that much remains to be done: to mainstream a gender perspective at all levels of future framework programmes and to continue to collect and disseminate statistics on participation by men and women. more generally, the question arises of better reconciling work and family life, for the benefit of both children and workers, through the establishment in each member state, in particular, of childcare and early childhood services.

- madam president, commissioner byrne, on behalf of the liberal group, i would like to thank our rapporteur, mr nisticò, for the quality and thoroughness of his work and i am extremely sorry that he is not here tonight to, in a way, reap the benefits. i am glad, however, that this joint text was adopted in the conciliation committee. ensuring a high and equal level of safety for blood donation and transfusion throughout the union was essential, as are all the improvements made to the legislative text throughout the procedure. improvements with, firstly, a major win for donors and patients: the inclusion of a specific chapter on haemovigilance which includes traceability and reporting of incidents and serious adverse reactions. the liberal group was particularly keen on this, i would say, simply because the need for this kind of vigilance is no longer being demonstrated in europe. the haemovigilance networks are an essential tool for maximum safety of the transfusion chain. on the thorny issue of free gift, my reading differs from mr lund's. i am glad that common sense has prevailed, and that is from the second reading. i welcome the fact that the joint text, while strongly encouraging voluntary and unpaid donations - this is the subject of our article 20 - leaves member states a certain amount of leeway to use paid donors in a strictly regulated environment. is it necessary to recall that the union imports no less than 50% of therapeutic products derived from plasma, and that patients with haemophilia, severe burns and others have a vital need for these medicines? in this respect, a voluntary policy for the supply of recombinant factors to patients is more necessary than ever in europe. another of the liberal group's wishes was to see a reference to scientific and technical progress inserted in the text. this concern was echoed in article 29, which sets out in particular clear guidelines for blood transfusion facilities. finally, the conciliation procedure completed the excellent work done by the three institutions on this matter. the only point of controversy, which was quickly resolved, was the question of the medical examination before donation. i am glad that the conciliation committee has decided that it is sufficient to ask that this examination be carried out by a qualified health professional. it would seem to me unreasonable, and i have said this since the first reading, to go against certain national practices which are producing excellent results. i am thinking in particular, but not exclusively, of the awareness campaigns and donations in buses which could be paralysed if we demanded the presence of a doctor. the european union is not immune to an incident, of course, or even to the emergence of a new virus. north america is currently experiencing a blood product safety alert caused by a virus from a western island. we must therefore be more vigilant, apply the principle of early detection to all uses of blood, ensure that the donor can be traced back to the recipient. at that price a blood supply will be established which will regain the confidence of all in europe.

- madam president, mr liikanen, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by thanking my 12 colleagues on the parliament's delegation to the conciliation committee. i see here mr bowe, mrs schörling, mr lund, mrs van brempt, all of whom have vigorously and firmly defended the right of consumers to safe and effective flame retardants. a joint project which could not have been carried out without the effective work of the danish presidency - we will convey the message to it - and of the commission, of course. we have come a long way since 16 january 2001, when the commission presented us with this proposal for a directive which, i would remind you, was intended to prohibit the placing on the market and use of pentabromodiphenyl ether alone, a brominated flame retardant used in the manufacture of polyurethane foam in particular. it is a chemical that is harmful to our environment and our health. it is found in high concentrations, particularly in breast milk, with a significant risk of contamination in infants. it is therefore logical that, at first reading, parliament voted in favour of a ban on pentabde in all its forms, thus confirming the red card given to it by the commission. so there is unanimity on the first substance. however, when the plenary voted in september 2001, it also wanted this proposal to cover two sister substances, octabromodiphenyl ether and decabromodiphenyl ether. in view of the strong suspicions of harmfulness of octar-bromodiphenyl ether, both for the environment and for public health, the european parliament has, once again by a very large majority, voted for its immediate ban. the plenary, on the other hand, wanted to give a yellow card to decabromodiphenyl ether, proposing to postpone its ban until 1 january 2006 at the latest and to make it conditional on the final results of the risk analysis. this approach was based on the strict application of the precautionary principle, which was not shared at that stage by the council in its common position or by the commission in its amended proposal. in this case, where chemicals are put on the defendant's table, the debate is always delicate. the aim is to strike a delicate balance between obvious fire safety benefits and the environmental and health hazards that have been identified in some cases. and it is this balance in risk management, taking into account such varied elements as scientific risk assessment, proportionality, cost-benefit analysis, that has been at the heart of the discussions in our conciliation committee. three trilogues were eventually needed to reconcile the views and reach a compromise acceptable to all, mainly for decabromodiphenyl ether, since, i would like to stress, for octrabromodiphenyl ether the pressure exerted by our assembly paid off. this pressure will have contributed to the rapid and ultimately negative conclusion of the risk assessment and convinced the council to side with us in banning the use of this substance. as regards decabromodiphenyl ether, the inclusion in the joint text of a recital calling on the commission to evaluate without delay the results of the risk reduction strategy, which is expected by 30 june next year at the latest, seems to me objectively the first compromise we can reach at this stage, because in this case, unlike octabromodiphenyl ether, in the absence of a finalised risk reduction strategy, and especially in the absence of risks to human health - the conclusions of the french rapporteur are formal in this respect - such a postponement with strict conditions is justified. it should be remembered, however, that decabromodiphenyl ether, used mainly in insulation products, construction and textiles, is a particularly effective and fire-resistant flame retardant. in the year 2000 alone, 155 lives were saved and 4 537 injuries prevented in the united kingdom alone by the presence of this substance in furniture upholstery. but what efforts, i would say, to reach this agreement in the conciliation committee. i have sometimes felt that, as rapporteur, i was acting more as a member of the european chemicals bureau than as a directly elected representative of the citizens. the problem is a cumbersome, slow and opaque risk assessment procedure. it took almost ten years in all for brominated infu­gies, which were included in the list of priority substances, to be finally evaluated. i very much hope that through this case study the commission and especially the member states will have understood the need, in the context of preparing the future regulation on chemicals, to put in place a new method of evaluation which is faster, more efficient and more transparent, a reach system which, as proposed in the resolution adopted by our parliament in november 2001, is based on a genuine risk management strategy at community level. in conclusion, and i repeat my football metaphor, whereas the commission in its initial proposal gave only half a red card to pentabromodiphenyl ether, parliament and the council, acting as an impartial and fair arbiter, are giving two red cards to pentabromodiphenyl ether and octabromodiphenyl ether and one yellow card to decabromodiphenyl ether, because the main thing is to reconcile the immediate safety of citizens against fire risks with the longer-term benefits in terms of public health and the environment. the joint text submitted to the plenary tomorrow fulfils this dual objective. for all these reasons, i shall call on my colleagues to support him.
