- madam president, i would like to welcome the decision of the european council to organise the next intergovernmental conference of 25 or even 27. it is a positive response to a legitimate demand from the new members, our partners on an equal footing, but it is also a clear signal that we share the responsibility for making the convention a success, so that the enlarged union is more democratic and more governable. we need results that are commensurate with our ambitions. i shall deal with two specific points here. first of all, i am glad to hear the prime minister of denmark defend the formula of qualified majority and co-decision for community legislation, and i hope that by that he means all legislation. secondly, the conditions for the entry into force of the future european constitution: the constitution must be able to enter into force on the basis of a double majority of the states and the peoples, and on this point, despite his temporary absence, i would like to congratulate president prodi for having carried out, under his direct responsibility, exploratory work on a comprehensive text. this work will make a major contribution to the work of the convention and includes the idea of entry into force by a double majority.
- it is now clear that the european countries have behaved - and i quote the words of former european commissioner karel van miert in a flemish newspaper just a few days ago - 'like the vassal states of america' by giving the green light to turkey's future accession to the european union. because, contrary to what is sometimes claimed, the turks have indeed been given a date for final negotiations and, in fact, for accession, around 2007. the copenhagen criteria are not insurmountable and are not much more difficult to achieve for turkey than for romania and bulgaria, for example. and yet turkey cannot be called a european country, either culturally, geographically, historically, religiously or politically. it is therefore, i think, self-evident that a european union which calls itself democratic should organise binding referendums in all european countries on such a far-reaching step, one whose consequences are hardly to be foreseen, one which could trigger a huge flow of migration to the west, and one which could even lead to a total implosion of the european union. if the democratic voice of the european citizens is denied, i believe that with the possible enlargement of turkey the european union will lose all democratic legitimacy.
- madam commissioner, you're not the only one who's angry. when i see how this event destroys the basis of life of many people and of an entire region, we are all angry. i say to you on behalf of our group that our hearts go out to all of them and i think we should offer help through all the channels we have. but we have to do what a parliament has to do here in parliament, which is to make rules and strengthen the existing rules. the first thing i would ask - and i would also ask my good friend bertel haarder - is that we apply the existing rules, that we make use of erika i and ii. there is one more item on the agenda for the council, mr minister, and i hope you will make use of it. we also need to sharpen a number of things that are in erika i and ii, and i am thinking more specifically of ports of escape and safe anchorages. to avoid greater disasters, we must make this a mandatory system so that ships in difficulty can go there and not be refused. we have to find a settlement. there was to be a study by the commission. we need to get it. we also need to have a compensation scheme for ports or shelters that receive a ship. now there's a ship lying at a depth of 3,500 meters with 50,000 tons of oil still on board, and that ship will cause trouble for years to come. we must avoid such things in the future. secondly, port state controls as brian simpson says. let's sharpen it for high-risk ships. the control of 25% of the good ships is of no use, you have to focus the control on risky ships and on ships of old age. we must ensure that ships in transit are better controlled in sensitive shipping areas. we ask you to open negotiations with third countries, including russia, to ensure that we can conclude bilateral agreements on this matter. the council and the imo and the commission must put an end to this quarrel. the commission must negotiate and be given the opportunity to talk about it, and then we will have much stricter rules. international law - and this is clearly stated in the resolution on which we all agree - cannot be a reason for destroying the environment, for destroying human life. one doesn't outweigh the other. finally, mr president, as regards the debate on procedures, we must act quickly and put rules on paper together with the commission and the council. that is why we are not in favour of a committee of inquiry, because it would take too much time.
- the president, ladies and gentlemen, madam commissioner, action must indeed be taken now and we can go even further than what has already been agreed. i am therefore pleased that the two largest groups in this parliament support our proposals for a european coastguard and a european civil rapid reaction force. first, it is preventive. the european coast guard is needed to safeguard the european interest and to prevent ships in difficulty from becoming the plaything of member states which look to their own interests first and therefore make wrong decisions. the coast guard must become the strong arm of the maritime safety agency on the ground. secondly, european civil protection must be able to respond immediately to disasters and to arrive on the scene with its own equipment. the green helmets can then be deployed throughout the union, as not all member states have the necessary equipment and knowledge to deal with disasters. i was a witness in galicia a week after the disaster and i saw how difficult the work was for our belgian civil protection and how slowly such action is taken. i understand that now large construction companies must be involved. of course you can't. finally, i would ask the council to work urgently on the liability directive. that is the crux of the problem of maritime transport in europe and worldwide. perhaps you could convey that message to the next presidency: "nunca maize". what are you talking about?
- mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, your answer is in line with that given by your predecessor as spanish president - i asked the question and he said 'no'. according to the information provided by the armed forces present during the war in afghanistan a year ago, no ammunition or depleted uranium weapons were used. but all the information we have, whether military or technical, shows that they used, in any case, a very dense metal, which is either uranium or tungsten. this is not a trivial matter at all, because if it is uranium, depleted or not, there would necessarily be a significant contamination of certain sites in afghanistan, where there are currently military troops, of course, but also ngos, humanitarian organizations and, of course, local populations. it seems to me that you were careful in your answer. personally, i'm not convinced you're well informed. i therefore think it would be useful to take part in a field investigation to verify whether there is no radioactive contamination which could cause significant damage to both civilians, ngos and the military.
- first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on this important report. in previous resolutions, most recently in 1997, parliament has called for a european year of sport. the european union's interest has so far been mainly in professional sport, with less attention paid to recreational sport and its cultural, educational and social merits. as has been rightly pointed out, this is probably due mainly to the fact that there is still no treaty article conferring explicit competence on the european union in this area. however, with one third of eu citizens actively involved in sport and over 600 000 sports clubs, sport is undeniably an important social phenomenon. sport is important not only in social and cultural terms but also in economic terms. it is estimated that the sector accounts for about 2% of the total gross domestic product of the european union. sport is of great importance as a vehicle for positive values such as willpower, courage, tolerance, loyalty and friendship. however, given its enormous impact, sport is also often negatively approached with feelings of chauvinism, racism and violence and unfortunately, sport also receives negative attention through violence, bribery or hooliganism. the emphasis on youth sports education and training in this european year is therefore to be welcomed. it is also true that less and less time and attention is being given to physical education in schools, a trend which urgently needs to be reversed. sports clubs can complement and support the school here. it is therefore essential to improve links between clubs and schools. sport must be a means of physical and mental balance and social integration, and this is particularly important in the context of enlargement in 2004, especially at a time when intolerance is becoming more and more widespread.
- the president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would naturally like to thank the commissioner for her communication and for the prospect of the green paper. but it is not often that i can agree with what mr langen has said. my group is particularly disappointed, commissioner, at the reluctance of the commission to come to the european parliament with a framework directive for the time being. as you yourself have said, services of general interest are the cornerstone of our european social model. it is about people's social rights, about basic services such as water, energy, public transport, communications, health care, which are also essential for social cohesion and for the functioning of our economies. my group, and indeed the whole of parliament, has been pushing for this framework directive for years. even the council is in favour of the commission, because the council urged the commission to adopt this framework directive in nice and then even more explicitly in barcelona. however, the commission will first launch a broad consultation via a green paper in spring 2003. i understand, commissioner, that the problem of services of general interest is complex. i understand the differences in approach between member states. i know that both public and private actors are involved, and it is true that there is a distinction between sectors. in the meantime, we are constantly faced with positions on the liberalization of various sectors of the economy without having a framework directive laying down the general principles. let there be no misunderstanding about it. my group is in favour of the single market, provided that it is socially and ecologically correct. but the market is an instrument and the market has its limits and when it comes to the basic social rights of citizens, it is not the rules of the market and competition that should determine the rules of the game. the green paper, commissioner, is also too late to contribute to the convention's discussion on the role of services of general interest. that is the discussion we are having at the moment. we will conclude it in february and the commission will not present a green paper until march. i think the commission is going to miss the train. finally, there is one last reason, one fact which worries me very much and which has something to do with what mr langen said. until now, the commission has assumed that member states could finance their public service obligations for a social purpose without this being considered unlawful state aid. i think you're right. but the treaty is clearly lacking in clarity. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the debate on the future of the european union. the commission is now apparently going to conclude that it cannot provide legal certainty to the member states in the area of the financing of services of general interest. where is the famous autonomy to organise their public services? the conclusion is clear to us, ladies and gentlemen. first of all: we call on the commission to present a framework directive without delay, as has been repeatedly requested. secondly: the convention should provide for the possibility of laying down the basic principles of public services in full respect of the competences of the member states. thirdly: our constitution must clearly state that services of general interest are social rights of citizens which cannot be left to the rules of the market and competition. i hope that the commission can support us in this ambition. (applause from the left)
- mr president, commissioner, i think your morning could have been more pleasant. in addition, you are responsible for representing the commission and your colleagues directly responsible for the matter, who have delegated this task to you. but that 's part of the job ! i shall not go into the content, because my colleagues have already largely expressed what i myself think. i have read the latest commission document, which is a few days old and is concise. it recalled in particular that the first general document on the subject produced by the commission was in 1996. six years have passed and the commission has announced a green paper. i don't think it's that serious. you have problems of timing which have already been pointed out by many: problems of timing with regard to the convention, problems of timing with regard to the implementation of the sectoral directives and certainly problems of timing - mrs ainardi has alluded to this - with regard to enlargement. that said, i think it is pointless, ladies and gentlemen, to continue to ask the commission to produce documents. i say this in a somewhat provocative way: the commission is divided. it is clear that there is no political leadership, which is a serious problem, since the commission has a monopoly on initiative. i think that we as parliamentarians must now take up our responsibilities within the convention. several contributions have already been tabled on the subject of services of general interest and their link with competition law. the commission itself, in its first contribution to the convention in may, indicated how important it is to have a legal basis for services of general interest. on page 6 of the document it is even stated that 'otherwise the adjustments will be made precisely to the detriment of the european model of society and the values recognised by european democracies'. the commission is therefore both aware of the problem and incapable of taking political decisions. we, the parliamentarians of the convention or the members of the european parliament, must turn to our national governments and we must, within the convention, fight for the future european constitution to actually include legal provisions governing competition law.
- mr president, like other members, i abstained for several reasons. firstly, for the reasons repeatedly expressed by our colleague mr goebbels, namely the inadequate funding for enlargement of the union. secondly, because we remain convinced that the thickest fog still exists over the budgetary margins of an enlarged union, and we see that the same fog now extends over the timetable of the legal operations which are essential to bring enlargement to a successful conclusion. finally, mr president, we would like you to take the initiative, especially when you take part in the summits, to defend the prerogatives of our assembly and the demands it makes.
in writing. - viewed from a historical perspective, the achievement of enlargement touches the very heart of the european union. this is about sharing a common set of values in liberal democracy, respect for human rights, social protection of the weakest, protection of minorities, market economy and peaceful coexistence in a state governed by the rule of law. enlargement is a way of reconnecting with europe's geographical, political and cultural identity. the prospect of a union of 25 is now a reality. this enlargement, on an unprecedented scale, corresponds to a historic duty to reunify our continent. it also requires us to rethink the european project in depth, the way in which an enlarged union functions. it is also an opportunity. the enlargement of the union, which has been formalised in copenhagen, to include more than 100 million new citizens will promote trade and economic activity and give a new impetus to the development and integration of the european economy as a whole. the accession of new member states will increase the weight and influence of the union on the international scene.
in writing. - equal opportunities, as enshrined in article 23 of the charter, is a fundamental right which is part of our values. since 1975, when the first equal pay and treatment directive was adopted, things have certainly changed, but there is still a lot to be done. gender-disaggregated statistics and the gender dimension in all our policies are a necessary prerequisite. concrete measures to ensure that parents, both men and women, can benefit from infrastructure (e.g. day care) enabling them to reconcile family and working life would be a considerable step forward. our actions must make people aware of the changes in mentality that need to be made so that, finally, more women can access positions of responsibility, the so-called typically male careers. it also requires a joint effort with the social partners to ensure that this basic principle is applied in every company and in every collective bargaining. equal pay is only one aspect of gender equality, not to mention domestic violence, sexual exploitation, etc. europe must set an example. i thank mrs kratsa for continuing in this direction.
- we hope that hong kong will not adopt the chinese system of general provisions which allows for the arbitrary prosecution of political opponents. we are particularly concerned, as mrs malmström is, about the falun gong practitioners whose peaceful protest does not justify any persecution. hong kong must be allowed to remain what it is, while waiting for china to follow the path of democracy and human rights. it is only on this basis that we can maintain dialogue and cooperation. i was able to visit china with some members of our green and efta group and i was struck by the way in which the economic model of hong kong is an example to the major cities of mainland china. but in the field of human rights, democracy and freedom of expression, the following still holds true: "one country, two systems". in this respect, hong kong remains a unique place in china's sphere of influence. many in china told us that in the long term, developments in the sense of greater democratization and human rights can be expected. it is therefore with great concern that we see the anti-subversion act coming into force in hong kong. we are afraid that this will be at the expense of freedom of expression, of objective jurisprudence, of religious and artistic freedom, of scientific work and so on. and that would be the exact opposite of what we have always hoped for, namely that a single area of democracy and human rights could be established.
tibet is a region with its own culture, its own religious and political traditions. it is also a so-called autonomous region in china, however little that autonomy may mean in practice. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject. political opponents are simply treated as common criminals. the fight against terrorism provides an additional alibi. the death sentences handed down on 3 december to tenzin delek, an influential buddhist lama, and his assistant, lopsang dhondrup, are a sad illustration of this. we do not believe the charges to be proven, but we are astonished by the way in which the two men were held in solitary confinement and finally sentenced to death after months of detention and a mock trial. these death sentences must not be carried out, the sentences must be reviewed on the basis of a fair trial. the dialogue between china and the representatives of the dalai lama, which is giving rise to so much hope here in this hemisphere, must be resumed, but we must not be naive in our relations with that great country. they are very interested in what we think of them, but we must not be blinded by appearances. we must see what is actually happening and bring about a change in law and in fact.
- madam president, i wonder if you have just had a majority vote. my voting card was in another place and that is why i voted late. would you please note that i voted yes?
